#6880. Why The Rings of Power Will Never Be the Same After the Season 3 Finale

A post-finale interview with the showrunners of the ‘Lord of the Rings’ series reveals what may come.

When the creators and showrunners of The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power embarked on season 3, they knew the endgame from the start: The forging of the One Ring. After two seasons in the Second Age of Middle-earth, which saw almost all the other Rings of Power forged—three for Elves, seven for Dwarves, and nine for Men—it was time for Sauron to forge the one to rule them all.

“We wanted a tighter, tauter, more profound season,” says co-showrunner Patrick McKay, in an interview with Esquire. “We wanted to start with a bang and never let up. We wanted to deepen our characters psychologically, and challenge them in new ways. You’re seeing the end of one part of the story, and the beginning of another.”

“We had to have Sauron forge the One Ring,” adds J.D. Payne, who also created and serves as co-showrunner with McKay. “To do that, all the storylines in some way are connected to Sauron’s attempts at world domination. That was the one that so much of our attention and focus went into getting right, and it took all of our departments working together at the top of their games to create.”

On Wednesday, just in time for Thanksgiving, Prime Video gave Tolkien fans a feast with the final two episodes of The Rings of Power season 3: episode 7, “Orodruin,” and episode 8, “The Doom of Men.”

In the penultimate episode, Sauron (Charlie Vickers) learns from the Balrog—who talks, apparently!—how to use the fires of Mount Doom to forge the One Ring. Then, with the power of the One Ring literally in his hands, Sauron mobilizes his army for battle against Pharazôn (Trystan Gravelle), the king of Númenor. But instead of a big, violent bloodbath in the spirit of, say, Game of Thrones, Sauron simply... surrenders. Or does he? We’re talking about a master deceiver, after all.

In a spoiler-filled interview with Esquire, McKay and Payne unpack a few of our biggest questions about season 3, including how and why the Balrog speaks (with actor Simon Pegg lending his voice) to why Pharazôn is actually one of the most consequential figures in the series.

This interview has been edited for clarity.

PATRICK MCKAY: One of the things we're constantly trying to do is go deeper into Tolkien’s life's work. He did not just create The Lord of the Rings, he wrote all this other stuff. He envisioned an entire world. It's his legendarium. When you do the deep dive, you find there are surprising things if you only know the book and film trilogy. The Balrog Gandalf faces down in Moria is part of this level of “super being” that is equal to Gandalf and Sauron. They’re Maiar. In prior ages, Balrogs could cast spells and command troops. They’re intelligent. They can talk, you just don’t see that one talk in that circumstance.

The idea of walking out onto a diving board and doing something risky, to show a different side of that being, was something that we felt compelled to do this season. It’s certainly unexpected. We’re always looking to do that. We don’t want to be familiar. We want to show you things in this world that you haven’t seen before.

Talk to me about designing a new One Ring. Granted, it’s a gold ring; there’s not much you can really deviate. But in what ways did you want to create a One Ring that belongs to The Rings of Power?

MCKAY: The books are pretty specific about the way it looks. It’s a plain gold band. If you’re gonna do that, it’s going to look like the One Ring you’ve seen in other adaptations. We did make a few modifications, just for camera. We broadened it to give it more chunkiness. But it is what it says on the tin.

How is season 3 a culmination of everything that’s happened before, and how is it setting up the rest of the show?

J.D. PAYNE: It pulls together everything we’ve been doing with Sauron and Galadriel from the first two seasons. In season 1, he befriended her and deceived her in the guise of Halbrand. In season 3, you see Celebrimbor tormenting him: “You’re never gonna be able to make a ring capable of dominating all the other rings—or dominating her.” Part of Sauron’s ambition to forge the One Ring, as a character motivation, is being able to dominate the person who said “no” to him over the last few seasons. You understand how crazy that must have driven him.

MCKAY: The show’s called Rings of Power, because the creation is the throughline of this entire era of Tolkien’s mythology. At the end of season 3, you’ve now seen the creation of all of them. That’s now the world we live in. Everyone in Middle-earth is a nuclear power, so to speak. Now we’re setting up for the next part of the story, which is, “What are the consequences going to be?” One thing in season 3 we really invested in was Númenor to set Sauron up as the bowling ball crashing into those pins. That’s sort of the beginning of the end of the Second Age. It’s a tragic story. The Rings have an impact on Middle-earth that is felt for thousands of years. Now they are in this world.

The finale is literally titled “The Doom of Men,” and it ends with Sauron “surrendering” to Pharazôn. Or so that’s what he wants them to think. Can you break down this moment? How is this Sauron’s big chess move?

PAYNE: Everything builds up to that. You’ve seen Gandalf getting through to the Dark Wizard and take out a massive portion of Sauron’s army at the last moment. So the game changes right from underneath him, and he has to make a calculation. Is he going to put all his chips on the table and lose his army in a stalemate? Or is there another way? The great thing about Sauron is that he’s amazing at constructing scenarios that are lose-lose. If he can’t beat Pharazôn on the field of battle, he reasons maybe there’s another way. That’s why the final moment is filled with tension. It’s not simply a surrender. Our Elves, particularly Galadriel, know Sauron so well. She knows this is not a defeat for Sauron, and he’s going to find a way to make this into a play for exactly what he wants. Those who know the lore know what happens when Sauron is taken prisoner to Númenor. We wanted that feeling of dread, watching Charlie Vickers have a hint of a smile.

One of the most consequential characters this season has been Pharazôn. In what ways does Pharazôn represent the downfall of man?

MCKAY: This is a major character in the mythology. We introduced him in season 1, he’s been sort of a politician gathering power. In season 3, he’s been king for five days, and he’s in his heyday. He’s in his golden era. But he’s driven by insecurity. There was this blacksmith we empowered, who got away, who’s now marching around saying he’s “Lord.” No, I’m the guy! There’s professional jealousy there.

We talk sometimes about how he’s Stalin to Sauron’s Hitler. You’ve got two characters who are quite full of themselves, and have a high opinion of themselves, which puts them on a trajectory. Pharazôn’s goal is, “I want to take on Sauron.” That makes him a compelling character because you watch the way he greases the wheels and maneuvers to bring about that end. We give a lot of credit to our actor, Trystan Gravelle. He leaps off the screen in season 3.

Speaking of showdowns, one of the biggest confrontations you stage in season 3 is between Sauron and Gandalf, which Tolkien never wrote in the books. What did you hope to discover by pitting these characters together?

MCKAY: The journey of Gandalf and his involvement with Ciaran Hinds’ character, Pollando, has real deep roots in some Tolkien deep cuts where he talked about wizards who went east and that their activities probably prevented the east from joining Sauron. The story comes from that. We wanted to dramatize that through some of the most iconic characters in the legendarium coming face to face.

PAYNE: Seeing the contrast between them felt like too delicious an opportunity to pass up. Sauron is seeking power at all costs, and Gandalf has been told by the Secret Fire, “To access the greatest power in the universe, you have to give up all other power.” So when Gandalf is offered the world by Sauron, he turns it down. If you demand power, it’s going to destroy you. Ultimately, having the One Ring, and forging it and trying to keep power, sets up the weaknesses of the Third Age that the heroes exploit to finally destroy Sauron.

One of my favorite hobbies is Magic: The Gathering, which has had both Lord of the Rings and The Hobbit as crossover sets. Are there any possibilities for a Rings of Power collaboration?

MCKAY: [Laughs.] No. This is the first we’ve heard about it.

PAYNE: We actually wrote a draft of a Magic: The Gathering script in the before times. It was one of the last scripts we worked on before turning to Rings of Power. But it was right before the Disney-Fox merger, and it kinda got lost there. But we love that universe.

#6876. The 33 Best Gifts for the Snobbiest People You Know

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

We found the stuff they don’t already own.

This is not meant to sound like a brag, but it inevitably will. Tons of luxurious products come across our desks; as such, I’ve developed especially discerning taste. I’ve tested more than my fair share of $500 colognes, $700 headphones, and $1,000 espresso machines over the years. Not that luxury is synonymous with price, but you know. While these occupational hazards might make me a challenge to shop for, they also give me a discerning eye for gifts that will actually impress my fellow snobs.

If they’re a coffee connoisseur who only drinks single-origin, always espresso, consider upgrading their at-home setup with Meraki’s dual-boiler espresso machine. For the audiophile whose vinyl collection rivals that of an actual record store, a Fluance turntable will do their records justice. And if their vanity would put a Saks to shame, an under-the-radar Creed fragrance and an award-winning red light mask are worthy additions.

The last thing they want is a bad interface or those ugly Alexa models. Thankfully, 2026 is the year Apple entered the home display market.

My brother has the most extensive at-home espresso setup I’ve ever seen, and he swears by this tamper. It’s spring-loaded to deliver consistent pressure, with an integrated lever for perfectly even pucks. If your espresso pulls aren’t flawless after this, time to look inward.

For a gift that will impress even the most particular of coffee connoisseurs, go with Meraki’s second-gen espresso machine. Featuring a built-in grinder, scales, and a dual-boiler system, it’s one of the most premium machines money can buy.

The ultimate snob’s wine opener. A needle and a bit of fancy pump work allow you to pour a glass without ever uncorking the bottle. Great for by-the-glass or sparing yourself any cork emergencies with older vintages.

Take it from someone who actually cooks—the home chef in your life doesn’t want gimmicky gadgets. Instead, gift them practical, high-quality cookware they’ll actually use, like this editor favorite stainless steel frying pan from All-Clad’s D3 line.

For the foodie who worships at the altar of Bourdain, consider gifting them his exact chef’s knife of choice. The Japanese-made Global G-2 has sleek all-metal construction, with a textured handle for a nonslip grip. What’s more, it can be custom-engraved with their name or initials.

For the giftee who’s always complaining that their matcha isn’t the right color (guilty), consider this tea kit. It has everything they need for the perfect cup at home, including a sifter, measuring spoon, whisk, and ceremonial-grade matcha.

Dubbed our top pick for audiophiles, this turntable has it all: consistently great audio quality, a built-in speed control mechanism, and an attractive wood finish.

Aesthetics and audio quality don’t always go hand-in-hand for headphones, which is precisely why this Bang & Olufsen pair is a standout. Our editors named it the best pair of noise-canceling headphones for sound quality, and it’s easily the most attractive model on the list.

If they’ve ever slept at one of the world’s best hotels, they have slept on Frette sheets and worn a Frette robe. Give them that luxury at home.

Every chess player I’ve ever met takes the game extremely seriously, and this is a set they’d be proud to display on their coffee table. Bonus: You can monogram it for a personal touch.

The best home gifts not only spark conversation, but also serve a purpose in their space. Pair this Greek mythology-inspired incense holder with some great-smelling incense (I’m partial to Le Labo), and you have an infallible gift for the artsy types on your list.

All the interior design obsessives I know in Brooklyn (myself included) are obsessed with this lighting brand, which draws from Bauhaus and mid-century modern influences. This retro, saucer-shaped table lamp is a foolproof pick for the giftee who wouldn’t be caught dead turning on “the big light.”

I have to hand it to the cinephiles here: Dolby Atmos is truly superior for watching movies (sorry, films) at home. This Sonos soundbar offers unmatched 3D surround-sound in a sleek package.

A candle is only “impersonal” if it’s ugly or smells bad. Loewe’s incense candle is beautiful, and it smells divine. No snob will ever turn one down.

Sure, Aventus might be Creed’s most popular cologne, but the fragrance snob you know doesn’t want to smell like every other guy. Instead, opt for one of the brand’s under-the-radar scents, like Bois du Portugal. Its notes of lavender, sandalwood, and cedar offer an aromatic scent reminiscent of a vintage barbershop.

Ah, the status hand soap. A gift that works for virtually anyone, especially those with discerning taste. This pick from Nécessaire somehow feels less sceney than other options on the market, and it’s infused with skin-softening ingredients like vitamin B3 and linoleic acid.

As a fragrance expert who tests new scents for a living, few earn me as many compliments as Althair from Parfums de Marly. It’s a grown-up vanilla, with notes of orange blossom, cinnamon, and a dose of smoky guaiac wood to keep it from going saccharine. Although it’s technically from the men’s line, its not-too-sweet gourmand scent profile is truly genderless.

The iRestore red light face mask just won a 2026 Gadget Award. Enough said.

Their parched, damaged strands deserve nothing but the best; in this case, it’s Kérastase’s Oléo-Relax hair mask. It’s got a ton of hydrating ingredients—rose oil, coconut oil, and shorea oil—to replenish dry hair. Not to mention, it smells damn good.

#6874. James Talarico’s Political-Powerhouse Girlfriend, Brianna Menard, Is Also a Master Thrifter

The 2026 midterm elections brought a blue wave of record-breaking wins. Among the most monumental was James Talarico, who became the first Democrat to win a Senate seat in Texas since 1988.

But his historic win is not all the internet has been talking about. At his celebratory Election Night event in Austin, Texas, the 37-year-old senator-elect was joined by his girlfriend, Brianna Menard, who was previously his chief of staff. Days later, the two attended a University of Texas football game—to which Menard wore a corduroy minidress she thrifted the week before—and the moment went viral across social media. (Even better, Talarico’s mom was the one who shared the photo of the couple at the game that quickly got traction on X.)

Chalk it up to her political prowess, clear sense of style, or the fact that she looks like an Instagram baddie, but some fans have even gone as far as to declare the pair this generation’s John F. Kennedy and Jacqueline Kennedy.

The couple first met in 2022, when Menard joined the politician’s team as chief of staff, and their relationship eventually turned romantic. Menard has been by Talarico’s side throughout his Senate campaign, accompanying him to the polls on Election Day in November and later joining him onstage to celebrate his historic victory.

So, who is Brianna Menard? Ahead, everything to know about Talarico’s accomplished and stylish girlfriend.

Menard is a born-and-raised Texan. She attended and graduated from the University of Texas at Austin, where she studied government.

In 2022, Menard was hired as Talarico’s chief of staff. According to reports, the two didn’t begin dating until a year later, after which Menard left her role working for the politician. In August 2026, Talarico’s campaign confirmed the couple’s relationship. “Four years ago, after he and his then-chief of staff developed feelings for each other and started dating, she left the office to pursue other opportunities,” a Talarico campaign spokesman told the New York Times.

After leaving Talarico’s office, Menard worked on the campaign for Rep. Salman Bhojani, the first Muslim member of the Texas legislature, and eventually became his chief of staff.

Menard is currently the director of TEXPAC and political education at the Texas Medical Association. According to her bio on the organization’s site, before stepping into the role, she had a decade of experience working in the Texas Legislature and previously served as executive director of the Early Childhood Caucus of the Texas House, where she led bipartisan efforts to “advance early learning, childcare access, workforce development, and healthcare affordability.”

Menard’s bio also lists her as the Board Secretary for Wheatsville Food Co-op and a member of the City of Austin’s Early Childhood Council.

According to a now-deleted bio on the Wheatsville Food Co-Op site, where she currently serves as Board Secretary, Menard called herself a “foodie and a committed vegan” who loves cooking. In the welcome post, she also revealed her love of yoga, dancing, and cuddling her cat named Zia.

“I’ve spent my career serving in the Texas Legislature and working on political campaigns across the state with the goal of making Texas a healthier and more equitable home for all of us,” she wrote in the post.

Menard joined Talarico at the polls to vote early in the midterm elections. On November 3, she also joined Talarico’s Election Night event in Austin, where she congratulated the Senator-elect on stage following his historic win. The couple shared a sweet hug and kiss to celebrate.

#6870. The Breakfast at Tiffany’s NYC Townhouse Sells for $11.5 Million

The historic residence traded at a $3.5 million discount.

Holly Golightly might have had questionable taste in men, but she had a keen eye for real estate. Memorably portrayed by Audrey Hepburn in Breakfast at Tiffany’s, Golightly lived in a handsome Upper East Side brownstone that had been subdivided into apartments. Though the 1961 classic film shot its interior scenes on a Los Angeles soundstage, the exterior of the New York City townhouse served as a backdrop throughout the movie.

In May 2025, the five-story residence hit the market for $15 million, following a total overhaul that included a slew of luxe upgrades, such as a new roof terrace and an elevator. Although StreetEasy listed the property as “in contract” last December, its official sale date is November 11, 2026. In the end, the two-family house traded hands for $11.5 million, roughly 23 percent off the original price, per Realtor.com.

Set on a tree-lined block between Lexington and Third Avenues, the East 73rd Street home is distinguished by its Tiffany Blue front door and matching window casings. Built in 1866 by architect John Sexton, the Italianate-style landmark encompasses nearly 4,500 square feet and has four bedrooms in total, three in the main residence—which spans the top four floors—and one in the garden-level apartment, which has its own separate entrance.

The seller was retired beverage executive Joseph Harkins, who acquired the property in 2015 for $7.4 million and spearheaded a complete transformation that modernized the interiors while still preserving the house’s 19th-century charm. The project even included excavating the basement level, which now has nearly eight-foot ceilings, a powder room, a second laundry room, and a wine cellar.

Outside, the 470-square-foot rear garden is fully enclosed by Walpole lattice fencing and sports stonework paving. It will no doubt be the scene of plenty of “Breakfasts” for the home’s newest steward.

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

#6869. Rihanna and A$AP Rocky Make a Stylish Outing in Big Coats and Big Pants

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

When it comes to stylish couples, Rihanna and A$AP Rocky are a match made in fashion heaven. Ever since their relationship turned romantic over five years ago, the pair have made it seem like they’ve been pulling from a his-and-hers closet. From their dramatic plush coats at the Met Gala to their matching olive moment on the Black Panther: Wakanda Forever red carpet, their coordinating looks are always on point. But, even more impressively, they always manage to complement one other without losing their individual sense of style.

On Monday afternoon, that impressive display continued as the stars were seen in Beverly Hills, California, where the dress code of the day appeared to be “open jackets and big pants.” It was the first time in weeks that the pair has been seen in public together, as A$AP has been on his Don’t Be Dumb Tour across Europe for the past couple of months.

For the outing, Rihanna curated a watermelon color scheme up top, layering a soft pink T-shirt underneath an army-green parka, presumably from Celine.

The utility-style outerwear featured rows of pockets in front and a plush brown shearling collar that also lined the inside of the jacket and cuffs. Notably, Rihanna may have borrowed the coat from her stylist, Jahleel Weaver, as he posted an Instagram Story yesterday afternoon in which he was seen wearing the exact same one.

To complete her fit, the “Diamonds” singer wore baggy white jeans and a pair of strappy white stilettos. Oh, and our queen of statement glasses added a set of cranberry-colored visor shades.

For his part, Rocky stacked a dark-wash jean jacket and a creamy corduroy one over a black tee, completing the fit with obsidian wide-leg slacks and inky leather Oxfords. As for his sunglasses, the Ray-Ban creative director chose a pair of the brand’s classic aviators.

The couple’s outing comes days after their daughter, Rocki, made her high-fashion debut in a new Dior campaign, proving that every member of this family really is programmed to have incredible style.

#6860. The Princess of Wales Swaps Her Classic Waves for a Romantic Boho Hairstyle: How to Recreate It

During her three-day tour of India, the Princess of Wales attended the 2026 Earthshot Prize Ceremony in Mumbai. Prince William founded the initiative in 2020, and it awards environmental solutions with grants to scale and advance their projects.

For the event, Kate wore an oxblood, floor-length Anarkali dress by designer Mahima Mahajan and paired it with extra-fluffy, voluminous bohemian curls pinned in a half-updo.

An elegant, extra-glossy wavy hairstyle has long been a beauty signature for the princess, often teamed with a side part, hats, or a polished ponytail. But for the green carpet in India, the royal opted for a much more dramatic style that matches the glamorous mood of her outfit, with tighter, cascading bohemian curls woven into a beautiful half-updo twist for a romantic finish.

"I would create these curls using a medium-sized tong," recommends Philipp Haug, co-founder of Haug London Haus salon. "Take relatively small sections and use the tongs with a twisting technique, curling one section away from the face and alternating with one section towards the face throughout. Slightly root stretch when you come to the top of the head instead of curling too close to the scalp."

To enhance the fluffiness and volume of the curls, shake them out with your fingers instead of combing them.

#6855. ‘I’m Excited to F*ck People Who Read’: Inside the New Dating App for Book Lovers

Readme wants to make modern dating more intellectually stimulating.

It was nearly 10:00 p.m. on a busy Thursday night in New York City when four young women brushed past a security guard at Funny Bar, a sexy late-night steakhouse, and crashed their way into the party. They made a beeline for the dance floor, knocking into me. Vodka splashed from my martini glass. This was the launch party for Readme, a dating app for readers.

Until the interlopers arrived, the room had been inundated with writers, publishers, podcasters, and other social intellectuals—people who loved to read or pretended they do. Now it was reaching full tilt. I squeezed between partygoers to ask Caroline Calloway—socialite, author of the memoir Scammer, and Readme creative director—about her involvement with the app. “I’m so ready for the dating-app space to not be such a hellscape,” she told me. “I’m more excited than ever to fuck people who are able to read.”

That is the promise of Readme. It aims to be an oasis among the swamp of dating apps where likeminded people can find someone intellectually stimulating that they want to fuck—or at least date a couple times. Dating apps like Hinge, Tinder, and the various off-shoots just aren’t cutting it anymore. A recent Match Group report found that 78 percent of users felt burned-out from the apps, and the number of people who pay for dating apps is declining. Nearly every single person I know who’s still on the apps tells me they never actually talk to anyone out there. That’s where Readme comes in.

“Everyone knows that the existing dating-app culture is deeply unsatisfying overall,” Emily Segal, the head of brand at Readme, says. “It often makes people feel overly performative, transactional, or disposable. We realized that when someone’s taste in books and literature resonates with you, you feel seen on a much deeper level.”

Or, as Calloway told me, “Out of my four major loves, the two that were built on intellectual respect were a lot deeper.”

The tagline for Readme is that it’s for “people whose love language is language,” and it comes at a precarious time for readers. We’re in what many have dubbed the postliterate age. Reading for pleasure has fallen from 28 percent in 2004 to 16 percent in 2023. Most of us would rather doomscroll than crack open a book.

Bypassing the superficiality of modern dating apps by making singles engage with their literary side seems like a novel (pun intended) solution. The app works by taking a detailed account of people’s compatibility. Readme creators Banu Gular and Segal were also behind Co-Star, the astrological-compatibility app with more than 30 million registered users worldwide. When you create an account on Readme, you have to fill out a lengthy quiz that will result in your taste profile. “You answer questions about the books and writing you like, and it gives you a portrait of your taste in language, which we think reflects how you approach love,” Segal says. “The app gives you a portrait of your literary taste and how you see the world, which we call an archetype. You’ll see a snippet of someone’s archetype when you match with them and some information about how you might get along—how you use the information is up to you.”

Back at the launch party, I met a guy who had just moved to New York City and came as a friend’s plus-one. As I quizzed him on his recent reads, Gloria Steinem came up, and to my utter horror, he didn’t know who she was. Desperate to redeem himself, he admitted he was a big Joan Didion fan; his ex introduced him to her writing. Maybe there is hope.

#6854. This St. Bart’s House Was Designed to Look Nothing Like Its Neighbors

Built for maximum family time, a converted boutique hotel puts the emphasis on entertaining, hanging out, and blending into the surroundings.

Few clients ask their designer to make the bedrooms in their vacation home smaller and simpler, but that was just the edit the homeowners made to plans for their house in St. Bart’s, says designer David Ries. “They didn’t want these humongous bedrooms with huge floating beds and massive bathrooms.” Instead, the couple wanted to be able to host as many people as possible, a request that didn’t surprise Ries, who’d already worked with the family on homes in Manhattan, Long Island, and Telluride. “It’s what this client has always asked for. They can’t have enough bedrooms.”

Situated on a steep hillside above one of St. Bart’s famously pristine white sand beaches, the house had initially been a boutique hotel. The plan was to turn it into a retreat for themselves and their adult children and grandchildren. The family are longtime visitors to the island—“the daughter told me it was her ‘happy place,’” says Ries, lead designer of the New York-based interior design firm Ries Hayes—and knew exactly what they wanted. Not the white box look popular with luxury new builds on the island, but instead something reminiscent of the “old St. Bart’s,” says East Hampton-based landscape designer Edwina von Gal, who, like Ries, has collaborated with the clients on other projects.

A Caribbean island is a challenging place to work under any circumstances. Everything has to be brought in from far away, including all the plants, which came by barge from Miami. St. Bart’s in particular requires anyone building to work with a local architect and engineer. In their initial proposal, this French team defaulted to that “slick and modernist” look, as von Gal describes it, with the oversized bedrooms and white color scheme that dominates much of the island’s luxury housing stock.

The design team pushed back—asking for a lot of bedrooms (eight) and generous living spaces. They also had strong opinions on the building materials. Given the island’s climate, the humid, salt-soaked air can be hard on everything. “If we did white paint, which is prominent throughout St. Bart's, it’s constant maintenance, constant painting,” says Ries. The design team insisted on cladding the buildings in Ipe, the durable tropical hardwood, which wears well. It mystified the local architects and set the complex apart from its neighbors. It also makes the buildings disappear into the greenery of the hillside.

But the architects quickly caught on to the new plans, and von Gal is eager to praise their technical accomplishments. Regulations require properties to conserve rainwater, so there are cisterns beneath the buildings, plus technology infrastructure and laundry facilities. “Working on the hill is so complex,” she says. “It's such a tight little package of technology and function. What they did was amazing.”

The team decided to reconfigure the property as a series of pavilions that face the view of the ocean and surrounding hills. The main entry is at the top of the steep hill, along with parking. The next level down is for family bedrooms; below that is a primary suite and study; and finally the fourth level is the main living space, including a playroom. “We made sure this playroom was front and center,” says Ries. “My client loves her grandkids.”

The playroom and study have built-in daybeds that can be used as spare guest rooms in a pinch. But the clients did get their eight bedrooms. “We know how to make compact bedrooms and make them very efficient, make them very livable, which allows us to fit in a lot of space for a lot of guests, and then that puts a lot of emphasis on having main entertaining spaces that are big and gracious,” says Ries. “The idea is that everybody hangs out together.”

Everything is united by generous outdoor spaces, which wind between the bedrooms. “Every room has an amazing view,” says Ries. “It feels almost like your own little treehouse in a way.” The bottom living area opens out to a pool and an expansive deck where the family eats most of their meals.

The house has an elevator, but stairs are the primary way the family moves between levels and finally down to the beach. “We hung on for a really great, gracious, fun set of stairs,” says von Gal. “They really introduce you to how amazing the different levels are and all the wonderful secret places.”

Durable fabrics and materials were chosen throughout and Ries made sure everything could be easily moved around and reconfigured. Outside on the main deck, three long dining tables from Sutherland were outfitted with casters so they could be maneuvered into a single long table or several smaller ones; 26 lightweight dining chairs can be pulled from different parts of the deck. If the infamous “Christmas winds” bring a downpour, the tables can even be moved indoors so a dinner party can proceed in inclement weather. Outdoor seating can be arranged as a 15-foot sofa or several love seats.

“It's so flexible,” says Ries. “We know this from working with a client that their needs for entertaining change every day, or sometimes a couple times a day.”

The kitchen is also efficient, designed to produce easy, clean meals. The family would rather spend time in the pool than over a stove. And no part of the living room is oriented around a TV. “They're readers,” says Ries. “There’s always stacks of new books when you go to their homes. So there’s all these cool little spots you can tuck into.”

Ultimately, the client and design team’s insistence on what they wanted paid off—but it wasn’t without a few moments of doubt. “When so many people are telling you ‘no, surely this is not what you want,’ it does start to erode your confidence a little,” says von Gal. “Then when it does come out well, there’s a real sense of triumph and satisfaction.”

Elizabeth Angell is a journalist and editor who writes about style, travel, parenting, and the British Royal Family. She has written for The New York Times and Town & Country and was most recently the Editor in Chief of Romper. She lives in Brooklyn, New York with her family.

#6850. Claude Intros Sonnet 5.5 with Model-Switching Cybersecurity Safeguards

Anthropic's new Claude release, Sonnet 5.5, brings cybersecurity safeguards that can change which model handles a request during a Claude conversation. Higher-risk tasks can fall back to Sonnet 5.

According to Anthropic's explanation of model switching, automatic fallback is enabled by default in Claude. Users receive a notice, and the answer is labeled with the model that provided it. The chat then stays on Sonnet 5 unless the user changes the selection.

The company identifies penetration testing and exploit generation among activities that may trigger cybersecurity fallbacks. Source-code vulnerability scanning remains supported. Biology and reasoning-extraction safeguards can block requests outright, rather than route them elsewhere.

The distinction matters operationally. A block stops the request; a fallback allows an attempt to continue with a different model. Neither outcome should be treated as interchangeable with completion by the model the user originally chose.

Anthropic says Sonnet 5.5 generates output more than 30% faster than Sonnet 5 and can reduce cost per task by up to 30%, based on its testing. Token prices remain unchanged.

Those claims describe performance under the company's test conditions. An organization evaluating the service also needs to examine what happens when its own assignments encounter safeguards.

An internal pilot could record how often work is interrupted, whether fallback answers are usable, and when users need help. Such measurements would give buyers a more relevant basis for deployment than assuming every task receives the advertised model's capabilities.

Anthropic has announced plans to expand its Cyber Verification Program with tiered access for Sonnet 5.5, Opus 5.5, and Mythos models. Its current program guidance explicitly excludes the two new 5.5 models and says the expansion is forthcoming.

Organizations should distinguish those planned arrangements from access available today.

The broader enterprise issue is accountability. When an AI service changes models during an assignment, someone still needs to determine whether the resulting work is suitable. A visible handoff helps, but organizations must decide how employees should respond to it.

For more information on Sonnet 5.5, go to the Anthropic site.

#6849. Jennifer Lawrence Sets the Record Straight on Those Blepharoplasty Rumors

In this day and age, it’s almost a rite of passage for a celebrity to have plastic surgery rumors about them swirling online, but Jennifer Lawrence has been speaking her truth on the matter as of late.

Her makeup artist, Shayna Gold, shared a mailer of products from the actress’s new haircare line, Mes, and Lawrence reposted the photo to her own story with a cheeky caption. “Iykyk she’s the one who did my bleph,” Lawrence wrote, crediting Gold and pointing to makeup as the reason she looks so wide awake, officially poking holes in any eyelid surgery rumors.

While fans aren’t exactly sold on Lawrence’s denial of a bleph, makeup artists often do have tricks up their sleeves for faking an eyelid lift. For starters, placement matters. When emphasizing the crease, apply shadow above the natural crease. “I like patting shadows onto the lid first, then blending the edges after. Too much back-and-forth blending can make the shape disappear,” celebrity makeup artist Christian Briceno previously told Bazaar. Also, opt for an elongated look, like a soft wing, to make the eye look instantly bigger. This also applies to mascara—artists recommend focusing application on the outer corners versus the center.

#6843. Convergence of AI, Quantum Risks Is Impacting Cybersecurity

Recent research from Thales highlights how AI adoption and emerging quantum computing threats are putting pressure on existing security measures. The 2026 Thales Data Threat Report: Quantum & AI Trends, based on responses from 3,120 security and IT management professionals across 20 countries, found that 98% are considering how the two technologies affect each other. Thirty percent reported a significant increase in security budgets specifically for AI.

The research, conducted by 451 Research by S&P Global, examines both the risks and potential benefits of the technologies. Fifty-seven percent believe quantum computing will enhance machine learning, while 54% expect it to enable advanced simulations of complex systems. The security findings span data governance, cloud infrastructure, and preparations for quantum-resistant encryption.

Cloud-based storage, cloud-delivered applications, and cloud management infrastructure were the three most frequently cited asset types targeted by attackers, at 35%, 34%, and 32%, respectively. The report connects that exposure to the large datasets required for AI training and the rapid expansion of AI infrastructure.

Organizations are also looking to cloud providers for protection: 67% reported investing in their cloud provider's AI-specific security tools, compared with 63% using an established security provider. Respondents could identify multiple sources of protection, including startups, large language model providers, and internally developed controls.

Encryption coverage remains incomplete. Only 7% of organizations reported encrypting more than 80% of their sensitive cloud data, while 29% reported encrypting more than 60%. The report's chart puts the average share encrypted at 47% in 2026, down from 51% in 2025.

The findings extend concerns raised in coverage of Thales' 2025 cloud security research, when AI security already ranked second to cloud security in spending priorities and 52% of respondents reported that AI security spending was displacing existing security budgets. The latest report again places AI security second, while its historical comparison shows average sensitive cloud data encryption coverage falling from 51% in 2025 to 47% in 2026.

AI security was the second-highest prioritized security spending category overall, behind cloud security. But protecting data ranked much lower among the measures organizations use to judge AI projects: Reducing or managing risks of data loss or noncompliance placed sixth among seven success criteria, at 35%.

Improving customer experience led those measures at 68%, followed by reducing employee toil through greater task automation at 63%. Meanwhile, poor data quality or initial data governance was the leading inhibitor to AI adoption, cited by 65%; security risks from exposed data followed at 61%. Another 51% cited AI initiatives moving too quickly to be properly secured.

In an active social engineering campaign, cyber attackers are impersonating IT help desks and using fake passkey setup requests to compromise employee identities and gain access to enterprise cloud data.

Microsoft is expanding the scope of its AI-powered Project Perception security platform to address the growing concern that AI agents may have access to sensitive information and systems, yet don't always behave as expected.

Ransomware attacks are becoming more automated, and AI could take that much further, according to cybersecurity company NCC Group's July 2026 threat intelligence report.

Avantis Education, the creator of ClassVR, has introduced two new immersive learning libraries for its Eduverse+ platform: EscapeRooms, a math suite for K-8 students, and LingoLab, a Spanish and English language learning suite for students in grades 6-12.

#6838. Flashpoint Receives Patent for Ransomware Risk Model, Tying Vulnerabilites to Likelihood of Attack

Cybersecurity company Flashpoint announced it has received U.S. Patent No. 12,705,360 for its Ransomware Risk model, a methodology designed to help security teams determine which newly disclosed vulnerabilities more closely resemble those historically used in ransomware attacks.

The patent comes at a time when security teams are confronting a growing vulnerability problem: Flashpoint's Global Threat Intelligence Report: 2026 Midyear Edition tracked 21,667 vulnerability disclosures during the first half of 2026, an 8% increase.

Of those, 4,015, or around 19 percent, already had public or functional exploit code. Flashpoint also reported a 45 percent increase in Ransomware-as-a-Service (RaaS) attacks during the period.

The patent was granted August 11 2026, although the underlying Ransomware Risk model isn't new. It has been available through Flashpoint Vulnerability Intelligence since 2022.

The model addresses a basic vulnerability-management problem: severity and real-world ransomware risk aren't necessarily the same thing, because severity scores do not necessarily indicate which flaws attackers are most likely to target.

The Common Vulnerability Scoring System, or CVSS, measures how severe a vulnerability could be. The Exploit Prediction Scoring System, or EPSS, estimates the probability of exploitation more broadly.

Flashpoint's Ransomware Risk model adds another dimension by comparing newly disclosed vulnerabilities with the characteristics of flaws previously exploited in ransomware attacks.

That could change where a vulnerability lands in an organization's patching queue. A lower-severity flaw might normally sit behind numerous critical vulnerabilities. But if its characteristics closely resemble vulnerabilities repeatedly targeted by ransomware operators, security teams may have reason to move it higher.

"A severity score alone can't tell you which vulnerabilities pose the greatest real-world risk of exploitation by ransomware actors," Flashpoint CEO Josh Lefkowitz said. "Our patented Ransomware Risk model applies years of threat intelligence to that problem, giving customers an earlier signal and helping them prioritize based on how attackers actually operate."

The firm's midyear research also illustrates the scale of the prioritization problem. Its vulnerability intelligence covers traditional IT systems as well as cloud, IoT, operational technology, open source software. and third-party libraries.

The company says it also tracks more than 105,000 vulnerabilities that aren't represented in CVE or the National Vulnerability Database, expanding the pool of flaws that security teams may need to evaluate.

The cybersecurity company's patented methodology profiles vulnerabilities using more than 60 technical characteristics, including whether a flaw can be exploited remotely without authentication, whether it affects operational technology systems, and how exploitation could affect data availability.

Those characteristics form a multi-factor fingerprint. The model maps each vulnerability against clusters containing flaws previously exploited in ransomware attacks and assigns a Low, Medium, High, or Critical Ransomware Risk rating.

Flashpoint says the rating can be generated when a vulnerability is disclosed, rather than requiring security teams to wait for public evidence of active exploitation. Ratings are then updated as new ransomware activity and threat intelligence emerge.

Within Flashpoint Ignite, Ransomware Risk appears alongside CVSS, EPSS, Social Risk, and exploit maturity. The idea isn't to replace existing scoring systems, but to give administrators additional information when deciding what to remediate first.

Nor does a High or Critical Ransomware Risk rating mean a vulnerability will necessarily be exploited. The model measures how closely its characteristics resemble vulnerabilities historically associated with ransomware attacks.

For security teams facing thousands of potential vulnerabilities, that distinction matters. The flaw carrying the highest severity score isn't necessarily the one that most closely resembles what ransomware operators have historically chosen to attack.

"Despite being the most popular indicator for priority, we know that severity alone doesn't tell you whether a vulnerability will be exploited or used in ransomware," said Ben Haynes, Data Science & Analytics Lead at Flashpoint and co-inventor of the patented methodology.

"A vulnerability can score low on a traditional severity scale and still share the characteristics we repeatedly see in ransomware attacks."

For more information, go to the Flashpoint site.

According to a survey by data and AI services provider Further, enterprises are reporting benefits from artificial intelligence even as their data preparation and oversight processes remain incomplete.

Our nation's largest early childhood program has not fully caught up with what science now tells us about how children learn.

A recent Cloud Security Alliance report urges organizations to inventory, govern, and continuously monitor machine and agent identities.

Recent research from Thales highlights how AI adoption and emerging quantum computing threats are putting pressure on existing security measures.

#6831. SailPoint Intros New Capabilities to Address Agentic AI Identity Security Challenge

AI agents are moving from experiments into everyday enterprise environments, but the security controls around them are not keeping pace, according to enterprise identity security company SailPoint. In its latest Horizons of Identity Security report, SailPoint found that 79% of enterprises already have AI agents running in production, while just 2% have deployed identity security tools specifically designed to govern them.

That gap could become harder to ignore as organizations give agents more autonomy. Agents can increasingly access applications, sensitive data, and credentials, and in some cases take actions on a user's behalf, effectively creating a new class of identity that IT and security teams need to manage.

SailPoint, which develops identity security and access governance technology for enterprises, is responding with a collection of new identity security capabilities announced at its Navigate 2026 conference. The company is expanding its Agentic Fabric to discover "shadow AI" agents and provide runtime authorization and controls capable of immediately disabling an agent.

The company also introduced what it calls SailPoint Autonomous Agents, specialized AI agents designed to govern other agents. The security agents can monitor runtime activity, intercept threats, and adjust permissions while allowing legitimate agents to continue operating, SailPoint said.

The idea reflects one of the challenges created by increasingly autonomous AI systems: Traditional human approval processes may not be fast enough to govern software capable of taking large numbers of actions on its own.

"The idea that a security admin is going to review and approve access for a group of autonomous agents making 10,000 tool calls a second isn't just outdated — it's a fantasy," SailPoint CTO Chandra Gnanasambandam said.

Another part of SailPoint's approach focuses on visibility.

New endpoint and browser sensors, along with SIEM and XDR telemetry and non-human identity discovery capabilities, are designed to uncover agents, MCP servers, credentials, tools, and data stores that haven't been brought under an organization's identity program.

The company is also introducing Autonomous Identity Security Posture Management, or A-ISPM, which uses identity information from its Atlas platform to identify dormant accounts, orphaned access, shadow administrators, and other potentially risky access paths. Organizations can then revoke access, disable an identity or AI agent, assign an owner or automate enforcement.

Just-in-Time provisioning is another part of the strategy. Rather than giving people, service accounts, and AI agents permanent access, permissions can be granted when needed and subjected to conditions such as reauthentication or a business justification.

Capabilities across SailPoint's Agentic Fabric, Human Fabric, and Atlas platforms are beginning to roll out globally. A-ISPM and several other announced features are expected in the company's fourth quarter of fiscal 2027.

"Autonomous agents are no longer just experimental copilots; they are becoming the primary execution layer of modern enterprises," SailPoint President Matt Mills said.

For more information, go to the SailPoint site.

In an active social engineering campaign, cyber attackers are impersonating IT help desks and using fake passkey setup requests to compromise employee identities and gain access to enterprise cloud data.

Microsoft is expanding the scope of its AI-powered Project Perception security platform to address the growing concern that AI agents may have access to sensitive information and systems, yet don't always behave as expected.

Ransomware attacks are becoming more automated, and AI could take that much further, according to cybersecurity company NCC Group's July 2026 threat intelligence report.

Avantis Education, the creator of ClassVR, has introduced two new immersive learning libraries for its Eduverse+ platform: EscapeRooms, a math suite for K-8 students, and LingoLab, a Spanish and English language learning suite for students in grades 6-12.

#6830. The Coolest Fashion Hub of the ’00s Was in a SoHo Basement

A new book captures the It girl scene at 163 Mercer Street

Back when Marc Jacobs was dressing the coolest girls of the aughts, retail wunderkind Robert Rich was his secret weapon. Not unlike Truman Capote and his "swans," Rich welcomed friends such as Kate Moss, Selma Blair, Sofia Coppola, Maya Rudolph, Natasha Lyonne, Parker Posey, and Winona Ryder to his basement office in the brand’s downtown flagship store, documenting fittings and gab sessions with a Polaroid camera. Now, 163 Mercer, a new book from IDEA named for the store’s address, shares stories and photographs of fashion’s golden years just before digital changed everything.

Rich's first book, Winona, made with Francesca Sorrenti, debuted in 2023 after he spotted merchandise on IDEA’s Instagram feed emblazoned with the name “Winona.” "I reached out and asked if they wanted to do a Winona Polaroid book, and they immediately replied, 'Yes!'" he recalled over Zoom. It sold out on day one, and a second printing followed the next year. The experience left him wanting more creative outlets.

163 Mercer is “directed” by Zoe Cassavetes, the director, screenwriter, and actress, who was the sales director of the nearby Mercer Hotel in the ’90s. She met Rich when Jacobs and Marc Jacobs International president Robert Duffy sent her to the store to pick out a thank-you gift for her professional hospitality at the Mercer Hotel, where Jacobs often stayed.

The duo, who are so close that they still speak on the phone every day, began brainstorming a project. "I had the photos of all the other girls, so I knew I wanted to do a book with those pictures," Rich said. They considered wallpaper based on the wall collage or a podcast featuring a different one of the girls per episode, but Cassavetes's film background, including her 2007 directorial debut Broken English, made her the perfect book creative partner. IDEA said yes again.

They worked at Cassavetes’s Los Angeles apartment, laying out images on her oversized office table. "It's a lot like writing a script, which is why I say I ‘directed’ the book; putting together the layout was like making a movie," she said. Rich occasionally asked her to add an edited image, and she agreed. "We have similar tastes, and we were open to each other's opinions. He has a cool eye," she added.

Their bond developed when Cassavetes worked down the street. "My title at the Mercer was director of sales, but I was really a VIP manager. I had a basement office, too," she recalled. Before New York's indoor smoking ban in 2003, the two took cigarette breaks indoors. "The logistics helped our friendship, hanging out in each other's offices smoking and having tea."

The book features images of regular visitors plus some who came once or twice, and the women graciously cataloged their memories for it. Don't expect captions throughout; it reads in parts like an IYKYK story asking viewers to figure out who is in the photo. "We didn't caption every Polaroid; they weren't like social media posts," Rich explained.

Nostalgia for an era before our feeds made the post-Covid years the perfect time for Rich to revisit his mementos. The book should appeal to members of Gen Z who are wondering what they missed before social media, and those in Gen X who are longing for a time that felt authentic, rather than conceived in a marketing strategy.

Trust bound everyone involved. Rich knew Moss previously, and Cassavetes knew Sofia and Ryder; Rich met the others on the job. Ryder came into the shop independently, and Rich professed his love of Girl, Interrupted. “’That's a girl's movie,’ she said, and I said, ‘I'm a girl's movie guy,’” Rich recalled. Ryder's infamous 2001 shoplifting case led to her starring in the brand's spring 2003 campaign, appearing on its "Protect the Skin You're In" T-shirts and wearing Marc Jacobs to court.

"Selma didn't know the others,” Rich said. “We hit it off when she tried on the Courrèges–inspired collection. I took Polaroids, sent some to Marc, and kept others. She kept coming in. I introduced her to Kate, Missy, and Frankie Rayder, all of them.”

The mood differed from today's brand-celebrity ecosystem of mutual monetary gain. "It was unpretentious; no one was there to get paid. We were hanging out and having fun. That was the Marc Jacobs spirit: more artsy, less corporate. It was a perfect zeitgeist moment," Cassavetes said.

Case in point: Rich met Hilary Swank before her Oscar win for Boys Don't Cry. "She would come to the store, and I would dress her up for all the pre-Oscar award shows and events. I put her in a tiered ruffle sundress with a beachy vibe for the Independent Spirit Award in 2000," he recalled.

Impromptu gatherings were plentiful. Once, Moss, Ryder, and Patric Reeves ended up in the office together. "I stocked my fridge with Sofia champagne cans. They were small but potent," Rich said with a chuckle. An image in the book documents the mini party. Though Polaroids waned in popularity after the brand declared bankruptcy and digital cameras and camera phones arrived, Rich kept a stockpile he used until 2015.

"Sometimes I wouldn't even recognize Winona because she had a new role, hair color, or style," he said. "But I met all the boyfriends, watched them get married, pregnant, have babies, and divorce."

Getting It girls to wear Marc Jacobs was simple. "Marc's clothes were unbelievable; you didn't have to beg them," said Rich, adding that the store's cool yet friendly vibe helped. "I was a big fan of Marc's, and that radiated to everyone I hired," including bartenders, who made great sales associates because "they knew how to talk to people." A generous clothing allowance, four outfits a season instead of uniforms, fueled employee enthusiasm.

Cassavetes remembers Rich's relaxed styling approach. "He made sure the girls didn't wear the same thing." Rich credits his former boss. "Marc liked everybody's take on his clothes. Winona would change the buttons or redo straps; he loved it."

Costume designer June Ambrose's favorite memories include discovering hidden treasures in the basement. "Robert has an incredible eye and instinctively knew what I needed; I could trust him. He understood the woman, the attitude, and the personality behind the clothes," she wrote in an email. "He made me fall in love with Marc Jacobs."

Rich also gave his editor and stylist friends early access to sample sales.

His Marc Jacobs career began when Joe Zee took him to his first runway show for the brand. "I was blown away and marched backstage and asked Marc for a job," Rich said. In 1999, he became store manager, eventually rising to vice president of public relations for retail stores during his tenure.

Before 163 Mercer became the Marc Jacobs store in 1997, it was once the Tony Shafrazi Gallery, which propelled the careers of Francis Bacon, David LaChapelle, and Keith Haring, for whom Rich once worked. "There was nothing in SoHo; Marc could have picked any space. There was good energy there. Tony would stop in and regret having given the space up," Rich said, noting Jacobs kicked off SoHo’s current designer shopping enclave.

"The store was so laid-back and a fun, creative safe space. It was also the center of the downtown fashion world at that time," Cassavetes added.

Rich hints that a third book would move away from SoHo. "We wouldn't just be in the office. Maybe it would be pictures from everywhere."

#6826. Survey: Most Organizations Are Not Consistently Measuring AI Value Against Costs

A new study from consulting firm KPMG found that while AI investment continues to grow, relatively few organizations consistently measure whether the technology's benefits justify its costs. The company's Global AI Pulse Q3 2026 report found that 89% of organizations report measurable value from AI, while just 12% consistently assess that value against costs across the organization.

Average planned AI investment for the next 12 months has risen to $210 million, compared with $186 million in the first quarter of 2026.

The findings are based on responses from 2,131 senior leaders across 20 countries and jurisdictions, surveyed between July 23 and August 26, 2026.

Most organizations have established ways to track AI spending: 61% review costs during project approval, and 59% monitor them once systems are operating.

Consistently connecting those costs to value remains less common. Beyond the 12% that do so across the organization, another 26% assess value against costs for most initiatives.

Organizations reporting established AI returns have stronger measurement practices. Among this group, 48% consistently assess value against costs, four times the proportion in the overall survey group.

Cost-monitoring dashboards are also more common among organizations reporting established returns: 77% use them, compared with 43% of those still experimenting.

"The organizations seeing a return did the implementation and the transformation properly: guardrails in place, workforce ready, data cleaned up," said Priya Emmanuel, global head of aIQ at KPMG International. "That is a sequence, not a secret."

Organizations are also developing more formal systems to manage AI deployment.

Fifty-five percent operate a formal AI management layer, which KPMG calls a "harness layer," comprising controls and tools between AI models and business applications.

Security and identity controls, along with data access controls, were the most common capabilities, each reported by 43% of respondents. Model routing, which determines which model handles a particular task, was reported by 23%.

Meanwhile, 53% assign accountability for AI-informed decisions to a named executive, the CEO, or an executive committee.

The proportion reporting significant employee adoption of AI agents rose from 25% in the first quarter to 34% in the third quarter.

Cybersecurity concerns were more common among organizations with mature AI programs.

Half of those reporting established AI returns identified cybersecurity as a barrier, compared with roughly one-third of those still experimenting.

Overall, 86% of respondents said their organizations are adapting their cybersecurity operating models to address AI-accelerated threats.

The report identifies accountability, operational resilience, and cost management as priorities as AI becomes more embedded in business operations.

"Many organizations have made AI models widely available and track their use," said Swaminathan Chandrasekaran, head of the US AI Center of Excellence at KPMG. "But consumption alone is not a strategy."

For the full report, visit the KPMG site.

In an active social engineering campaign, cyber attackers are impersonating IT help desks and using fake passkey setup requests to compromise employee identities and gain access to enterprise cloud data.

Microsoft is expanding the scope of its AI-powered Project Perception security platform to address the growing concern that AI agents may have access to sensitive information and systems, yet don't always behave as expected.

Ransomware attacks are becoming more automated, and AI could take that much further, according to cybersecurity company NCC Group's July 2026 threat intelligence report.

Avantis Education, the creator of ClassVR, has introduced two new immersive learning libraries for its Eduverse+ platform: EscapeRooms, a math suite for K-8 students, and LingoLab, a Spanish and English language learning suite for students in grades 6-12.

#6824. Curtain Bangs Are an Elegant, Low-Maintenance Style for Mature Hair: How to Wear It Now

Timeless and face-framing, this is the easiest way to update your haircut

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

From Sabrina Carpenter to Dakota Johnson, curtain bangs were among the most fawned-over hairstyles in the past few years. It’s not surprising; they flatter all face shapes and offer the face-framing effect of a fringe without the harshness (or any awkward growing-out stage) of a full, blunt chop.

Curtain bangs are also versatile at any age, and the cut has recently stepped into the spotlight as one of the most flattering styles for mature lengths. Penélope Cruz’s recent red-carpet appearance makes a convincing case for the look.

"Curtain bangs are one of the most flattering fringe options for mature hair because they soften the face rather than dominate it," explains hairdresser David Cassidy from Hove's Palette & Bristle. "They frame the cheekbones, draw attention to the eyes, and create movement around the front of the hair, which instantly feels more youthful."

Unlike a blunt fringe, he adds that curtain bangs blend into the rest of the haircut, creating an elegant, modern look with just the right amount of youthfulness.

Here, he explains how to get the perfect curtain fringe for mature hair this winter, along with key styling pointers.

As with any bangs cut, an expert eye and professional hand are key to achieving a look that suits your face shape.

"The key with curtain bangs on mature hair is softness and proportion," says Cassidy. "You want them to start no shorter than the cheekbone and taper gently into the length. Anything too short or too heavy can feel aging."

He also emphasizes texture; a slightly broken-up finish keeps them from looking flat and gives a more relaxed, lived-in effect.

"Before committing to curtain bangs, think about your natural hair texture and growth pattern," he adds. "Cowlicks, partings, and how your hair falls when it air-dries all matter. A good stylist will tailor the fringe to work with your hair, not against it. Curtain bangs are versatile. When done well, they grow out seamlessly and can be worn parted, swept back, or tucked away."

Before you veer away from your signature everyday style, consider how much extra styling time a new fringe might require.

"Curtain bangs are surprisingly low-maintenance if they’re cut properly," says Cassidy. "A medium round brush or even a large Velcro roller at the front while the hair cools will give that soft sweep away from the face."

He recommends styling your curtain bangs with the hair dryer angled side to side rather than straight down. "It creates lift without stiffness and keeps your bangs looking light and flattering."

#6822. Your Capricorn Monthly Horoscope for December 2026

It’s time to ditch the practical and turn toward the mystical, Capricorn.

#6820. Report: Cyber Attacks Increasingly Exploit Trusted Workplace Tools

In its latest threat intelligence report, Ontinue found that attackers are increasingly using trusted workplace tools to trick users into giving them access to enterprise networks. Approximately 30% of organizations monitored by the cybersecurity company experienced at least one attempt to impersonate IT support through Microsoft Teams during the first half of 2026. About one in every 990 external Teams contacts showed signs of someone pretending to be an internal IT worker.

Ontinue's report says the trouble is that these attacks blend in with ordinary workday communication, which makes them harder to catch.

"The majority of effective attacks today don't rely on sophisticated exploits. They rely on trust," Balazs Greksza, director of Advanced Threat Operations at Ontinue, said in the company's announcement.

Ontinue broke down how a typical attack works: An employee gets an unexpected Teams message or call from someone claiming to work in the organization's IT department. The person warns of a security issue, a software problem or an update that can't wait. Then they walk the employee through opening Windows Quick Assist, installing remote-access software, or running a PowerShell command. Following those steps can hand the attacker control of the employee's computer, often without the employee realizing it.

The approach is very similar to the Black Basta ransomware group, which helped popularize similar attacks in 2024. Unfortunately, Ontinue found the technique has since been adopted by other cybercriminal groups, including some with no known link to Black Basta.

Some threat agents are also combining tactics. They might first overwhelm an employee's inbox with unwanted e-mails, then reach out through Teams pretending to be IT support offering to fix the problem.

"Phishing no longer resides solely in your inbox. It has spread across the entire corporate communications environment," said Mika Aalto, co-founder and CEO of Hoxhunt.

Teams impersonation is only part of the problem. Ontinue also found attackers taking advantage of legitimate Microsoft sign-in processes. Between May and June, researchers identified 10 confirmed device-code phishing compromises across five customer tenants.

In these attacks, criminals trick employees into completing what appears to be a normal Microsoft authentication process. But doing so can give the attacker access to the employee's account and system without actually breaking their password.

The report also found attackers using blockchain-based infrastructure to hide malicious activity, accounting for more than 25% of confirmed malicious ClickFix findings.

"Attackers are no longer looking for gaps in your perimeter. They are looking for the systems your organization already trusts and finding ways to move through them undetected," said Shane Barney, chief information security officer at Keeper Security.

The security firm says that IT needs to keep a closer eye on unexpected Teams contacts, remote-support requests, suspicious PowerShell activity, and changes to authentication settings. Employees should also confirm sensitive requests through established internal channels, rather than relying on the name or account that appears on their screen.

Aviv Nahum, co-founder and CEO of Above Security, noted that AI is making these attacks more convincing by helping criminals imitate familiar voices, writing styles, and conversations.

"The security model therefore must shift from 'does this look real?' to 'can I independently verify that this person is who they claim to be?'" Nahum said.

The full Ontinue report can be found here.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

In an active social engineering campaign, cyber attackers are impersonating IT help desks and using fake passkey setup requests to compromise employee identities and gain access to enterprise cloud data.

Microsoft is expanding the scope of its AI-powered Project Perception security platform to address the growing concern that AI agents may have access to sensitive information and systems, yet don't always behave as expected.

Ransomware attacks are becoming more automated, and AI could take that much further, according to cybersecurity company NCC Group's July 2026 threat intelligence report.

Avantis Education, the creator of ClassVR, has introduced two new immersive learning libraries for its Eduverse+ platform: EscapeRooms, a math suite for K-8 students, and LingoLab, a Spanish and English language learning suite for students in grades 6-12.

#6818. On the Couch with Valentina De Santis

The third-generation Lake Como hotelier shares her favorite places to travel, shop, and dine—both in Italy and abroad.

If Eloise had the Plaza, Valentina De Santis had the Grand Hotel Tremezzo, the luxury Lake Como resort her family has owned and operated for three generations. A Como native who spent her early career consulting at Bain & Company, she began working at the property in an official capacity in 2010, the year of its centenary. “Unofficially,” she tells us, “I clocked in as a child, running through the corridors and convinced the whole hotel depended on me.”

In 2016, De Santis took over as CEO and, by 2022, she and her parents had bought a nearby 18th-century villa and transformed it into another five-star hotel, Villa Passalacqua. As De Santis’s portfolio continues to expand, she finds comfort in working with her family and the team that’s known her for her whole life: “People who grow with you, believe in what you are building, and care about it as much as you do, that’s everything.”

In our On the Couch survey, we asked De Santis about all the places, people, and pieces that inspire her. She also divulges a few of her best travel recommendations—both in Como and beyond.

Describe your studio in three words: Full. Of. Chaos.

Greatest Influences: My parents, my first and forever creative directors.

Favorite Eras of Design: The Belle Époque, when more was more and every room seemed to have a little champagne in its veins.

Favorite Sofa: A Daisy curved Italian midcentury sofa from the 1950s. It's beautifully sculptural, it's pink. It's Gio Ponti.

Maverick design style trick: Break one rule. If everything in a room is behaving, something is missing.

Most beloved buildings: Villa Necchi Campiglio in Milan. When I lived in the city, it was right on my doorstep, so I was there all the time. And always, always a little hello to the pink flamingos next door!

Architect you admire: Not the big name you’d expect, but the one closest to my heart, Ico Parisi. He made a real difference in my family’s story. He taught my parents how to look at art, and how to love it, and I grew up seeing the world through that same eye.

Destination Museum: The Peggy Guggenheim Collection in Venice and the Fondation Maeght in Saint-Paul-de-Vence. Before they were museums, they were the homes of great collectors, and it shows. You half expect the host to walk in and offer you a drink. With Casabianca, we hope to bring the same magic to my parents’ collection. Peggy, I hope you approve.

Go-to galleries: Lia Rumma in Milan. It has a strong point of view and never plays it safe. It is where we found one of Anselm Kiefer’s Women of Antiquity, which now lives at Casabianca. I’ll never forget my mother’s face the day we saw her; that was when we knew she was coming home with us.

Favorite Artist: Mark Rothko. My wildest dream is to have one in my living room, one of those enormous canvases where color doesn’t just sit on the surface; it explodes, then slowly pulls you in.

Vintage Piece of Your Dreams: An original Margherita by Franco Albini for Bonacina. Yes, I know, more from the ’50s! I clearly have a type. I love how sculptural it is, while the woven rattan keeps it feeling warm and relaxed.

Favorite getaway: Puglia. I have spent so many summers there with my family that it feels like a second home.

Hotels to Check Into: The Amalfi Coast is hard to beat. In my opinion, some of the best hotels in the world are concentrated in just a few miles, each one an incredible house. On top of that, they are all dear friends, so I would love to check into every one of them. But my favorite memory? Lemon linguine on the terrace of the Santa Caterina.

Best Shop for Gifts: Chez Dédé in Rome. Daria and Andrea are dear friends, and the co-authors of our Passalacqua book, and everything in their shop seems to come with a story. My favorite is their Lettera d’Amore necklace, a love letter you can actually wear.

Best Host/Hostess Gift: Something made with your own hands, or close enough! Olive oil from your grove, homemade biscuits, chocolate-dipped orange peel. Add a handwritten note, and it beats anything you could ever buy.

Entertaining Tips: Always have more food than you need. Italian mothers have taught me this much.

Style Icons: Lady Di. I’ve had a crush on her since I was a little girl, and honestly, I never got over it.

Fashion Designers: JJ Martin (La DoubleJ) for the extraordinary way JJ brings color, print, and joy into fashion. Emilia Wickstead for her beautiful, feminine silhouettes.

Style Tip: Go tone-on-tone. My friend Patrizia, who designs Moresi's collections, taught me there's nothing chicer than a head-to-toe single-color look.

Required Reading: Va' dove ti porta il cuore by Susanna Tamaro. It is the book I have turned to whenever I have had a big decision to make, and somehow it always brings me back to the same place: follow your heart.

Cocktail of Choice: A Bellini. It is hard to improve on a classic.

Favorite Restaurant: La Tirlindana on the lake for old-world magic. In Milan, I stay loyal to the classics: Da Giacomo and Savini never miss.

Must-Have Accessory: A great pair of unscratchable sunglasses. I live by a lake, so they are both a fashion accessory and a survival tool.

Watch to Wear: A Cartier Tank Louis Cartier in yellow gold. It has been chic for so long that it has nothing left to prove.

Favorite Jeweler: Felice Gabaglio in Como. I have known Marina and her family for as long as I can remember. My father used to take me there to choose jewelry for my mother, and I was always so excited to discover those little treasures with him. It is still where I go when I want something truly special. And now Marina’s daughters have launched JAM, a new contemporary line of their own. So now I have another very good reason to stop by.

Annie Goldsmith is the senior editor and digital lead at ELLE Decor, where she covers design, culture, style, and trends. She previously held positions at The Information, covering technology and culture, and Town & Country, writing about news, entertainment, and fashion. Her work has also appeared in Vogue, Rolling Stone, and the SF Standard.
