#6816. Most Black Friday Sales Are Fake. These Ones Aren’t.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

The Internet is a deep, dark place this week, but we found where the deals are worth it.

How do you even define a good deal anymore? The whole point of Black Friday was for retailers to make us wake up at the crack of dawn the day after Thanksgiving, wait in line, and then fight someone over a TV that was 50 percent off at Sears or Best Buy. How did they do that? They would sell a few products (doorbusters) at a loss just to physically bring people into the store. Those deals were good, and everything else, which you would buy because you were already there, was where the money was made.

That is now a thing of the past. Lines and physical altercations aside, in the past 20 years there has been a real swing in the favor of big retailers. First, we created Cyber Monday (though most people do their Black Friday shopping online already). Then the deals started a week earlier. Now, they last all month. And instead of having door busters to get you in the door, retailers just have very aggressive online marketing programs. In fact, it turns out that most Black Friday deals aren’t even deals at all. In a recent study, it was found that one in three Amazon Black Friday deals provided no true savings at all—prices were marked up earlier in the year, only to be brought back down to a standard retail value. The big retailers like Amazon and Walmart are the most to blame for these tricky strategies, but you should still be judicious when looking at any so-called Black Friday sales.

The good news? Black Friday is still filled with great deals—you just have to know where to look and what to expect. A lot of big wins revolve around private sales at more niche, direct-to-consumer stores. For example, Filson, one of our favorite outerwear brands, is having a 25-percent-off sale for Black Friday. That’s not the 75-percent-off you might have wanted, and there are other times that Filson goes 25-percent off. But the best strategy these days is to think about holiday shopping: Just buy the things you already would, but do it while they’re on sale now.

You should be especially skeptical of tech products. The increase in all tech prices has made it easy for those brands to push a 10-percent-off sale when products were 10-percent-off the current price. This is the sort of stuff we’ve been watching as the sales roll around. Based on this and years of covering sales and knowing what’s good and what’s not, we determined these are the best deals of the weekend. These are the deals our editors think are actually worth shopping this Black Friday.

Mr. Porter has an absolute goldmine of a sale. Like sure, you can grab this Brunello Cucinelli Jacket for $6,500 at 50 percent off. But it’s also filled with basics from brands like On that rarely go on sale.

Fortela is our favorite luxury westernwear brand with Italian design sensibilities. If you pay full price, you get every dollars’ worth in quality and concept. But shopping its archive sale and saving 50 percent? That’s highway robbery.

A cashmere-blend overcoat for 30 percent off is a real steal. Use code BLACKFRIDAY before it sells out.

A few days ago, the Samsung Frame Pro was 50 percent off. That deal was real, and it sold out. Today the Hisense Art TV (which we’re fans of) is 50-percent off. These deals vary daily, but today, this is the one we’d buy.

Dyson deals are confusing as fuck. You need to be knowledgeable about the model you’re buying into, and here that number matters. You will be able to find models like the V8 and the V10 for super cheap this week—but that’s because those models are old and outdated. Right now, the best and newest Dyson cordless vacuum is the V16—with all the bells and whistles, it retails at $1,100, but it’s $200 off right now.

But as someone who has used every Dyson, I know the V12 is a damn good model. And while it isn’t the newest, it still has lots of the same features as newer models and will last you years. So the fact that it’s on sale for $400 this weekend makes it a truly good deal. Also, I can’t find this deal on Amazon. For the best deals from Dyson, I would always check its actual site.

As previously mentioned, we love that many of Filson’s jackets are 25 percent off this week. This is a good example of when a brand just wants to participate in a sale, so they’re willing to mark down popular items. This jacket is so good our commerce editor Luke Guillory endorsed it last week, calling it the best $250 jacket. Guess what? Now it’s only $187. We know for a fact that this deal won’t last past Black Friday.

It’s expensive, yes, but Begg x Co. is one of the best cashmere brands anywhere in the world. $500 off a new cashmere sweater is something that doesn’t happen until the end of the season. But if you buy it now, you still have six good months of wear to get in.

Water filtration has become very expensive, especially when it comes to reverse osmosis and a countertop version that looks sleek. We have been recommending this one for over a year at $500, so the fact that it’s $150 off for once makes it a pretty good deal.

Furniture on sale is a whole category we can’t ignore. Furniture markups are a real thing, and a lot of it has to do with quality and materials. Marble is overpriced for sure, but good quality marble is worth it. We love Soho Home’s marble, and this dining table has been one of its most popular pieces for a while. Getting it for almost $3,000 off when you’re not a member is a big deal.

If you’re on the home redo kick, then a brand like Le Maé, which is offering some of its best wood pieces at 50-percent off, is a great place to start. Made with high quality materials and oak, this is one of those furniture pieces you will own forever.

If you’re a camo guy or do any non-deal hunting, then you know that Tom Beckbe jackets never go on sale—but this is your lucky week.

#6815. The 10 Best Lipsticks for Women Over 40 That Deliver Instant Hydration and Lasting Color

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

A swipe of lipstick is one of the easiest ways to look instantly put together with minimal effort. Even if you have nothing else on your face, a hint of lip color can lift your complexion. It also works as an accessory or finishing touch for even the simplest outfits.

As we get older, lips naturally feel drier and lose volume. A good balm nourishes and comforts parched lips, but if you want more pigment, many lipsticks are designed for mature skin.

First, steer clear of ultra-matte formulas, as they tend to dehydrate lips and highlight every fine lip line imaginable. They also tend to look flat.

For extra long wear, try a velvety, soft-matte formula instead. It delivers a similar, statement-making finish that's elegant while creating a pillowy, plush effect.

Satin formulas perform well, too. The gentle shine creates the illusion of healthier-looking lips, and it feels less drying throughout the day. That said, brighter shades like reds and oranges can be a little messy to apply, so apply one layer, blend with a fingertip, and blot with a tissue. Repeat to build intensity. If feathering (when the color "bleeds" into fine lines around your lips) is an issue, a clear lip liner around the perimeter should create a barrier.

It's always best to get your skincare benefits from your lip balm, but nourishing plant oils and hydrating ingredients such as glycerin and hyaluronic acid in your lipstick will help maintain hydration as the day goes on.

For makeup artist and Jones Road founder Bobbi Brown, there are no hard-and-fast rules—no matter your age. "It's about your style, what you feel most comfortable in," she tells Bazaar UK.

If you're keen on neutral tones, remember that neutral doesn't mean beige. "Pay attention to what your natural lip color is. I have a slightly bluish-purple tone to my lips. You might have more of a pink tone. It's really great to find a lipstick that really looks like your lips, and the only way is to test it on your bottom lip," says Brown, who notes that a great neutral should not wash you out.

Revered makeup artist Bobbi Brown is no stranger to creating iconic lipsticks. The Classic Lip has all the hallmarks of a modern formula: creamy, intensely hydrating, and richly pigmented. The 12-color range offers enough variety without overwhelming consumers. Expect skin-enhancing neutrals and bolder tones for any occasion.

Trinny London excels at makeup for mature skin, and this recent launch is certainly no exception. It's a glossy, mega-hydrating formula that reflects light beautifully, making lips look plumper, and it's formulated with peptides to help reduce fine lines over time. There's a full spectrum of nude shades, but we love the bold red Poppy for festive nights.

A richly pigmented, buttery-smooth formula that promises to minimize dryness without sacrificing impact. It contains plenty of nourishing oils to keep moisture lasting without feathering into fine lines. It has a rosy scent, so keep that in mind if you're sensitive to fragrance.

Everything Victoria Beckham does delivers instant polish to the lips without extra effort. This slimline lipstick is no exception. It glides on like a tinted lip balm, adding a touch more color while infusing skin with hydration. VB has perfected the art of neutrals, so expect to find easy-to-wear hues for all skin tones. Glide on, then pat to blend any hard edges. It's one of those formulas you won't need a mirror to apply.

Refillable makeup isn't new, but Lisa Eldridge has painstakingly ensured every part of her Rouge Experience lipstick is recyclable, while it still looks like it belongs on a movie star's dresser. The lipstick formula combines oil, gel, and cream to ensure all-day comfort. The color pigments build easily and sit between a soft matte and a shiny finish. It's unfragranced, too.

Creative and image director of Christian Dior Makeup, Peter Philips, reviewed Dior's bestselling lip colors worldwide to create a tightly curated collection of 10 sophisticated, wearable shades for every skin tone. Nude doesn't mean beige, according to Philips, and this line includes a complexion-lifting neutral, whatever that looks like to you. The emollient formula glides on like balmy, satin lipstick but won't budge once it sets. Expect long-wear color without the drying side effects.

When Hourglass creates matte lipsticks, rest assured they feature elevated colors and sit beautifully on the lips without highlighting fine lines, feathering, or flaking. This elegant formula feels velvety soft, with buildable pigments that stay put.

Sisley's interpretation of a shimmery, luminous lip color is as elevated as you've come to expect from this storied French brand. Phyto Rouge Shine is infused with the finest pearlescent particles in a translucent base, delivering sheer, buildable color and a plumping effect that still looks understated and grown-up.

Hermès Beauty creative director Gregoris Pyrpylis drew inspiration from Hermès scarves when designing this collection. This translates into buildable, luminous color and a smooth, tactile finish that feels like a second skin. Even the deepest shades look refined, with a radiance that brightens the face. Plus, the wide, angled bullet makes application as easy as wearing a lip balm.

Not all liquid lipsticks are created equal. Violette's soft, whipped texture is truly modern, delivering intense colors without a heavy feel. The gel-cream texture blurs out fine lines, too, so lips appear plush and smooth.

#6813. Sophos Launches Tool for Shadow AI Defense

Unauthorized artificial intelligence tools and agents are creating security risks for organizations as AI adoption increases, according to cybersecurity company Sophos. In a recent product announcement, the company highlighted the growing use of shadow AI, referring to AI tools and agents operating without formal organizational approval or oversight. The security firm cited research indicating that 69% of organizations suspect or have evidence of unauthorized AI use.

Separate research from Gartner and Cloud Security Alliance found that 82% of organizations discovered shadow AI agents in their environments during the previous year.

According to the security firm, organizations have struggled to identify which AI tools employees use, what information those tools access, and whether their use complies with security policies.

"Organizations are adopting AI faster than they can secure it, and most security teams can't answer three basic questions: which AI tools are running, what data is going into them, and whether that activity lines up with policy," said Kyle Falkenhagen, senior vice president of product management at Sophos.

The company also cited an IDC projection that the number of AI agents could reach 1.3 billion by 2028.

Sophos researchers reported observing attackers use AI agents within a customer's network to build and test nearly 80 malware modules.

The cybersecurity firm announced the general availability of Sophos AI Defense, a security offering designed to identify AI tools, monitor their activity, and enforce organizational security policies.

The product operates through Sophos Fusion, the company's cybersecurity platform, and uses information from devices, networks, identity systems, and browsers.

According to the company, AI Defense can inspect prompts, uploads, and other AI interactions to identify potentially risky activity and help prevent sensitive data exposure.

For more information, go to the Sophos site.

According to a survey by data and AI services provider Further, enterprises are reporting benefits from artificial intelligence even as their data preparation and oversight processes remain incomplete.

Our nation's largest early childhood program has not fully caught up with what science now tells us about how children learn.

A recent Cloud Security Alliance report urges organizations to inventory, govern, and continuously monitor machine and agent identities.

Recent research from Thales highlights how AI adoption and emerging quantum computing threats are putting pressure on existing security measures.

#6805. How Nara Smith Crafted Her Style From Scratch

The model-slash-chef joined Harper’s Bazaar’s The Good Buy podcast to discuss styling herself for videos, the vegetable jacket she has her eye on, and the pieces she steals from her husband

The Good Buy is a podcast from Harper’s Bazaar in which editors Leah Chernikoff and Lynette Nylander invite celebrities, designers, models, and tastemakers to talk shop: what they buy, where they got it, and why it matters. Come down the fashion rabbit hole and take a peek inside the closets and shopping carts of the world’s most stylish people. Learn how they use style to tell their stories.

It’s been three years since Nara Smith first burst onto the scene, carving out her own niche of CookTok with her highly stylized “from scratch” videos, in which she makes home-cooked versions of everyday food for her family. Narrating her process in a soft ASMR voice, she’s crafted everything from Skittles and Starbursts to chili soup and cornbread, with easy-to-follow (yet labor-intensive) recipes now immortalized in her debut cookbook, Homemade. Always dolled up and dressed in an archival ensemble or a look pulled straight off the runway, she’s blurred the lines between food and fashion—and managed to make spending a full day in the kitchen look utterly glamorous.

Smith has translated this signature formula into brand deals and partnerships, “cooking” Marc Jacobs totes and sunscreen bottles from scratch. She’s sat front row at Fashion Week, embarked on a cookbook press tour dressed in Schiaparelli vegetable prints, and starred in Skims campaigns alongside her model husband, Lucky Blue.

She’s a chef, a writer, a model, and a mother—but if there’s one title she wholeheartedly rejects, it’s that of a trad wife. “That’s one of the narratives that I have a really hard time wrapping my head around,” she told Harper’s Bazaar. “You don’t see me getting on a plane, hopping to New York, modeling, coming back—all while I have a newborn—paying bills, filming content, getting my kids dressed. Being put into a certain box, just because people think that I’m slaving away, is so weird to me. I’m a working mom who gets to go about her day in a very different way than [someone with] a normal nine-to-five job would.”

“I love dressing up. I love fashion so much,” she continued. “Sometimes my outfits aren’t the most practical, but then again fashion isn’t always practical. That’s what I do. I post videos of me cooking for my kids and my husband. It’s really not that deep.”

Now, the star joins Harper’s Bazaar’s The Good Buy to dive into where she shops, the designers catching her eye, and how she styles herself for video shoots.

“It’s my Super Bowl. It’s so fun seeing what brands send down the runway, it’s nice to see trends, it’s nice to see what everyone’s gravitating towards—but it’s also so nice catching up with friends. I’m always like, ‘I don’t know anyone,’ but then I go and I’m like, ‘Hello, hello, let me say hi to that person.’ It’s always really fun, and then I get to dress up. I’m a glam girl, fashion girl, through and through.”

“It was [an IMG Models contest.] I hash-tagged all of my photos and then one day [scout] Jeni Rose reached out to me via DM. And two weeks later, I was in Paris. I don’t know why they said ‘That girl.’ My eyebrows were so thin, I was in the highest heels you could imagine, couldn’t walk in them. I was 14. And they saw something in me that I didn’t at the time.”

“I met my husband and I moved to the States and I couldn’t work because of my visa situation. In that time, we also had two kids. So I was like, ‘Okay, I’ll stay at home with them, you go model.’ So Lucky did what he did, and after a while, I was finding my way back into modeling while also simultaneously starting to post on Instagram and TikTok. I got diagnosed with an autoimmune [disorder] which forced me to cook, and then without knowing I really just blew up and then transitioned into a content creator.

“It’s nice having built a name that is a brand, to where now I get to collaborate with brands. It’s not just me being a model or a mannequin for a brand, where I’m bringing their vision to life. Now it’s a collaboration, because they usually tap into what I have to offer and it feels like I have creative control and I can speak my mind. It feels very warm.”

“I forget [how big the audience is] because to me, it’s my phone and me in the kitchen with my tripod. I don’t have a team, I don’t have people at my house. It’s me styling myself, getting myself ready, doing the recipes, doing all of these things. And then I post it and I go about my day. But then when I leave the house and I get stopped a bunch, I’m like, ‘Oh! People actually watch me.’ And I get so excited.”

“I think about where my counter cuts me, which is around my hips, so I make sure everything hip and up is good. Sometimes the bottom is a little wonky or I’m not wearing a shoe. I feel like tops can get boring, so I try to play with silhouettes and textures or sleeves.

“Sometimes I like to match the color or the texture [to the food]. Sometimes I like to match the time period. I remember I made a milkshake and I was like, ‘When were they really popular? What were they wearing in that era?’ There’s always some element that ties it back to what I’m making.”

“It was a Bottega bag, a big brown woven one. That was my first splurge ever, and I felt sick to my stomach about it for three weeks. I thought about returning it for so long, but I’m glad I didn’t because it fits everything.”

“I don’t think I regret buying anything, because I’m really careful. If it’s not an immediate yes, I will leave the store and think about it for weeks. I remember this vintage Cartier watch that I saw two years ago and I still think about it daily. Maybe in a few years I’m going to get it, but it was just too much money to spend. Maybe when I hit 30.”

“Vintage denim. You can never have enough denim. They come in all washes, all sizes, all cuts—I love a bootcut, I love a flair, I love a straight cut. The possibilities are endless. I have been really lucky where I walk into stores and they look at me and pull me stuff. Ebay is great. Or my husband—sometimes I’ll go onto his side of the closet and I’ll get a pair and take it to the tailor. He’s never gonna know.”

“There’s this Schiaparelli jacket that just walked down the runway; it has vegetables on it. They gave it to me for this press tour, but I want to buy it when it comes out and it’s probably going to cost me an arm and a leg. It feels special. It feels like it’s in my realm. The craftsmanship is insanity, and the color black is so velvety and saturated, it pulls you in. And it fits me like a glove.

“If I do splurge, I want it to be timeless enough. If [my daughter] Rumble sees that in 30 years, she’s going to think that’s the most iconic jacket she will ever see. When there’s an estate sale, when I’ve passed? Do you know how iconic that’s gonna be?”

“I got some Sonny Angels that were really cute—these little figurines with cute heads. Also, my friend Iris [Law], she’s the most whimsical person. We never get time in Paris, but we got time in Paris, and she knows the best [shops]. She took me to this perfume place with the best Parisian perfume that has all these stories. So I kind of went ham and bought a bunch of perfumes. And one of them was a scent that Marilyn Monroe wore.”

Back in the old days of Black Friday shopping, you had to fight other members of your community—your kids’ bus driver, the people that sat next to you in church, a friend’s personal trainer—to score a good TV on sale. #6802. Denmark’s Queen Mary Delivers a Trifecta of Designer Looks During Her Historic State Visit to Colombia

From a D&G pencil skirt to a Saloni maxi dress

Denmark’s Queen Mary is looking posh and joyful on day two of her historic state visit to Colombia—a trip which makes her the first Danish queen to travel to the country.

Today, the monarch was given a tour of the Gold Museum in Bogotá, and later walked through the city’s main square, Plaza de Bolívar, with Mayor Carlos Fernando Galán.

The official visit runs through November 26, and the queen’s agenda includes learning about the country’s post-earthquake reconstruction efforts (three months after a 7.4-magnitude earthquake struck Colombia) and ongoing sustainability projects.

To arrive in the country, Mary went sharp in a black Forte Forte single-breasted blazer with a frilly sky-blue Stenströms Darya blouse, which she paired with black trousers, Malone Souliers Marion 70 pumps, and black Gucci glasses, plus some gold jewelry.

Then, for this afternoon’s engagements, she slipped into a sophisticated business look, the highlight of which was a black-and-white tweed pencil skirt from Dolce & Gabbana and a black collared top from Red Valentino. To round out the ensemble, she reached for sheer black tights, plus matching stilettos and a leather handbag.

The royal performed another quick change for her evening engagement: a reception in the Biodiversario greenhouse at Bogotá’s Botanical Garden. The look was a breezy wine midi dress with a fun dotted print by Saloni. In semi-sheer fabric, it featured a high neck, long sleeves, a cinched waist, and a long skirt that reached her ankles. Black-and-white heels added the perfect contrast to the colorful maxi.

#6801. Microsoft Expands AI Security Platform to Address Agentic AI Security Risks

Microsoft is expanding the scope of its AI-powered Project Perception security platform to address the growing concern that AI agents may have access to sensitive information and systems, yet don't always behave as expected.

In a company blog post, Hayete Gallot, executive vice president of Microsoft Security, outlined how Microsoft plans to use Project Perception to monitor, manage, and contain what it calls "agentic insiders." These are AI agents deployed within an environment that can access data, use software tools, and make decisions without constant human supervision. While they can improve productivity, Microsoft warns that they also introduce security risks, even when they haven't been compromised by an attacker.

"Agents are unpredictable," Gallot wrote. "They will adapt as the context changes, may not behave as intended, and will collaborate and collude with other agents to reach their objectives, without needing to be compromised."

Project Perception was first introduced in July. The platform, which entered public preview Aug. 3, is designed to help security teams respond to increasingly sophisticated cyber threats using AI-powered automation.

Instead of leaning only on traditional security alerts and manual investigations, Project Perception uses three kinds of specialized AI agents working together. Red team agents hunt for possible vulnerabilities, blue team agents look into suspicious activity, and green team agents step in to fix problems and strengthen defenses.

To spot threats and respond faster, the system pulls in security data from identities, endpoints, cloud environments, applications, and other sources.

Now Microsoft wants to use those same capabilities on the AI agents already running inside enterprise networks. Project Perception would find those agents, give them identities and permissions, and keep an eye on what they do across different systems. It would also keep assessing the risks that come with their actions.

Microsoft also described a plan for "Guardian Agents," specialized AI agents that supervise other agents and step in when their behavior gets risky. That might mean limiting an agent's permissions or blocking certain actions before they cause harm.

"Monitoring and securing those agents must become continuous," Gallot wrote.

The initiative also reflects concerns raised by Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella in an Oct. 10 essay on AI security.

Nadella argued that enterprises should treat powerful AI models much like potential insider security risks, particularly when those models can access confidential information or perform critical business operations.

"We simply can't outsource responsibility for what intelligence does on our behalf," Nadella wrote.

He called for security controls that operate independently of AI models, including restricted permissions, detailed activity logging, and mechanisms that allow administrators to stop an agent's actions.

"In other words, we need to separate the supply of intelligence from the authority over it," Nadella wrote.

Microsoft admits that watching what AI agents do is only part of the answer. Gallot said that future security tools will also need to show why AI models make the choices they do, not just what actions they end up taking.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

In an active social engineering campaign, cyber attackers are impersonating IT help desks and using fake passkey setup requests to compromise employee identities and gain access to enterprise cloud data.

Microsoft is expanding the scope of its AI-powered Project Perception security platform to address the growing concern that AI agents may have access to sensitive information and systems, yet don't always behave as expected.

Ransomware attacks are becoming more automated, and AI could take that much further, according to cybersecurity company NCC Group's July 2026 threat intelligence report.

#6795. Survey: Organizations Report Positive ROI on AI Investments, Yet Data Readiness and Oversight Lag Behind

According to a survey by data and AI services provider Further, enterprises are reporting benefits from artificial intelligence even as their data preparation and oversight processes remain incomplete.

The company's recent State of Enterprise AI Maturity research surveyed 515 U.S.-based AI decision-makers. Just 5% believed their organizations' data was largely ready for AI.

The findings suggest that gaining value from individual AI applications and preparing to operate them more broadly are separate challenges. Reported returns alone do not establish that an organization has the foundations to sustain or expand those results.

Further found that 37% of respondents' organizations had paused, scaled back, or abandoned an underperforming AI initiative in the previous 12 months. Among that group, data quality or availability was the most frequently cited main reason, at 24%.

Meanwhile, 29% reported completing a formal data audit and remediation plan. The research also placed 69% of organizations in the exploring, experimenting, or implementing stages of adoption.

Further distinguishes data prepared for conventional reporting from data prepared for AI. Its explanation emphasizes data origins and documentation, along with access controls that support ongoing evaluation and governance.

For readers assessing their own readiness, the practical issue is whether data supports the requirements of a particular application. A collection suitable for a dashboard may need additional preparation before it can reliably support an AI system.

Seventy-seven percent reported a positive return on AI investment. However, only 3% said their organizations had a documented AI-audit process.

The findings measure different things: perceived financial benefit and the existence of a repeatable oversight process. Neither substitutes for the other.

"In reality, confidence without a documented process is exposure," said Kierra Dotson, associate director of AI strategy and governance at Further. "Closing that gap doesn't take new technology; it requires an auditable governance process."

For the full report, visit the Further site (registration required).

#6791. How to Read ‘The Hunger Games’ Books in Order of Release

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

In the mood to read some Hunger Games books after seeing Sunrise on the Reaping in theaters? Maybe you never visited Panem on the page in the first place. Here’s your guide to how to read Suzanne Collins’s dystopian young adult book series in order. (Remember, even though there are six movies, there are only five books.)

Collins’s trilogy was published in a timely succession back in the day. Then a decade passed, and all of a sudden the author surprised us with a new installment. Are there more Hunger Games books on the horizon? We certainly hope so. But for now, let’s be grateful for what we have.

In the world of young adult fiction, “what if you had to fight to the death with the boy in your class who has a crush on you” is about as perfect as premises get. The emotional stakes are as high as the external stakes. Katniss Everdeen was a survivalist before she volunteered to replace her younger sister in her dystopian society’s televised child slaughter. She could hunt and forage better than most of the people in her Appalachian hometown. What she didn’t know how to do was smile for the cameras and perform for the crowd if that’s what it takes to survive.

Katniss returns to District 12 in a PR relationship with her fellow tribute, victor, and ally Peeta Mellark. They’re miserable on the victory tour, unable to process their feelings and seeing the consequences of their actions everywhere. Then, a year later, President Snow announces that the third Quarter Quell will be Hunger Games: All Stars. The tributes are all former winners and adults. Are Katniss and Peeta simply back where they started? Not really. This time they have allies, and plans to finally move against the Capitol.

In the final book of the original trilogy, Katniss is being dressed up and manipulated for the cameras yet again. But it’s not the Capitol doing it this time. It’s the rebellion, led by the secret District 13. It’s scary to think about how even if you believe in the cause, you may find yourself using the same methods as your enemy. War has broken out in Panem, and Katniss and Peeta are separated. So much happens in this book that they had to split it into two movies.

Ten years later, Collins returned with a fascinating subject for a new prequel: the 10th Hunger Games, during which a teenaged Coriolanus Snow mentored District 12’s lost victor, Lucy Gray Baird, and fell in love with her while brainstorming how to get the murder reality show better ratings with his classmates. Their romance is not really one that you can root for, because we know we can’t fix him. He had bad vibes all along. But it’s a fascinating, twisted tale of how the Hunger Games as we know it came to be.

Next, of course, Collins answered fandom prayers by writing a Haymitch Abernathy prequel. We now know the story of every single District 12 victor’s games. Background details link District 12 women like Lucy Gray and Maysilee Donner to Katniss Everdeen in fun ways. It also serves as a nice companion to Catching Fire, with many appearances from the victors Katniss and Peeta meet during their own Quarter Quell. But be careful what you wish for, because this is a brutal and bloody book about the many ways the Capitol can edit and control your story using propaganda.

The party line is that Suzanne Collins writes when she is inspired to write, and not a second before. As of the release of the film adaptation of Sunrise on the Reaping, there has not been a new book announcement. But that hasn’t stopped the theories.

In the fandom, the character who probably has the most support in this realm is Finnick Odair. He’s sexy, confident, and has a tragically beautiful love story with Annie Cresta, a fellow victor from his district. They were also Career tributes. The districts that chose to buy into the Hunger Games by training children to kill haven’t been explored in depth in the books. There’s definitely potential there for a socially relevant story. What is that process like from the inside? Do they have stage moms? I am all for this, but just have one note: let’s tell it from Annie’s POV.

But there are so many other avenues that a future Hunger Games book could take. Collins could write about Cinna and the world of the stylists. That could include an update on President Snow’s cousin Tigris. She could leave the games behind and write a book about District 13. (Alma Coin origin story, divas?) What about the unknown, presumably dead victor of the first Quarter Quell? There’s also the Dark Days and the first rebel uprising in Panem. I wouldn’t mind a Plutarch story, as well...but I really think we need to hear from a female character this time.

#6788. Researchers Find AI Is Accelerating Vulnerability Discovery

AI-powered vulnerability research is contributing to a major increase in security updates, raising concerns that IT teams could fall behind as patching demands grow, according to cybersecurity company ThreatDown.

In a recent blog post, the company stated that in September 2026, vendors addressed 998 common vulnerabilities and exposures (CVEs) around Patch Tuesday, compared with 96 during the same period last year. In addition, the three-month rolling average of patched Linux kernel vulnerabilities tripled year over year, while Google's Chrome browser experienced a 36-fold increase.

The researchers point to advanced AI models, including Anthropic's Claude Mythos Preview, as a major factor behind the increase in vulnerability discovery.

Introduced in April 2026, Mythos Preview is being used through Project Glasswing, an initiative involving Microsoft, the Linux Foundation and other organizations to identify vulnerabilities in critical software.

While AI could ultimately make software more secure, the cybersecurity company warns that vulnerability discovery is accelerating faster than organizations can deploy fixes.

IT teams must still evaluate patches, test compatibility and schedule updates without disrupting critical business operations.

The challenge is particularly significant for organizations already struggling with patch management.

The cybersecurity vendor cites Verizon's 2026 Data Breach Investigations Report, which found that 35% of known actively exploited vulnerabilities remained unpatched after 28 days in 2025.

Ryan Powell, vice president of IT and security at ThreatDown, said automated patching systems can help organizations manage the growing workload. However, additional patches still require more testing and validation, increasing operational overhead and the risks associated with frequent system changes.

The report also highlights the risks of vulnerabilities in widely used software components such as OpenSSL, embedded in applications, network devices, and security products.

Unlike standalone applications, these components often depend on downstream vendors to release updates before organizations can deploy fixes.

The security firm's analysis points to CVE-2022-0778, an OpenSSL vulnerability patched in March 2022, as an example. Some affected products did not receive updates until several months later.

As AI accelerates vulnerability discovery, these dependencies could delay remediation and extend organizations' exposure to known security flaws.

ThreatDown's findings illustrate a growing challenge for enterprise cybersecurity: AI is making vulnerabilities easier to discover but fixing them remains a complex and time-consuming process.

For the full report, visit the ThreatDown blog here.

In an active social engineering campaign, cyber attackers are impersonating IT help desks and using fake passkey setup requests to compromise employee identities and gain access to enterprise cloud data.

Microsoft is expanding the scope of its AI-powered Project Perception security platform to address the growing concern that AI agents may have access to sensitive information and systems, yet don't always behave as expected.

Ransomware attacks are becoming more automated, and AI could take that much further, according to cybersecurity company NCC Group's July 2026 threat intelligence report.

Avantis Education, the creator of ClassVR, has introduced two new immersive learning libraries for its Eduverse+ platform: EscapeRooms, a math suite for K-8 students, and LingoLab, a Spanish and English language learning suite for students in grades 6-12.

#6782. Ransomware Attacks Move Beyond Enterprise Systems to Theaten Employees' Families

Attackers are exploiting enterprises' own artificial intelligence systems and extending pressure beyond internal networks to employees and their families, according to a new Semperis survey of organizations that suffered disruptive ransomware attacks.

In a recent announcement, the company highlighted findings from its 2026 Ransomware Risk Report, which examines AI-related attack tactics, personal intimidation, and recovery preparedness. Censuswide surveyed 1,200 information technology and security professionals across nine countries and eight industry sectors in the first half of 2026.

The sample has an important limit: This year's study included only organizations where at least one ransomware attack caused business disruption. The results describe those victims' experiences, rather than the likelihood that an organization will suffer an attack. The report says that sampling approach changed from previous studies.

Semperis sells identity security, recovery and crisis management products.

The report says 84% of respondents experienced attacks that exploited AI in some way. Its breakdown distinguishes AI-based attacks, reported by 40%, from attacks that manipulated victims' own AI agents or systems, reported by 44%. That distinction matters: The broader figure covers both attackers using AI and attackers exploiting AI deployed inside an organization.

Defensive use of AI also produced mixed responses. Nearly half of respondents, 49%, said they successfully used AI for detection or response, while 23% said their AI tools failed to detect or respond successfully. Those are survey responses about victimized organizations, rather than a controlled comparison of security products.

Chris Inglis, former U.S. national cyber director and a Semperis strategic adviser, described the difficulty of recognizing AI's involvement. "Many attacks are not perceived as being aided and abetted by AI because they're just too slick, too sophisticated," he said in the report.

Data erasure, destruction, or release remained the most frequently reported threat, at 56%. Blackmail, extortion or public shaming followed at 41%, with system lockouts or operational sabotage at 40%. The report also recorded psychological intimidation at 23% and physical threats at 17%.

According to a survey by data and AI services provider Further, enterprises are reporting benefits from artificial intelligence even as their data preparation and oversight processes remain incomplete.

Our nation's largest early childhood program has not fully caught up with what science now tells us about how children learn.

A recent Cloud Security Alliance report urges organizations to inventory, govern, and continuously monitor machine and agent identities.

Recent research from Thales highlights how AI adoption and emerging quantum computing threats are putting pressure on existing security measures.

#6781. All the Details on ‘Phony’ Season 2 for Everyone Obsessed

It’s been a moment since a show’s had me more locked in than Phony on Hulu, and with just one more episode left, inquiring minds (me) want to know: WHEN IS SEASON 2? And also important: is there even going to be a season 2?

Phony follows high schooler Sonny Cooke, who wakes up in the hospital after a car accident to find that his mom seems...off. For starters, her facial features are ever-so-slightly different—but there’s also the fact that she doesn’t remember their special way of saying “I love you” (three hand squeezes), and the fact that she’s just acting generally weird. Was she swapped out by an imposter? Does Sonny have Capgras syndrome? Is there some sort of alternate reality happening? Hopefully we’ll find out by the end of season 1, but then what? Which brings us to...

Not yet. But given how freely the cast and creators are talking about a second season (and the fact that the show is pretty popular), it seems likely to get a sophomore pickup.

Yes...but it’ll also end on a cliffhanger. Creator Nick Paley told Us Weekly “I’m really excited about the cathartic emotionality of the [season 1] cliffhanger. This thing kicks us into the next story in this world because I don’t like constantly depriving people of information. I want this to feel like it means a lot and it feels very important to the characters emotionally. So that cliffhanger does both. Every episode has very big turns at the end. When you’re telling a mystery, it’s really important to show it to people who don’t know.”

Paley told the Los Angeles Times that Phony Season 2 will feature “a whole new story” after the season 1 finale delivers “resolutions and answers.”

The majority of Phony Season 1 was filmed from March through May of 2026, and it premiered in October—a pretty short production time. So, if the show follows a similar schedule for season 2 and starts filming this spring, we could be looking at another October premiere.

Potentially a LOT. Connie Britton, who plays Ellen, told The Wrap, "I think Nick has this thing laid out through probably five or six seasons. He's so clear about what the story is, and so I would be very surprised if he has doesn't have Season 2 all ready to go."

#6779. Every Pair of Levi’s Is on Sale for Black Friday, Starting at 40% Off

“I prefer my jeans to hug up top, drape down low. As a cheap pair of jeans go, it's the best way to go.”

#6777. What Exactly Is an Aura Gap Relationship?

It’s all about matching and complementing energies, and it’s one of my non-negotiable.

When I first heard about the social media concepts of age, height, and swag gaps, I was confused as to how they could possibly threaten established relationships, or generally anyone who was into someone else. After all, these are generally known factors.

When you meet a new love interest, there will always be characteristics that reveal themselves over time—that's what dating is. Whether you meet someone in person or online, you generally know what you're getting into. Sure, you can lie on Hinge, but one IRL date should usually sets the facts straight.

Unless you're Pedro Pascal in Materialists or Demi Moore in The Substance, there's not much you can do about age and height gaps. Swag gaps, on the other hand, are a little more amenable. You can become a guy's stylist, educate him on the lingo, spots, and social niceties needed to play on the same aesthetically-pleasing field. It takes brute force, hard work, and someone willing to give you carte blanche with their time and dime, but it is possible to close that gap.

What is far more dangerous, explosive, and slow-killing is the aura gap. But what exactly is aura? It’s basically the vibe you give off, from your confidence, to your demeanor, to if you’re warm and personable or cold and short. It’s something you’re born with, not something you can buy. Sure, could you learn social skills and etiquette, of course. You could even pay someone to make you seem like you have good aura online, but the truth will eventually present itself in person. You can manufacture an image, but you can't concoct the energy you bring into a room.

When we consider aura-gap relationships and their longevity, I suspect the issue is less about understanding someone's faults and more fundamental to a partnership's long-term compatibility.

The best long-term example I can think of is my parents. My mom is larger-than-life, extroverted, crass, and will leave most people in ab-clenching laughter. My dad is brilliant, detail-oriented, and gets anything and everything done. While those attributes complement each other wonderfully, it's the indescribable warmth and ferocity the duo exude together that makes them such a good team. Their personalities are different, but their auras are entirely compatible.

And I think that's where the distinction lies. An aura gap isn't necessarily about one person being louder, funnier, or more charismatic than the other. It's about whether the energy you bring into a relationship is reciprocated, complemented, or quietly diminished.

Bad habits, political and religious differences, and financial compatibility are all big factors on marriage checklists, but aura, to me, is even bigger. You can agree on where to live, how many children to have, and what constitutes an appropriate amount of money to spend on dinner, but what good is all that if the person sitting across from you makes you feel like a less interesting version of yourself?

As we date, the question should be: does the person opposite me radiate a consistent energy that not only complements my own, but makes mine better? Aura is my non-negotiable, and frankly, it should be yours, too. I'd rather be with someone who makes me feel more myself than someone who makes me feel like I have to be enough for the both of us.

#6776. IDC Report Finds Significant Gap Between AI Spending and Measurable Financial Returns

Nearly 70% of organizations report a significant gap between artificial intelligence spending and measurable financial returns, according to a recent survey by IDC.

The research firm's 2026 Worldwide AI Economics and Cost of Ownership Survey found that 69% reported such a gap, while only 15% had achieved an AI return on investment exceeding 100%.

In a separate May 2026 survey, 73% of respondents identified excessive AI costs as a significant risk.

IDC highlighted the findings in an October 20 analysis examining what enterprise customers expect from AI technology vendors.

Enterprise customers are placing greater emphasis on measurable business outcomes when evaluating AI products, according to IDC.

The firm identified three priorities for vendors: demonstrating financial value, addressing governance and security requirements, and building support beyond IT departments.

The analysis also highlighted the gap between initial agentic AI deployments and broader adoption. Although 80% of organizations have started deploying agentic AI, only 17% are using it across the enterprise.

IDC said evaluating AI investments can be difficult when the technology changes how employees work and how business processes operate.

The firm expects AI infrastructure capacity to catch up with demand around 2029, potentially easing costs. Until then, vendors face pressure to demonstrate the long-term financial value of their products.

AI funding decisions involve a broad range of executives. IDC's September 2026 C-Suite Tech Survey found that CIOs and CTOs were involved in 63% of those decisions, business-function executives in 62%, and chief financial officers in 61%.

A separate IDC survey found that 42% of CEOs planned to hire a chief AI officer within the next 12 months.

Buyers also reported concerns about the products and terms vendors offer. Limited integration with existing systems, insufficient model and data transparency, and concerns about vendor lock-in were each cited by 34% of respondents.

Overpromising and underdelivering, along with unclear pricing models, were each cited by 31%.

IDC said vendors need to substantiate financial returns and address security and governance requirements while working with decision-makers across IT, finance, and other business departments.

For more information, go to the IDC blog.

In an active social engineering campaign, cyber attackers are impersonating IT help desks and using fake passkey setup requests to compromise employee identities and gain access to enterprise cloud data.

Microsoft is expanding the scope of its AI-powered Project Perception security platform to address the growing concern that AI agents may have access to sensitive information and systems, yet don't always behave as expected.

Ransomware attacks are becoming more automated, and AI could take that much further, according to cybersecurity company NCC Group's July 2026 threat intelligence report.

Avantis Education, the creator of ClassVR, has introduced two new immersive learning libraries for its Eduverse+ platform: EscapeRooms, a math suite for K-8 students, and LingoLab, a Spanish and English language learning suite for students in grades 6-12.

#6775. The 7 Lessons I Learned About Skin After Losing 85 Pounds

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Plagued by the prospect of excess and loose skin, one writer investigates the surgical and noninvasive options that lie ahead

I lost 85 pounds without bariatric surgery. Weight loss has been on my mind basically my entire life, especially since I started this journey in earnest in July 2019. And, as proud of myself as I most certainly am for losing this weight and finally getting healthier, it is met toe-to-toe with pervasive anxiety and a nagging question: What about excess skin?

Because I’m losing weight more slowly, I haven’t yet encountered loose skin, though I still have at least 50 more pounds to go. I worry, perhaps too much, about whether I’ll make it to my goal weight without needing skin tightening at the end of this journey.

It’s a pretty unsexy topic—which is why it’s rarely, if ever, discussed. But for those like me who are on major weight-loss journeys, or whose bodies have changed significantly through pregnancy, it’s a question that plagues us: Am I going to encounter excess skin the more weight I lose? And if I do, what can I do about it?

Apparently, I’m not the only one with questions, according to New York City plastic surgeon Lara Devgan. “Skin tightening remains one of the elusive black boxes in the world of plastic surgery,” she says. That said, we’re all still learning about best practices—from me to medical professionals.

So I decided to dig deeper. I rounded up five renowned dermatologists and plastic surgeons from across the country and asked them my questions. And here’s what they have to say.

“Losing weight gradually rather than rapidly may prevent excess skin after major weight loss,” says Los Angeles–based dermatologist Harold Lancer. “However, rapid large-scale weight loss will invariably result in excess skin.” Devgan seconds that. “The best way to prevent skin laxity after weight loss is to do your best to lose weight at a slow and steady pace,” she says. “Although it may sound appealing to get to the finish line quickly, that is actually the worst thing you can do in terms of skin laxity.” Losing weight slowly, she says, allows the skin to contract and shrink as the body shape changes.

Though I personally haven’t opted for weight loss surgery, many do—and there is zero shame in that. But because weight is lost more rapidly after surgery, the likelihood of excess skin increases, so experts recommend pairing weight loss surgery with a skin removal procedure. Why worry about excess skin, especially after faster weight loss? It’s “because collagen and elastin fibers are destroyed when skin is stretched significantly and remains stretched for an extended amount of time,” says New York City plastic surgeon David Shafer. “Patients should plan on staged procedures if having a weight loss surgery followed by a skin removal surgery.”

“Typically, younger patients with thicker and more sebaceous skin will have more skin contraction during weight loss due to better elasticity,” Devgan says. “Starting in the 20s, tissue elasticity begins to decline. Even in the absence of weight fluctuation, loss of tissue turgor and decreased firmness can be seen in midface descent in the late 20s, breast laxity in the 30s, abdominal laxity in the 40s, and extremity laxity in the 50s.”

However, Devgan says everyone’s body is different. “There is a tremendous amount of variability in the human experience, so some patients may lose 50 pounds with minimal change in their body, while others can lose as few as 10 and see tissue laxity.” If you are genetically blessed with unusually superb tissue elasticity, Devgan says, it can be less dramatic. (And lucky you.)

Lifestyle choices can also factor in, Boston-based dermatologist Ranella Hirsch says. How long were you at the weight you started losing from? Are you a smoker? Do you spend a lot of time in the sun without protection? All these factors play into skin elasticity.

“Using an emollient moisturizer rich in vitamin E will help improve the barrier protection of the skin and lock in moisture,” Devgan says. She also says the topical medical-grade ingredients you use on your face for firmness and tightness—like hyaluronic acid, peptides, niacin, squalene, vitamins C and B, retinol, and bakuchiol—can work elsewhere, too. “Most people focus on using these for the face and neck in their daily routines, but from a molecular perspective, their efficacy is present anywhere on the body.” But save your money on creams that promise you the world. “Losing large amounts of weight rapidly tends to leave skin behind,” Hirsch says. “Though creams with incredible claims abound, they actually do little.”

Nécessaire The Body Lotion with Niacinamide

Biossance Squalane + Caffeine Toning Body Cream

Sol de Janeiro Bom Dia Bright Body Cream with Vitamin C

U Beauty The SCULPT Arm Compound Body Serum

Building muscle through weight training might help reduce the appearance of lax skin, New York City dermatologist Dendy Engelman says. “This helps reduce the appearance by replacing the lost fat with muscle mass.”

Nonsurgical options can help with loose skin after weight loss, but they won’t help with skin folds or hanging skin. “Noninvasive treatments such as laser and radiofrequency can help tighten the skin, but there is a limit to how much they can do,” Shafer adds. “Hanging skin generally needs to be surgically excised with procedures such as abdominoplasty, body lift, and arm lift.” Shafer says there’s a difference between loose skin and hanging skin. “It’s possible patients can manage and treat excess skin cases without undergoing skin removal surgery, such as if the skin is loose but not hanging.” He adds, “In that case, noninvasive treatments may be useful, but hanging skin most often requires surgical excision.”

“After major weight loss, people may have skin that hangs off their body,” Engelman says. “These heavy folds can cause health problems such as pain, inflammation, and frequent infections from the skin constantly rubbing together. This can happen during exercise or everyday activities. When a person is having functional issues because of their excess skin, it should be a sign that this problem needs to be addressed.” And, of course, everyone’s journey is different.

Lancer says some individuals will tolerate moderate laxity and be happy with the weight-loss accomplishment; others will seek skin tightening for as little as a five-pound weight loss. But, he says, if plastic surgery does become the option on the table, “I would recommend getting at least three opinions from doctors who exclusively do excess body skin reduction. Once you select a doctor, do one small area on your body, so you can evaluate the procedure, how your body heals, and your results. Afterward, you can determine whether you’d like to proceed with a full-blown larger procedure.”

He suggests consulting a dermatologist for nonsurgical options, then asking that doctor to recommend plastic surgeons who specialize in excess body skin reduction if you feel that is the right path for you. This is a major decision and shouldn’t be taken lightly. Bring medical professionals along for the entire weight-loss journey (even if you’ve already started) and ask all the questions: Which specific procedure is best? How much will it cost? Will insurance cover it? (From my research so far, no, if it is cosmetic, but perhaps yes if it would help correct something that limits your daily functionality.) No question is off-limits, and don’t try to make these decisions alone.

If this piece applies to you at all, whether it’s longstanding weight you carried, weight you lost during pregnancy, or somewhere in between, that means one thing: You’ve taken major steps toward better physical health, and that should be applauded. “Everyone’s weight loss story is different, and some days are harder than others,” Engelman says. “I say, try to be kind to yourself. You’ve just reached a huge accomplishment and should be proud of that.”

Devgan agrees. “Accomplishing a personal health goal like major weight loss is an incredible achievement that anyone should be proud of,” she says. “While excess skin can feel like a burden or source of self-consciousness, it is also a reminder of the metaphorical and literal shedding of the weight of a prior version of oneself. You cannot have a couture gown without a seam, and you cannot have a surgery without a scar. Life has limitations, but that does not make it less beautiful. It makes it more real.”

#6772. Report: Global Ransomware Attacks Rise 23% in Q3

Global ransomware activity increased 23% in the third quarter of 2026, with organizations in North America and Europe accounting for the largest share of attacks, according to cybersecurity firm NCC Group.

The company's Cyber Threat Intelligence Report Review of Q3 2026 recorded 2,865 ransomware victim claims, up from 2,325 in the previous quarter.

Despite the quarterly increase, attacks declined 26% month over month in September to 821. North America recorded the most attacks that month with 390, followed by Europe with 211.

The industrial sector accounted for 29% of quarterly attacks, while Qilin remained the most active ransomware group for the sixth consecutive quarter, recording 365 victims. The Gentlemen followed with 356, and INC Ransom ranked third with 109.

The report highlighted a July cyber attack against a small UK power generation facility that forced operations to stop for approximately four days.

Although the incident did not affect the national electricity grid, it demonstrated how cyber attacks can disrupt physical infrastructure.

The security researchers said the incident was consistent with techniques previously associated with Iranian-affiliated campaigns, including attacks against internet-exposed industrial controllers.

However, the UK government has not formally attributed the attack, and the techniques used remain unconfirmed.

The report also identified growing security challenges as organizations connect traditional IT networks with operational technology (OT) systems for remote maintenance and centralized management.

The firm recommended network segmentation, stronger privileged-access controls, and continuous monitoring of OT environments.

The research found that more than 40 vulnerabilities affecting enterprise applications, VPN appliances, e-mail gateways, and management platforms were added to CISA's Known Exploited Vulnerabilities Catalog during the quarter.

The report examined Gunra, a ransomware-as-a-service operation that has targeted 53 victims across multiple industries as of Q3.

According to the cybersecurity company, Gunra exploited vulnerabilities CVE-2024-55591 and CVE-2025-24472 in internet-facing Fortinet devices to gain access to corporate networks.

The group also used stolen credentials, session hijacking, and administrative tools to move between systems before deploying ransomware.

Other emerging ransomware groups included Deadlock and Storm, with the latter claiming 78 victims by the end of September.

Researchers also observed initial access brokers advertising compromised VPN, Remote Desktop Protocol, and SSH access, alongside access to cloud identities, source code repositories, and security management platforms.

The quarterly review also highlighted BraZetsu, an AI-enhanced Windows malware framework that evolved into a specialized initial access toolkit.

According to the report, BraZetsu uses AI models to automate victim profiling, analyze compromised environments, and identify valuable corporate systems.

The framework searches for enterprise software, financial data, digital certificates, and cloud credentials, helping operators identify compromised access that can be sold to other cyber criminals.

The cybersecurity firm also identified risks involving legitimate AI agents, including prompt injection, excessive permissions, and unintended access to external systems.

The report cited incidents involving U.S. and Australian government systems as examples of agents accessing resources beyond their intended scope.

Researchers warned that these incidents do not necessarily involve malicious intent but can create consequences resembling conventional cyber intrusions.

For the full report, visit the NCC Group site.

In an active social engineering campaign, cyber attackers are impersonating IT help desks and using fake passkey setup requests to compromise employee identities and gain access to enterprise cloud data.

Microsoft is expanding the scope of its AI-powered Project Perception security platform to address the growing concern that AI agents may have access to sensitive information and systems, yet don't always behave as expected.

Ransomware attacks are becoming more automated, and AI could take that much further, according to cybersecurity company NCC Group's July 2026 threat intelligence report.

Avantis Education, the creator of ClassVR, has introduced two new immersive learning libraries for its Eduverse+ platform: EscapeRooms, a math suite for K-8 students, and LingoLab, a Spanish and English language learning suite for students in grades 6-12.

#6769. Jennifer Lopez’s “Spiced Apple Cider” Manicure Is the Coziest Winter Nail Trend

The perfect manicure for minimalists this season

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

There's something so comforting about a rich autumnal manicure that embraces the colors that make cozy season so magical.

Deep, earthy tones and foliage-inspired hues come into their own in the colder months, and no one knows this better than Jennifer Lopez, who appeared on The Drew Barrymore Show yesterday with her fingertips wrapped in a warm, cozy shade of “spice apple cider,” according to her nail artist, Tom Bachik.

Lopez’s manicure, a sophisticated smorgasbord of brown, burnt orange, and burgundy, is made to go with pillow-soft cashmere knits, but as she showed this week, it can also be dressed up for party season.

For her appearance on Barrymore’s show, the star looked typically stylish in a form-fitting black dress complete with buttoned detailing and a faux fur collar. Lopez added height in pointed black slingbacks, but her terra-cotta manicure tied the look together and looked intentional against the dress’s jet-black hue.

For anyone looking to up the ante slightly this party season, warm, orange-tinged manicures like Lopez’s are the perfect base for a metallic cat-eye finish (a technique that uses swipes of glossy magnetic polish with minuscule metal particles).

As Daisy Kalnina, founder of The Gel Bottle, previously told Bazaar, cat-eye finishes applied to a burnt orange shade “catch the light in such a striking way.” Kalnina added: “It’s daring but incredibly flattering. It’s the kind of shade that instantly elevates a beige knit or camel coat.”

#6767. Microsoft Expands Protections and Controls for Sovereign Cloud

Microsoft is introducing new tools within its sovereign cloud offerings designed to give organizations tighter control over where their data resides, who can access it, and how critical systems operate, especially in light of AI becoming a bigger part of enterprise infrastructure.

In a recent announcement, Douglas Phillips, president and chief technology officer of Microsoft Specialized Clouds, outlined updates spanning public cloud, private cloud, and partner environments. The changes target governments, regulated industries, and organizations that need tighter control over sensitive workloads without abandoning cloud-based AI capabilities.

"Digital sovereignty should give organizations greater control and security over the technology they depend on, while continuing to innovate," Phillips wrote.

One of the biggest additions is Microsoft's Sovereign Control Panel, which will enter public preview at Microsoft Ignite. Available through the Azure portal, the tool will give IT administrators one place to manage data sovereignty settings, spot compliance issues, and identify potential risks that could disrupt operations.

Microsoft is also tightening controls over who can access sensitive systems. Its Data Guardian feature will require oversight by personnel based in Europe when Microsoft engineers access certain production systems in the European Union and European Free Trade Association. Another tool, Production Touch Ledger, will keep tamper-resistant records of that access, giving organizations a clearer picture of who accessed their systems and what they did.

Microsoft is also giving customers more control over encryption. External key management with Azure Managed HSM is now generally available, letting organizations keep their main encryption keys on hardware they own or manage outside Microsoft's cloud.

The company is also expanding its Sovereign Private Cloud offerings, giving organizations more options to run systems with or without an internet connection. New capabilities include GitHub Enterprise Local, GitHub Copilot with locally hosted AI models, and additional data services running on Azure Local.

The push for greater operational control comes as Microsoft is also reconsidering how enterprises should secure increasingly autonomous AI systems.

As reported by Redmond magazine, Microsoft is expanding Project Perception to address risks posed by what the company calls "agentic insiders," AI agents capable of accessing sensitive information and performing actions without constant human supervision.

That initiative introduces plans for Guardian Agents, specialized security agents designed to monitor other AI systems, evaluate their behavior and intervene when their actions become risky.

"Monitoring and securing those agents must become continuous," said Hayete Gallot, executive vice president of Microsoft Security.

Project Perception is about limiting what AI agents can do. Microsoft's sovereign cloud expansion tackles a different set of questions: where those systems run, who gets to administer them, and how the data stays protected.

Taken together, the two efforts point to a shift in Microsoft's enterprise AI strategy. The company is putting more weight on oversight, not just automation.

Phillips said AI is making these questions more pressing as organizations roll out intelligent systems across sensitive business processes and critical infrastructure.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

#6765. The Berkshires Are Made for a Soft-Life Getaway

Two hours from New York City, this corner of Massachusetts pairs farm-fresh finds and open-air beauty with restorative retreats

Every Saturday from May to November, Great Barrington has a popular farmers’ market. Lines of local Berkshirites, visitors, little kids, and friendly dogs on leashes gather under packed canopies to buy a vibrant mix of fresh fruits, vegetables, flowers, and more. An older woman overheard me talking to my husband—in town for our end-of-summer vacation—about honeycrisps and insisted we go to the opposite end of the market to Maynard Farms’s booth. The family-owned orchard carries a diverse range of apples and “amazing stone fruits” she swore our excitable toddlers would love. She was right—the boys devoured nearly all of the produce before we left the market.

Last August, in the final warm days of summer, we drove winding highways through Hudson River mountains to the westernmost area of Massachusetts: a lush and culturally rich destination composed of 30 towns and two cities, collectively known as the Berkshires and located just two hours outside of New York City.

Lenox, the town we stayed in, is a quaint enclave that boasts multimillion-dollar Cape Cods, luxury day resorts, golfing, country clubs, and farm-to-table restaurants. It’s an ideal place for a weekend getaway with friends, a romantic escape, or a family trip with your littles to touch well-landscaped grass.

We stayed in a newly built rental home, directly across the street from the Boston Symphony Orchestra at Tanglewood. In the evenings, if you’re within three miles of the parking lot, you can hear concerts from world-renowned artists. “Whether you're sitting in the front row enjoying James Taylor in the Koussevitzky Music Shed or sharing a candlelit picnic under the stars on the lawn, there is something magical about being here,” says Amy Aldrich, senior director of patron experience at Tanglewood. “We take great pride in creating a welcoming atmosphere where world-class music and the beauty of nature come together to create something truly special.”

Well-to-do families have long escaped the hustle of metropolitan New England cities to decompress in the Berkshires because of its natural beauty. Author Edith Wharton, who chronicled the sordid lives of Gilded Age elites, built her 16,000-square-foot mansion and expansive garden estate in Lenox in 1902.

Back in the day, when affluent housewives needed a respite, they’d go to the “spa.” I never fully understood the older meaning of the term until I visited Miraval Berkshires, a 380-acre resort that boasts ancient Eastern treatments, healing equine therapy sessions, meditation classes, macronutrient dining, sound baths, astrological readings, and much more. It’s an adults-only, device-free space, so disconnecting from stress is mandatory.

Equestrian activities were a common theme for us during the trip. HomeFarm at Undermountain, run by executive director Lori Pestana, is a gem of a property. The animal sanctuary is dedicated to caring for and maintaining the health of horses—but also houses goats, sheep, and donkeys. “It’s a place where life slows down, where people reconnect with nature, with animals, and often with themselves,” Pestana says. “Whether visitors come to ride, explore, learn, or simply experience the peaceful rhythm of farm life, they leave with something they didn’t necessarily know they were looking for—a sense of belonging, joy, and connection.”

Pestana’s small but dedicated staff offers riding lessons and a holistic view of what restoration can look like with a lot of care.

For dining, expect most restaurants to serve farm-to-table dishes that are sourced from the seasonal produce and livestock being raised in the area. You couldn’t possibly get a closer farm-to-table experience than Highlawn Farms, which has delicious cheeses, ice cream, and milk made from the same cows grazing its property. It’s a general rule of thumb in my family that we never pass up the opportunity to have a wood-fired pizza—Pizzeria Boema has a chic Scandinavian interior and New England quaint courtyard, serving the crispy, flavor-filled pies made with locally sourced mozzarella. And if you’re more interested in a fine-dining experience, Cello, run by couple Ryan and Kim Boya, takes a contemporary approach to comfort food. The 29-seat intimate restaurant has a thoughtfully curated menu of autochthonous plates like oyster mushroom pappardelle and a honey-nut squash served with goat cheese, pickled poblanos, granola, and a drizzle of hot honey.

Wildlife is ever-present in the Berkshires, from the calming transparently clear waters of Lenox Beach to the newly renovated aquarium wing of the Berkshire Museum in Pittsfield. Out the window of our car, driving from town to town, we saw peaceful creeks, grazing deer, and chirping birds. I soaked it all in, even stopping at the side of Glendale Road in Stockbridge to take in the beauty of the Housatonic River.

In recent years, because of the chaos of flying with toddlers, I’ve come to really appreciate the convenience of going to places just outside of New York City. I just want a soft life—or one as soft as humanly possible. With a good playlist on deck (we lean toward Afrojazz or Bad Bunny), we strap the kids into their car seats and roll. Open space and calm have to be on the itinerary.

There are many places we could go, but this experience in the Berkshires really stuck with me because it offered a quiet luxury that doesn’t position itself as high-maintenance or arrogant. There’s an ease and openness to this place, and if nothing else, an amazing peck of apples I’ll be looking forward to on my next visit.

#6763. Cisco Intros Privileged Infrastructure Access for People and AI Agents

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

In a recent blog post, Cisco's Matt Caulfield described a system that uses cryptographic identity to connect an administrator or agent to the infrastructure needed for a particular task. Access is governed by short-lived privileges, with session recording providing a record of the work performed.

"If there is no work being done, no privilege exists," Caulfield wrote. Cisco positions the offering as a way to bring identity controls directly into infrastructure operations while reducing dependence on static credentials, shared secrets, and broad access.

The announcement follows Cisco's previously announced Teleport partnership and its Infrastructure Identity concept, which connects the identity of a person or machine with the resources it can access, the actions it performs, and the permissions governing those actions.

Caulfield described a gap between the controls organizations apply to workforce applications and those used to administer network infrastructure. Companies have invested in single sign-on, multifactor authentication, and risk-based controls for application access, he wrote, but those protections often do not follow an engineer logging into a router or switch.

Instead, infrastructure access may depend on long-lived credentials, shared accounts, or separate policy systems. According to Cisco, that separation makes it harder to enforce least privilege and establish which person or system performed a particular action.

The company illustrates the problem with an administrator updating hundreds of switches during a maintenance window. If some updates fail, the administrator needs to troubleshoot before the window closes. The permissions available to handle that work, however, may cover more infrastructure and remain active longer than the task requires.

Traditional privileged access management can reduce risk through credential vaulting and rotation, Caulfield wrote. But he argued that operational friction can undermine those controls when teams create exceptions to complete urgent work. Those exceptions can leave credentials and privileges usable after the immediate need has passed.

Cisco's stated objective is to give infrastructure teams a consistent route to the devices they manage while providing security teams with policy enforcement, temporary permissions, and a record of activity. The company describes operational convenience as part of the design, rather than treating access governance solely as a credential-management problem.

Privileged Infrastructure Access makes access an on-demand action, according to the announcement. Cryptographic identity establishes the actor, and the resulting connection provides only the privileges needed to complete the task. Those privileges expire when the task concludes.

For customers using Cisco Identity Services Engine (ISE) and Terminal Access Controller Access-Control System Plus (TACACS+), the offering can draw on existing policy and device context to govern access to Cisco routers and switches. Cisco says customers can extend the same access model to other infrastructure, including servers, Kubernetes clusters, and cloud resources.
