#6762. “Classic Red” Nails Are Back for Winter: 10 Elegant Alternatives to Nude Manicures

Embrace a simple but bold refresh on the timeless look

Amid passing beauty trends, some looks are considered classics. Take simple red manicures: whether kept vintage-inspired with rich, expensive-looking shades or modernized with simple shaping and high-shine finishes, the color has long symbolized sophistication. That's perhaps why celebrities keep returning to it season after season, with Selena Gomez recently making a case for its appeal in New York City, wearing a glossy cherry-red manicure that felt both effortlessly timeless and modern.

"A clean red manicure is timeless because it’s powerful, but understated at the same time,” says the nail artist Jessica White. “It doesn’t need embellishment—it’s a statement on its own.”

Though red nails never seem to go out of fashion, White explains that with brighter days ahead, the current mani minimalism movement could now make way for more lively colors. “After so much quiet and neutral beauty, people are craving a bit of personality again. And even a muted red or sheer cherry tone can feel like a fresh update if you’ve been living in nudes.”

Whether you’re embracing bolder shades this winter or gearing up for party season, subtle touches of red are as easy as they are elegant.

Ahead, see 10 of Bazaar’s favorite takes on a classic red manicure to inspire your next nail appointment.

A wash of vintage ruby red signifies confidence and grace in equal measure. Paired with a sharp squoval shape and a glossy top coat, it's ever-flattering.

Perfect for the gradual transition into the holiday season, deep wines and merlots are ideal for those looking for a statement-making hue with wearability.

According to White, “the classic red is associated with femininity, but it can also feel modern and minimal when done on a short, natural shape.”

Those with cooler undertones will suit blue-based reds or cherry tones, says White, while sleek, rounded almond shapes with length elongate the fingers with ease. Want to add a playful touch? Simply add a sleek blue dot at the base.

For those partial to a simple, minimalistic design, consider upgrading a traditional French tip with a wash of red.

All red tones (whether vibrant or dark) are effortlessly elevated with the help of a few gems. Opt for small rhinestones or white pearls for grown-up glamour.

For a brighter, joy-bringing variation, look no further. “Warmer undertones often suit tomato or brick reds with a hint of orange,” suggests White.

Those erring on the side of maximalism can find balance and boldness in a cat-eye manicure, which uses a reflective, magnetic finish over classic gels.

Micro-French tips with a touch of shimmer are perfect for the holiday season. Ultra-precise lines with a clean, nude base coat make the perfect pairing for everyday.

Brick reds and burgundies are easily elevated with a 3D glazed overlay. Recreate at home with a chrome powder and dense nail brush, and top with a high-shine, chip-resistant coat.

#6760. Survey Finds 84% of Organizations Reported at Least One AI-Related Incident in the Past Year

Companies are running into problems as they give artificial intelligence more control over critical business applications, with 84% reporting at least one AI-related incident in the past year, according to a new survey from identity security firm Delinea.

The company's 2026 Application Access Governance report found that some organizations have experienced incorrect financial transactions and AI actions they couldn't trace or explain. The findings raise concerns about whether enterprises have enough oversight of AI systems that can make decisions and take actions on their own

The findings are based on an independent survey of 1,000 senior finance and finance technology leaders in the United States and United Kingdom. The AI-specific analysis included 879 respondents whose organizations use the technology in critical business applications.

The report found that 35% of surveyed organizations have deployed AI agents capable of taking actions within business applications. However, only 11% of organizations using AI said their governance and security controls were keeping pace with deployment.

Delinea also found gaps in centralized visibility into agent deployment and permissions, including who owns agents and who approved their use.

Some respondents reported segregation-of-duties conflicts involving AI, while others identified agents that continued operating after the employees who deployed them had left the organization.

The research also highlighted gaps in monitoring access across business systems. Only 46% of respondents said they continuously monitor and manage cumulative access risks across applications.

Those gaps can make it difficult to identify segregation-of-duties conflicts, in which a person or agent has permissions across systems that should be kept separate.

Despite those issues, 95% of respondents said they were confident their access controls would withstand scrutiny in an external audit.

However, 26% reported access-related audit findings that had recurred in consecutive years, while 25% said some findings had remained unresolved for three or more years.

Only 22% of organizations had a single named owner for each application, with ownership tracked in one system of record.

Meanwhile, 60% reported an increase over the past two years in the number of critical applications operating outside the scope of access governance or Sarbanes-Oxley Act compliance.

The findings highlight the oversight challenges associated with giving AI agents authority to act within business systems, particularly when organizations cannot consistently identify who owns an agent or trace its actions.

Frank Vukovits, chief security scientist at Delinea, said organizations remain responsible for the actions AI agents take within their systems.

"When an agent can approve a transaction or change permissions, the business owns the outcome. You need to know what every agent can do, who owns it, and who approved it, before an auditor asks or your financials are misstated," Vukovits said.

For the full report, go to the Delinea site.

It also comes in more sizes (4 through 15, compared to the Ring 5’s 6 through 13), so it’s the one to get if yours falls outside that range.

One shopper called the Ring 4 a “game-changer” as someone living with chronic illnesses and said they’re able to “better understand [their] body, track [their] patterns, and recognize changes.” Another added: “As a woman, the cycle tracking alone has been incredibly valuable. Being able to see how different phases of my cycle affect my sleep, recovery, stress levels, energy, and overall readiness has helped me make much better decisions about work, exercise, and self-care.” That’s a pretty compelling argument alone!

#6757. Jaime Ray Newman Thinks Season 2 of The Hunting Wives Is “Shakespearean”

Bazaar speaks with the actress in an exclusive interview to discuss the finale of The Hunting Wives season 2

In the first season of Netflix’s erotic melodrama The Hunting Wives, desire drives most, if not all, of Callie’s decisions—and indiscretions. Her unyielding loyalty to Maple Brook’s conniving diva, the formidable Margo Banks (Malin Akerman), springs from a delusional, obsessive, unrequited love—a love worth spilling blood over. This ardent personality is exactly what first attracted Jaime Ray Newman to the role.

“The first thing that struck me about Callie, when I auditioned for the part, is that she is a woman who yearns,” the actress tells Bazaar about her sharp-tongued character ahead of The Hunting Wives season two release. “This is a woman who deeply wants what she cannot have and maybe will never be able to have because of where she was born. We’re all victims of time and place. There’s so much that she wants that she cannot and may never have.”

The Hunting Wives, like Callie’s yearning, focuses mostly on Margo, the salacious wife of the richest man in town, Jed (Dermot Mulroney). Much of the first season’s conflict stems from Margo’s extramarital affairs with both 18-year-old Brad (George Ferreira) and Massachusetts transplant Sophie O’Neil (Brittany Snow). Then, the murder of a teenage girl rattles the tiny Texas town, and the gossip and backstabbing that follow are what both pushes Callie away and brings her back to Margo’s side in a triumphant, bloody ending. This valiant act revives her romance with Margo and also lands her a reality TV show, Married Justice, with her husband, Sheriff Jonny (Branton Box). “Season 1 ends on a high for Callie,” Newman says. “She’s just defended her woman, and Nancy Grace told her, ‘You’re a star.’ There’s no place but up from here. Callie ends season 1, thinking, ‘The world is my oyster,’ literally.”

In season 2, The Hunting Wives supercharges its soap-operatic storytelling, with much of the tragedy circling Callie’s character, a role Newman doubled down on for these eight episodes. “I find this to be Shakespearean in many ways,” Newman says. “It’s like a Greek tragedy, high drama.” This season, Callie’s arc is defined by utter heartbreak, as she finally confronts Margot’s true, evil, manipulative side and falls in love with a newcomer to Maple Brook, Nadia (Kim Patula), Married Justice’s producer. An effective tragedy depends on someone having something substantial to lose.

Bazaar spoke with Newman about Callie’s central role in the second season of The Hunting Wives, the tragic final two episodes, and how she believes her character is gearing up for revenge in season 3. Spoilers ahead!

I’m not a reality-TV-show watcher, but when I figured out we were doing this, I did a deep dive. For the photo shoot, I probably had 50 photos of these women from the Real Housewives in my trailer: how they posed, what they did with their lips, what they did with their hands, where their breasts were. My trailer was plastered with [their photos] and also Kristi Noem. We copied Kristi’s wig, and I studied her make-up. She’s in baseball hats and tactical gear. We stole a lot of ideas from her belts. May her career rest in peace, but she was a real North Star.

Margo gives Callie the greatest gift in saying, “You’re not my person; you need to move on.” She’s very clear about that. There’s the scene when I pull up in my truck, and I realize she left my romantic dinner I made for her and went to be with Sophie. Callie is devastated by it, but very soon after, this wonderful door of Nadia opens.

Kim had a really tall order, and it’s just become a hit with all these characters that have [gotten big enough to be parodied] on SNL, for God’s sake. To come and fill those shoes and find her little corner of this universe, I give her so much credit because she didn’t push and didn’t try to overwhelm anyone. It’s a very natural reaction to come on set and feel like you have to prove yourself. Kim is such a good actress that she leaned back rather than leaned forward. She made us come to her, and you can feel that in her performance. She’s so good. Isn’t she amazing? I loved working with her. Every time there was a Callie and Nadia scene, every day was a little different. She and I would rehearse on our off days. We’d run lines together, go to the gym together, work out together. We became really good friends. I didn’t know she was going to be killed because I didn’t get all the episodes. Early on, like in season 1, we got all the episodes. We walked into season 1 knowing everything, whereas in season 2, they came out in real time. But I had a hunch that happiness doesn’t last for very long in Maple Brook. Callie’s a little too happy here. Rebecca Cutter is going to rip that from her clutches very soon.

In the bar scene when Nadia tells me that she’s gay. I remember there were a bunch of takes that we did where I could barely even say the word lesbian. It could not exit my mouth. I don’t think they used any of those takes. I don’t think Callie had ever seen someone living a life she probably would want for herself. You wanting to be an actor as a child and having a real talent and propensity for it, but your family saying, “No, you have to be a pharmacist.” You [can] go about your whole life only thinking there’s one way, in one direction, and when you’re 40, you meet someone who actually was able to do that and did have a family that was supportive, and Callie sees these parallel lives. That’s what she sees in Nadia.

I was so devastated when I saw that Nadia was killed. I couldn’t believe it because I didn’t know. How can you do that to me?! I was so upset for Callie. What’s interesting is that when I watched the whole thing—they sent it to us a couple of months ago—in the final scene where I’m shooting at the Married Justice poster, I wasn’t shooting at that in real life. Rebecca had the idea to superimpose it, and I’m shooting Margo in the head.

We played it a bunch of ways. We did it one where Callie was 100 percent certain, I’m seeking justice. She made up her mind. We shot one one-take where I’m totally conflicted, and I’m crying. Actually, we put black eye makeup on to copy Nadia. We took black eyeliner and circled my eyes, and I had black makeup tears streaming down my face. We did one where I decided, “Fuck it, I’m going to suppress everything that I know is right and I’m going with Jonny to Austin.” So I didn’t know what would end up there.

My impression from that is Callie is going to go full force for revenge. How that plays into season 3, I don’t know. But how can she not? She’s a woman who has morals and values. She was on a TV show called Married Justice! She’s not going to abandon justice now that her show’s been canceled.

Who hasn’t had a moment where you tell your partner, “I did that,” and you’re like, “Oh my God, are they still going to love me?” That’s what that moment was, because she knows those photos can unlock and really take Margo down, but it’s also showing a side of Callie that’s very unattractive. She was under a spell—in the cult of Margo, which a lot of us join. She needed to believe that she could show these photos and take the risk that Nadia might think she’s too fucking batshit. She does it to serve justice.

Before we started shooting season 1, Rebecca Cutter, our showrunner, said, “Callie and Jonny probably have the best marriage on the show. I don’t think they are beards. I think they really, really love each other, but they can’t fully fulfill each other.” Also, he’s trapped. God bless Branton. I love acting with him, and he’s really a cowboy. He’s from Texas, and he’s a full foot taller than me. It’s great casting. A relationship is about understanding. What those rules are is made up by those two people, so who am I to judge their relationship? We all enter into a pact when you commit yourself to someone else. Callie’s makes me a little sad that she could be able to live a better life without so many secrets, but these are the circumstances she was born into.

We’re all speaking in code for much of this season. We say things through daggers in our eyes that we can’t say in public. Margo has caused nothing but grief for Callie, but she can’t quit her. She can’t escape the web that this woman weaves. Who better to do it with than the magnificent Malin Akerman? Callie goes from someone who thinks that Margo is the love of her life to Margo being the devil. I don’t know about season 3, but I don’t think Callie will rest until she gets justice.

#6755. Report: Cyber Criminals Move Toward More Targeted Phishing Attacks

Cyber criminals are moving away from mass phishing scams and focusing on more targeted attacks, increasingly using trusted websites and services to trick victims, according to a new report from KnowBe4. The security firm found that spear-phishing attacks increased 41% over the past year.

The company's Phishing Threat Trends Report, Vol. 8 found that overall phishing volume increased 29.8% year over year. Organizations received an average of one dangerous e-mail for every 733 messages, but the bigger concern is how attackers are changing their approach.

Rather than sending the same fraudulent message to thousands of people, criminals are spending more time researching their targets and creating convincing messages that appear to come from trusted coworkers, businesses, and technology platforms.

The findings are based on threat data from KnowBe4 Defend, the company's inbound e-mail security platform, which processes approximately 1.15 billion event signals daily.

KnowBe4 found that several traditional phishing methods declined significantly compared with 2025. Mobile-focused scams dropped 47.3%, advance-fee fraud fell 31.3%, and missed-message scams declined 11.3%.

Meanwhile, spear phishing increased 41.1%, pre-attack reconnaissance rose 33.7%, and company impersonation climbed 28%.

The shift suggests that attackers are finding more value in carefully crafted scams that exploit existing business relationships and workplace routines.

Business e-mail compromise (BEC) remains particularly common, accounting for 51.2% of phishing e-mails analyzed in the report. Human resources, finance departments, and executive leadership are among the most frequently targeted groups.

Attackers also appear to be timing their campaigns around normal business activity. KnowBe4 found that 90.4% of phishing messages arrive Monday through Friday, with Monday accounting for the largest share at 20.6%.

One of the report's more troubling findings involves the growing use of legitimate technology platforms to deliver malicious content.

KnowBe4 found that phishing attacks were twice as likely to originate from legitimate infrastructure in the third quarter of 2026 compared with the previous quarter.

That includes AI services, where attackers are taking advantage of trusted domains to host fake developer consoles, login prompts, and credential-stealing pages. The report also identified a 228% increase in AI-brand impersonation attacks between March and August.

Because these malicious pages can operate on legitimate domains with valid security certificates, users may not see the usual warning signs associated with suspicious websites.

"For years, security advice has focused on a predictable checklist: Check the sender address, hover over the link, and look for the padlock," said Jack Chapman, senior vice president of threat intelligence at KnowBe4.

"While still foundational, that guidance is becoming less useful by the day."

The findings echo a recent report about management software and AI e-mail assistants. Research from Fortra and Barracuda showed how criminals are using legitimate administrative tools and hidden AI instructions to manipulate users and compromise systems.

In an active social engineering campaign, cyber attackers are impersonating IT help desks and using fake passkey setup requests to compromise employee identities and gain access to enterprise cloud data.

Microsoft is expanding the scope of its AI-powered Project Perception security platform to address the growing concern that AI agents may have access to sensitive information and systems, yet don't always behave as expected.

Ransomware attacks are becoming more automated, and AI could take that much further, according to cybersecurity company NCC Group's July 2026 threat intelligence report.

Avantis Education, the creator of ClassVR, has introduced two new immersive learning libraries for its Eduverse+ platform: EscapeRooms, a math suite for K-8 students, and LingoLab, a Spanish and English language learning suite for students in grades 6-12.

#6751. “I Don’t Care About the Pointed Foot or the High Leg”— Celia Rowlson-Hall Looks for Authenticity in Movement

The choreographer is taking on Spring Awakening’s Off-Broadway Revival

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

One evening in November, Celia Rowlson-Hall slipped into a conference room for a rare break. The choreographer has spent the past four weeks rehearsing a new Off-Broadway revival of Spring Awakening, directed by Tony winner Danya Taymor, and it’s the second day of tech week. She had been holed up in Studio Seaview’s adjacent theater, fine-tuning “The Bitch of Living,” an angsty number about wet dreams, busty piano teachers, and Nietzsche. Previews were set to begin in a little over a week, and Rowlson-Hall eagerly tucked into a takeout grain bowl, fueling up for her evening ahead.

Set in 19th-century Germany, Spring Awakening is about the turmoil of developing sexuality in teenagers in a society attempting to keep them in ignorance. In 2006, the playwright Steven Sater and the musician Duncan Sheik adapted it into a musical that won eight Tony Awards, including for choreographer Bill T. Jones, who translated rebellion and repression into a gestural vocabulary. That original production also launched a generation of musical theater stars, including Jonathan Groff, Lea Michele, and John Gallagher Jr. In this spirit, the production held an open audition that attracted 2,532 performers (two actors were cast from this process). Rowlson-Hall kept her eye out for those actors with a sense of grounded earthiness and truthful storytelling. “I’m always going to be looking out for authenticity and personality inside of movement,” she says. “I don't care about the pointed foot or the high leg; it's never interested me, even as a dancer. I've never understood dance for dance’s sake.”

Spring Awakening will mark Rowlson-Hall’s theatrical debut after nearly two decades of directing and choreographing for screen. Among her work are the scenes of ecstatic prayer in The Testament of Ann Lee and Aftersun’s devastating rave sequence (she also played the adult version of the lead character). Humorously, she recently appeared in The Drama as a dance teacher intent on perfection, a mentality that couldn’t be farther from Rowlson-Hall’s own intuitive and empathetic style.

With big blue eyes and a choppy mop of dark hair, Rowlson-Hall, 42, is expressive even when sequestered behind a conference room desk. She frequently puts her fork down to gesture, including when recalling a friend’s suggestion “that all artists have one or two rocks that they will turn over their entire lives.” Rolling a pair of invisible stones around in her palms, she names religion and femininity as her thematic preoccupations. “The impact of religion in the body, specifically the female body, and what femininity looks like as taught to me by my parents versus from society and my own experiences.” She laughs, adding, “As my other friend said, ‘Celia loves a repression era.’”

Like the characters in Spring Awakening, Rowlson-Hall grew up in a small, religious community. In her case, it was Urbanna, Virginia, population 492 as of 2020, in a Christian Scientist household. She started dancing at 5 and knew from a young age that she wanted to use movement to tell stories of her own. After studying at the North Carolina School of the Arts, Rowlson-Hall moved to New York in 2006, the same year Spring Awakening premiered. “I remember hearing about the show, but also being too poor to go,” she says.

Two years later, she was planning to start her own dance company when she was asked to choreograph a music video for MGMT. She immediately felt at home on a film set, and also appreciated that the medium offered a more enduring result than a concert dancen ephemeral live performance. “The forever part,” she says. “I needed it then in my life.” She continued working on music videos, commercials, and television, absorbing lessons on filmmaking along the way. After a series of movement-based feminist shorts, Rowlson-Hall used Kickstarter to finance her first feature, Ma (2015), in which she stars in the dialogue-free dance film as a modern-day Virgin Mary on a pilgrimage to Las Vegas.

Rowlson-Hall met her now-wife, director and showrunner Mia Lidofsky, while choreographing Girls’ acclaimed “Beach House” episode in 2013. Rowlson-Hall was in a heterosexual marriage at the time, and she describes coming out at 30 as “this incredible aha moment.” But then, “Shame came in and clamped me down. I stopped moving.” It was a confusing contrast: while experiencing a profound sense of freedom and selfhood, she became disconnected from her body for the first time. Rowlson-Hall is anxious to elaborate. “The impact of me coming out was very difficult on my family, they initially had a hard time accepting it,” she says. “Then my mom got very ill, and I put my life and body on pause because I was so scared for her.” From there followed a decade of extreme highs (falling in love, marriage, motherhood) and lows (divorce, several deaths including both parents). “There were a lot of roadblocks for me to be able to find physicality and movement again because I kept getting knocked down,” she says.

She credits Ann Lee with allowing her to feel that connection again. The writer and director, Mona Fastvold, is a close friend; the two met back in the day on Gossip Girl. Rowlson-Hall was eager to inhabit the 18th century visionary, an illiterate blacksmith's daughter who became the founder and leader of the Shakers. “As a kid, I used to love to pray, it was my favorite thing,” she says. “I really wanted to ask myself ‘what if I got to decide what prayer looks like?’” She could also relate to the Shakers’ use of dance as transcendence. “From when I was young, I always felt like I was needing to exorcise things out of my body that weren't mine,” Rowlson-Hall says. “So much of what I do is trying to clear out to actually understand who I am.” The viscera is not strictly a metaphor. “When I feel like I'm going to vomit, I know that I've tapped into the right thing,” she says. “I choreograph from there, and then I can't touch it again.” She makes a gagging sound. “And then it's out of my body.”

Rowlson-Hall returned to the stage last year with Sissy, a semi-autobiographical dance-theater piece reimagining the myth of Sisyphus as the struggles of a female artist tasked with caring for a young child and an ill father. “There's something about theater and motherhood that feel very aligned,” she says with a laugh. “It's all alive and happening in front of you, and you've got to love it and let it go. It journeys on without you, you actually have no control.” That said, she’s working on her next film, which she describes as “a time traveling love story of Eve and Lilith exiting the Garden of Eden” and spending lifetimes together and apart. “There they are again,” Rowlson-Hall says. “Those old stones.”

#6750. Study: Sign-In Problems Are Primary Drivers of Help Desk Tickets

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a new study from ControlUp.

Analyzing 1,069,110 help desk tickets, the IT operations company identified password resets, account lockouts, and multifactor authentication (MFA) issues as major drivers of support demand.

At one large enterprise, account-related tickets took a median of 11 minutes to resolve, compared with 50 hours for network problems. Meanwhile, 87% of network and phone tickets were reassigned between teams, compared with 20% of account tickets.

MFA enrollment and re-enrollment problems appeared at 40% of organizations, with one in five linking these issues to employees replacing or upgrading their phones. Virtual desktop problems appeared at 55% of organizations analyzed.

Traditional phone support remained a major channel for IT assistance.

Among organizations that recorded how support requests were submitted, 57% reported that phone calls accounted for approximately two-thirds or more of contacts. At one organization, that figure reached 99%.

Machine-generated tickets from monitoring, security, backup, and patching systems appeared at half of the organizations studied. At 15%, automated tickets represented between 25% and 50% of all requests.

The research covered enterprises across retail, healthcare, financial services, manufacturing, construction, energy, and IT services.

ControlUp said the findings highlight the need to evaluate recurring problems and their operational impact rather than relying solely on ticket volume and resolution speed.

"IT organizations have traditionally measured support performance by how quickly they close tickets, but that doesn't tell the whole story," said Simon Townsend, senior vice president of marketing and office of the CTO at ControlUp.

"A password reset that takes a few minutes and an infrastructure failure that disrupts an entire location shouldn't carry the same operational weight."

For more information, go to the ControlUp site.

In an active social engineering campaign, cyber attackers are impersonating IT help desks and using fake passkey setup requests to compromise employee identities and gain access to enterprise cloud data.

Microsoft is expanding the scope of its AI-powered Project Perception security platform to address the growing concern that AI agents may have access to sensitive information and systems, yet don't always behave as expected.

Ransomware attacks are becoming more automated, and AI could take that much further, according to cybersecurity company NCC Group's July 2026 threat intelligence report.

Avantis Education, the creator of ClassVR, has introduced two new immersive learning libraries for its Eduverse+ platform: EscapeRooms, a math suite for K-8 students, and LingoLab, a Spanish and English language learning suite for students in grades 6-12.

#6747. Enterprise AI Spending Outpaces Adoption Growth, Report Finds

Enterprise AI spending is growing faster than the number of organizations paying for it, according to a new Menlo Ventures report that points to a shift from buying access for employees to financing work performed by agents.

The venture capital firm's 2026 State of Enterprise AI report estimates deployment spending reached a $176.8 billion annualized run rate by September, up from $33 billion in 2025. The share of enterprises paying for AI rose from 55% to 65%.

A larger bill can reflect broader use inside an organization, but it does not independently establish that the additional work is useful or profitable.

Menlo's methodology describes revenue figures as annualized run rates. The $176.8 billion estimate therefore should not be read as $176.8 billion already spent during the first nine months of 2026.

The report also distinguishes unusually heavy spenders from the typical respondent. Average reported spending reached 4.7 times the prior year's level, while the median reached 2.2 times. That gap suggests a smaller group of large spenders drove the average substantially.

The practical implication is that an industry growth figure may be a poor benchmark for an individual organization's budget. Buyers need to understand their own workload growth and the results it produces.

"Agents are changing the economics of software," Menlo principal Derek Xiao said in the firm's announcement.

Menlo reports that agents account for 80% of tokens on OpenRouter, a service providing access to multiple models. That measures activity on one platform, rather than all enterprise AI use. Token consumption also measures processing volume, rather than completed business outcomes.

An agent that makes repeated attempts can consume more resources without delivering proportionately more value. For procurement teams, the relevant comparison is consequently the cost of completing acceptable work, including the review it requires.

Menlo estimates that Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google capture 69% of enterprise AI deployment spending. That measure is distinct from their shares of enterprise language-model API spending, a narrower category. The figures should not be used interchangeably.

The report draws on a Morning Consult survey of nearly 400 U.S. enterprise decision-makers responsible for more than $4 billion in AI spending. Market totals are modeled estimates informed by surveys, public filings, and third-party reports. Menlo discloses investments in Anthropic and OpenRouter.

A separate announcement from Information Services Group describes buyers putting more emphasis on the economics and reliability of production deployments. ISG says ongoing security and observability work remains necessary as organizations scale.

That provides a useful distinction between market demand and business value. Suppliers can generate growing revenue while customers still determine which deployments justify the cost.

For enterprise leaders, the new spending estimate strengthens the case for examining usage beyond license counts. It leaves the harder purchasing question with the customer: How much worthwhile work did the increased spending actually buy?

Menlo's State of Enterprise AI report is available here.

#6744. Phishing Threats Totaled 7.3 Billion in Q3 2026

E-mail phishing threats reached 7.3 billion in Q3 2026, with September activity increasing 41% from August, according to Microsoft Threat Intelligence.

According to a report from Microsoft Threat Intelligence and the Microsoft Defender Security Research Team, phishing detections reached nearly 3 billion in September alone.

The findings also highlighted changes in attacker techniques, including business-service impersonation, credential theft, and social engineering through Microsoft Teams.

The Redmond-based firm recognized approximately 129 million malicious messages spoofing the sender domains of tracked business services during Q3.

DocuSign was the most frequently spoofed brand in September, accounting for 29% of the month's tracked spoofing activity. Adobe, SurveyMonkey, and Barracuda also experienced increases toward the end of the quarter.

The research found that some malicious messages were delivered despite failing e-mail authentication checks and being identified as threats.

In September, tenant- or user-level security overrides applied to 63% of these messages with a recorded security outcome — most involved tenant-level exceptions.

Microsoft warned that overly broad exceptions intended to support legitimate business communications can weaken protection against impersonation attacks.

The company emphasized that these findings do not establish whether recipients opened the messages or whether accounts were compromised.

Microsoft also recorded approximately 141,000 malicious Teams call attempts during Q3.

Attackers increasingly used ordinary personal names instead of explicitly impersonating IT support personnel. Such display names accounted for 46% of an observed sample in July, rising to 88% in September.

Meanwhile, QR code phishing declined 67% compared to Q2, totaling roughly 12.1 million attacks.

CAPTCHA-gated phishing activity increased toward the end of the quarter, reaching 3.1 million detected attacks in September. SVG attachments played a significant role in the increase.

Credential theft remained the primary objective of malicious attachment campaigns, with approximately 94% to 96% of payload-based attacks directing users to phishing sites or fraudulent sign-in pages.

Microsoft recommended reviewing Exchange Online Protection and Defender for Office 365 configurations, enabling Safe Links and Safe Attachments, and strengthening authentication protections.

The company also advised organizations to review security exceptions and verify the identities of external Teams contacts before responding to suspicious requests.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog post.

According to a survey by data and AI services provider Further, enterprises are reporting benefits from artificial intelligence even as their data preparation and oversight processes remain incomplete.

Our nation's largest early childhood program has not fully caught up with what science now tells us about how children learn.

A recent Cloud Security Alliance report urges organizations to inventory, govern, and continuously monitor machine and agent identities.

Recent research from Thales highlights how AI adoption and emerging quantum computing threats are putting pressure on existing security measures.

#6740. The Case for Ending a Relationship Even When Nothing Is Wrong With It

During my sophomore year of high school, in the throes of the COVID-19 pandemic, I started a relationship with my first love. While I am known to be a Type A planner, I tried to live in the moment, experiencing all the butterflies, first-times, and ups and downs of adolescent affection, just like my idols in the best coming-of-age movies.

It wasn’t until the start of the college application process, two years later, that my love-blinded bubble popped and I realized we simply wanted different things. But looking back, I wonder how much of my break up decision was about the relationship itself, and how much was about the life I was looking forward to in college and beyond.

I think that’s common for a lot of young people in relationships. Many of us break up at young ages not because there’s something wrong with the other or the dynamic—there's no cheating, no explosive fight, and no obvious villain—but because we feel like we should. I’ve witnessed friends who are perfectly happy together in their early 20s call it quits for the fear of missing out on the future experiences that are a fundamental part of being young.

If I’m being honest, I think it’s good to expand your dating portfolio before finding your forever love; there's great value in diversifying. Different relationships introduce us to different versions of ourselves, revealing what we need, what we're willing to compromise on, what we never want to experience again and simply what we do and don’t lie. And while I admire the people who are “one and done,” I can't help but wonder how you could know what you want forever if you've only ever known one version of someone—mainly yourself.

In relation to my first love, I knew I was ready to end things, but remained in limbo for months. Not only was that a waste of his time, but it was stupid to waste my own. Looking back, I don't regret the relationship, nor do I regret leaving it. Since then, I've loved and been loved by different people, for different reasons, and learned something new about myself each time. I'm not saying you have to date half of your city before settling down, or that those who stay with their first love are missing out. For some couples, growing up together is precisely what makes their relationship so special and I would never knock something like that. But I do believe there’s also value in choosing to leave a relationship even if there isn’t anything wrong with it—not for the reason that it’s all you’ve ever known, but because you realize you want to know more.

#6730. So, You Want to Tie Someone Up During Sex

Consider this your beginner’s guide to bondage, minus the complicated knots.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Being tied up during sex sounds pretty hot in theory, but the logistics of actually making it happen? Slightly less hot. What are you supposed to use? Where do your hands go? And how do you tell your partner you'd like to be temporarily relieved of all decision-making responsibilities in the bedroom without making it a whole thing?

Fortunately, experimenting with bondage doesn't have to involve a complicated rope setup or a trip to the hardware store (in fact, please skip the latter).

“You don’t need to rush to the most elaborate version of bondage to make it hot,” says Lisa Opel, the resident sex educator at MYLO.

Even the simplest forms of bondage, though, require a little more communication than your average hookup. Whether you're interested in doing the tying, being the one who's tied up, or taking turns, a little preparation goes a long way.

Below, sex experts break down how to tie someone up during sex, what to use (and what to leave in your junk drawer), and how to explore bondage safely without getting in over your head.

Bondage is a form of consensual play that involves restricting someone's movement. It's the “B” in BDSM, but you don't necessarily have to be into pain, punishment, or even dominance and submission to enjoy it.

“People enjoy rope play for all kinds of reasons: the feeling of surrender, the trust and power exchange, the anticipation, restriction, or simply because it feels meditative, pleasurable, or playful,” says Wildilisa Moronta, a relationship therapist and sex expert at Taimi.

For the person being restrained, part of the appeal might be relinquishing control or experiencing familiar sensations in a new way. For the person doing the restraining, it might be the excitement of taking charge or building anticipation for their partner. And for some people, the visual alone is a major turn-on.

Bondage can be as simple as wearing a pair of soft cuffs or as elaborate as practicing shibari, a form of rope bondage with Japanese roots. Some people even enjoy rope bondage as an artistic practice outside of sex, says Moronta.

Basically, there's no minimum level of kinkiness required to enjoy bondage. You can be perfectly content with a pair of quick-release cuffs and never feel the urge to pick up a rope.

You don't have to go from zero to a full-blown BDSM scene the first time you experiment with bondage. Both Moronta and Opel recommend keeping things simple and getting comfortable with the experience before introducing anything more complicated. Here's how to get started:

Before anyone breaks out the handcuffs, make sure you're working with the same definition of bondage. “One person might be imagining wrists in cuffs while kissing; the other might be picturing pain, control, or a much more intense scene,” says Opel.

Discuss who wants to be restrained, what kinds of touch you're comfortable with, and what your hard and soft limits are. You'll also want to talk about any injuries or joint issues that could make certain positions uncomfortable. Establish a safeword and discuss how you'd like to communicate throughout the experience.

You don't need anything particularly elaborate for your first experience with bondage. Opel recommends soft or padded cuffs with quick-release fastenings designed specifically for bondage, since they're easier to remove.

If you're specifically interested in rope bondage, both experts recommend taking a class with a qualified instructor before attempting any knots on a partner. Whatever restraints you choose, make sure you know how to remove them quickly before using them.

You can experiment with the feeling of bondage without actually tying anyone up. Moronta suggests loosely draping a scarf or rope over your partner's wrists while they're sitting or lying comfortably, without securing it or restricting their movement. It's a low-pressure way to figure out whether the sensation and dynamic appeal to you both.

If you're ready to experiment with actual restraints, Opel recommends starting with soft, quick-release cuffs around the wrists, keeping your partner's hands in front of their body rather than behind their back. There's no need to attempt complicated positions, especially ones that put unnecessary strain on the shoulders or joints. The experience should be about enjoying the sensation of restraint, not testing anyone's flexibility.

If you're the person doing the tying, you're responsible for more than setting the mood. You also need to pay attention to your partner's physical and emotional comfort and be prepared to release them immediately.

If you're the person being tied up, think about what appeals to you and what might make you uncomfortable. Maybe you love the idea of relinquishing control but get anxious when you can't move freely. Communicate those feelings to your partner, and speak up if something doesn't feel right.

Giving up control doesn't mean giving up your ability to make decisions. You're allowed to change your mind at any point, even if the experience was your idea in the first place.

Checking in doesn't have to take you out of the moment. “A quiet ‘How are you feeling?’ can help someone feel safe enough to relax and surrender,” says Moronta.

“The restrained person shouldn’t have to carry all the responsibility for saying when something is wrong,” adds Opel. Rather than relying on a safeword alone, ask specific questions about your partner's physical comfort and pay attention to changes in their behavior. If something seems off, pause and check in.

When you're finished, remove the restraints and spend some time checking in with each other. What that aftercare looks like will depend on what you both need.

“That might be a cuddle, water, a blanket, space, reassurance, or just a very normal conversation,” says Opel.

It's also worth talking about what you enjoyed, what you didn't, and whether you'd want to do anything differently next time. You might discover that you love bondage, that you prefer being restrained to doing the restraining (or vice versa), or that you'd rather leave it in the realm of fantasy.

As much fun as bondage can be, restricting someone's movement comes with very real physical risks. And just because a restraint looks comfortable doesn't necessarily mean it is.

“A common suggestion is to see whether you can slide a finger beneath a tie, but that doesn’t prove the tie is safe or rule out nerve pressure,” says Moronta.

Keep restraints away from the neck, avoid positions that restrict breathing, and be mindful of pressure on joints, nerves, and blood vessels. Both experts recommend leaving suspension, weight-bearing ties, and complicated rope work to people who've received proper training.

To keep things safe, both experts also recommend the traffic light system for safewords: Green means you're comfortable continuing, yellow means slow down or check in, and red means stop and release. If speaking might be difficult, establish a nonverbal signal beforehand that the restrained person can reliably make and their partner can easily recognize. If you can't establish a reliable way to communicate, don't restrict your partner's ability to speak or signal.

During play, Opel recommends asking specific questions, like whether anything feels too tight or whether your partner is experiencing any tingling or pain. Numbness, burning, weakness, dizziness, loss of movement, and difficulty breathing are all signs to stop and release the restraints immediately. Seek emergency medical help for difficulty breathing or other severe symptoms, and seek medical attention if symptoms persist after the restraints are removed.

You'll also want to stay sober, keep any necessary release tools (including safety shears if you're using rope) within reach, and never leave a restrained partner unattended, even for a minute.

If someone wants to stop for any reason, release them immediately. “Don’t persuade them to continue and don’t make them explain themselves while they’re still tied up,” says Opel.

Consent is ongoing, even after the cuffs go on, and agreeing to try bondage once doesn't mean either of you is obligated to do it again.

Not everything that can be used as a restraint should be. Ideally, you want something that's comfortable against the skin, won't tighten unexpectedly, and can be removed quickly.

“I’d choose purpose-made soft or padded cuffs, Velcro or other quick-release restraints, or bondage tape designed to stick to itself rather than skin,” says Opel. If you're shopping in person, take the opportunity to feel the materials, check the sizing, and familiarize yourself with how the restraints fasten and release.

If it's rope bondage you're interested in, Moronta notes that materials like jute, hemp, cotton, and nylon vary in texture and how they feel against the skin. Try handling a few varieties during a bondage workshop before investing in your own rope.

As for household items? Scarves, bathrobe belts, and neckties might look relatively harmless, but they can slip or tighten unexpectedly. The experts advise against using metal handcuffs, zip ties, thin cords, hardware-store rope, or anything else that's rigid or difficult to remove. Duct tape and electrical tape are also a no-go.

For more beginner-friendly options, check out our guide to the best bondage restraints. You don't need to invest in an entire collection of BDSM accessories to figure out whether bondage is your thing.

#6723. A Complete Timeline of Justin and Hailey Bieber’s Relationship

The singer and supermodel-turned-skin care mogul are one of Hollywood’s leading power couples.

Justin Bieber and Hailey Baldwin dated on and off long before they became husband and wife. Hailey first met Justin in the late aughts, and they eventually struck up a casual romance.

Though they decided to keep it out of the public eye, this didn’t stop fans from obsessing over their relationship. After a breakup and surprise reconciliation-turned-whirlwind engagement two years later, Justin officially made Hailey his Mrs. during a September 2018 New York City courthouse ceremony. They got married in front of friends and family in a more formal wedding a year later. In May 2024, they announced they were expecting their first child. They welcomed their son, Jack, in August 2024.

From their highly publicized South Carolina nuptials to their adjustment to parenthood, here’s the couple’s full history and how their relationship has evolved.

Hailey has her father, Stephen, to thank for introducing her to her future husband. There’s even footage of their first meeting, which took place backstage at The Today Show. Hailey and Justin—then in their teens—shared a brief handshake and the rest, as they say, is history.

Chaperoned by her father yet again, Hailey attended the New York City premiere of Justin’s documentary Never Say Never. Later that month, Justin confirmed his relationship with Selena Gomez when the couple made their red carpet debut at Vanity Fair’s Oscar After Party.

Hailey officially declared herself a member of the Jelena fan club. The model took to Twitter, sharing her support for the couple. “I don’t care what anyone says but Justin Bieber and Selena Gomez together is the definition of a teenage dream! #word,” Hailey wrote.

After calling it quits with Gomez, Justin was photographed attending church services at Hillsong alongside Hailey and fellow supermodel Kendall Jenner. It wasn’t long before fans began speculating that Hailey and Justin were an item.

Hailey denied any romantic connection between her and Justin. She set the record straight during an interview with E!. “I’ve known [Justin] since I was like 13—and we’ve just been good friends over the years,” Hailey said. “We have just stayed close, and there’s nothing more to it than that.”

Justin backed up Hailey’s statement in an Instagram post. The singer shared a snap of himself and Hailey, writing, “I’m super single and this is my good friend u would know otherwise.”

Hailey joined Justin and his family on vacation in Anguilla, fueling rumors that their friendship was actually something more.

Justin made his romance with Hailey Instagram official. Shortly after New Year’s Day, he shared a photo of them kissing.

Hailey and Justin revealed that their relationship was casual. “We are not an exclusive couple,” Hailey told E!. Meanwhile, in an interview with GQ, Justin called Hailey “someone I really love,” but he added that he didn’t “want to be held down by anything.”

Hailey and Justin ended their brief fling. According to People, Justin also unfollowed Hailey on Instagram amid his new relationship with Sofia Richie.

Following their split, Hailey and Justin completely lost touch for almost two years. In 2020, Hailey shared her thoughts on their time apart: “Our lives seemed to be going in very different directions,” she told ELLE. “I actually think—now that I look back at it being married—that it was a good thing for the two of us, very healthy.”

During Justin and Hailey’s rift, the singer rekindled his romance with Gomez in November 2017. However, by March 2018, Jelena was over for good.

Hailey revealed that she was speaking to Justin again. “We went through a long period of time when we weren’t friends,” she told The Times U.K.. “We’ve moved past that.”

Justin and Hailey appeared as if they were back together. After sharing some PDA at a religious conference, they were spotted together at LIV nightclub in Miami. Just days later, they were photographed holding hands and kissing in New York City.

After rumors of the couple’s engagement began to swirl, Justin confirmed the news on Instagram. He posted two portraits with his new fiancée and penned a lengthy caption devoted to her. “Hailey I am soooo in love with everything about you,” he wrote. “So committed to spending my life getting to know every single part of you.”

Hailey joined the love fest, tweeting, “Not sure what I did in life to deserve such happiness but I am so utterly grateful to God for giving me such an incredible person to share my life with!”

Just two months after their engagement, TMZ ran photographs of the couple walking into New York City’s Marriage Bureau. The next day, People claimed that Hailey and Justin obtained their marriage license, officially making them husband and wife.

Meanwhile, Hailey dismissed the gossip on Twitter: “I understand where the speculation is coming from, but I’m not married yet!”

The couple finally confirmed their marriage status. Justin shared an Instagram photo of him and Hailey with the caption “My wife is awesome.” The following day, Hailey changed her last name to Bieber on all of her social media accounts.

TMZ broke the news that a second wedding was imminent after the date was moved several times. The outlet acquired a photo of a save the date card, which revealed that the ceremony would take place on September 30 in South Carolina.

Mr. and Mrs. Bieber officially tied the knot…again! People disclosed the details of their ceremony, which took place at Somerset Chapel in Bluffton, South Carolina. Vows (and Tiffany & Co. wedding bands) were exchanged “as the sun set.” The star-studded guest list included Kendall Jenner, Camila Morrone, and Jaden Smith, among others.

A day later, Justin basked in wedded bliss as he shared photos from the couple’s reception.

The Biebers continued to share photos from their big day. Hailey put her Off-White by Virgil Abloh wedding dress on full display, including her elaborate train, which read “’till death do us part.” Justin wore a Celine tuxedo.

Weeks later, the newlyweds found themselves embroiled in Jelena fan drama, after Justin’s ex dropped her new single “Lose You To Love Me.” The track captured Gomez’s feelings moving on from her 10-year on-off romance with Justin and closing that chapter.

Shortly after the song came out, Hailey upset some Jelena fans when she posted an Instagram Story, where she shared that she was listening to Summer Walker’s “I’ll Kill You.” Hailey denied that this was a dig at Gomez. Commenting beneath JustJared’s Instagram post, she wrote, “please stop with this nonsense. There is no ‘response.’ This is complete BS.”

Gomez, meanwhile, told her fans on Instagram Live not to attack Hailey. “I am grateful for the response that the song is getting. I’m so grateful,” she said. “However, I do not stand for women tearing women down. And I will never, ever be by that. So please be kind to everyone. It doesn’t matter what the situation is. If you’re my fans, don’t be rude to anybody, please. Don’t go off and say things that you just feel in the moment. And just please, from me, know that that’s not my heart. My heart is only to release things that I feel that are me and that I’m proud of. And that’s all that I’ll say, yeah.”

Hailey tried to shut down feud rumors herself when she liked Gomez’s Instagram post promoting “Lose You To Love Me.” A source close to the Biebers also assured E! that things were copacetic. “It isn’t ideal for Hailey,” the insider told the outlet, “but she understands the creative process of writing music and that Justin had a past. Justin and Hailey both want Selena to be happy and in a good place and aren’t fazed by it.”

Justin rang in the decade with his new single “Yummy,” which details his sex life with Hailey.

Hailey landed the March 2020 cover of ELLE. She spoke candidly about the couple’s decision to delay their South Carolina ceremony for a year. “When we first got married, we were just figuring out our life together,” she said. “I felt like putting a wedding in the middle of all of that would be really hectic and stressful.”

Although the Biebers weren’t ready for children yet, Hailey was already thinking about parenting. Amid worldwide protests in solidarity with Black Lives Matter, Hailey discussed her outlook on motherhood. Regarding her future children, she told Vogue India, “They will be raised knowing how to treat people, why we don’t say certain things, and why we respect and acknowledge and give credit where credit is due.”

The Biebers attended their first Met Gala as husband and wife. Hailey stunned in a Saint Laurent column gown and matching wayfarer sunglasses. Justin paired his sleek suit with customized Nike sneakers.

Hailey and Justin appeared on the In Good Faith with Chelsea & Judah Smith podcast, where they discussed the challenges they faced in their first year of marriage. At the time, Justin was battling with severe bouts of depression. Hailey admitted that watching her partner struggle wasn’t easy.

“There were days where I literally was like, ‘I don’t know if he’s going to be okay.’ Something really deep down inside of me was like, ‘He’s going to get through it.’ I just didn’t know how long it was going to take. That’s what I think was the hardest thing. There’s no guarantees,” she said.

Nevertheless, Hailey was determined to stick by her husband: “Imagine abandoning somebody in the middle of the worst time of their life, potentially. I’m not that type of person,” she said.

Amid the launch of her skin care line Rhode, Hailey did an interview with WSJ Magazine. The entrepreneur shared her thoughts on having children. “I think ideally in the next couple of years we would try. But there’s a reason they call it try, right?” she said. “You don’t know how long that process is ever going to take. Definitely no kids this year; that would be a little bit hectic, I think.”

Hailey also revealed her decision to stop discussing her marriage in interviews. “It doesn’t feel worth it to me anymore,” she explained. “I try to have an open conversation with someone, and then it gets taken out of context.”

Hailey was hospitalized after having stroke-like symptoms, though she fortunately fully recovered. Her health scare left Justin reasonably shaken, with a source close to the couple telling People, “[He] is probably more traumatized than even she is. When it happened, there was a lot of panic. Justin texted everyone he knew and asked everyone to pray for her, and he never left her side. He was saying that he can’t lose her, that whatever it takes, he wanted the best medical treatment available.”

Justin later discussed the situation during one of his concerts. “You know, it’s kind of crazy how life throws you curveballs,” he said during a Denver performance. “Most of you probably know or seen the news about my wife …but she’s okay, she’s good, she’s strong. It’s been really scary. But I know for a fact that God has her in the palms of his hands, and that’s a good thing.”

Hailey accompanied her husband at the Grammys. She opted for a flowy strapless gown, which led some fans to speculate that she was hiding a growing baby bump. After RadarOnline published an Instagram post about the rumors, Hailey swiftly shut them down. “I’m not pregnant; leave me alone,” she commented.

Just months after Hailey’s hospitalization, Justin had a health scare of his own. A virus called Ramsay Hunt syndrome left half of the singer’s face paralyzed, but he eventually recovered. A source told People that although this year has been “overwhelming” for the couple, relationship-wise, “they’re unbreakable.”

During an interview with Good Morning America, Hailey shared a promising update on her husband’s health. “He is getting better every single day. He’s feeling a lot better and, obviously, it was just a very scary and random situation to happen, but he’s going to be totally okay, and I’m just grateful that he’s fine,” she said.

Ahead of the couple’s fourth anniversary, Hailey discussed her relationship with Justin in Harper’s Bazaar. “[Justin’s] still the person that I wanna be rushing back to,” she said. “I might fly somewhere and go do a job, but I can’t wait to come back and hang out. And I feel like that’s because of the effort that’s been put in on both sides. At the end of the day, like, he’s my best friend, but it still does take a lot of work to make it work.”

Hailey appeared on the Call Her Daddy podcast. In a conversation with Alexandra Cooper, Hailey insisted that she never “stole” Justin from Gomez and that it’s “not [her] character to mess with someone’s relationship.” She also confirmed that Justin had already “closed a chapter” with Gomez before he rekindled things with her in 2018.

Hailey went on to discuss her seemingly speedy engagement to Justin, which took place just two months after they began dating again. “I’m like, ‘yeah, that seemed really drastic and really crazy,’” she said. “But that was what we felt was a decision between the two of us, and it felt correct for the two of us at the time, and clearly, we were right because here we are, four years later.”

Hailey and Gomez posed for pictures together at the Academy Museum Gala, proving once and for all that there is no drama between them. A source informed People about Justin’s reaction to the news. “[He’s] very happy with the fact that they can all move on and that it can be peaceful between everyone,” the source said.

Of Hailey and Gomez’s motivation, a source told Entertainment Tonight, “Selena and Hailey were both super chill together at the Academy Museum Gala. They’ve both moved on and are happy in their own lives. They wanted to show the world that there’s no beef or bad feelings between them anymore.”

A source gave Entertainment Tonight a brief update on Hailey and Justin’s relationship as 2022 closed out. “Hailey and Justin are doing incredible as a couple,” the source said. “They are each other’s rocks. They make sure they’re there for each other for whatever each of them needs and show their support for one another.”

“They have so much love and passion in their relationship and share the same beliefs and values, so it’s easy for them to get along,” the insider continued. “They love doing pretty much everything together, especially traveling.” The couple ended their year by traveling to Aspen and celebrating the start of 2023 with Kendall and Kylie Jenner.

Justin interviewed Hailey for Vogue Australia. He asked her what her favorite part of being married is. “Mmm... My favorite thing about being married is honestly the companionship I feel we have,” she said. “Genuinely, you are my best friend in the entire world. There’s nobody I would rather spend more time with or do anything with.”

Hailey posted a birthday tribute to Justin, ignoring the ongoing TikTok discourse

about her history with Gomez. She wrote to her husband, “29 never looked so good ☺️Happy Birthday to you best friend! Words couldn’t possibly sum up all that you embody. So here’s to more joy, more travel, yummy food, adventures, more peace, fun, and most of all more love. ✨🥳”

#6722. 7 Sneaker Outfits That Put a Fresh Spin on Your Favorite Kicks

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

I’m what you might call a sneaker hoarder. But after building a capsule wardrobe I currently swear by, I’ve found that three pairs have become the foundation of most of my fall outfits: my black Adidas Superstars, which tap into the retro sneaker revival and rising noir sneaker trend I’m loving lately; my earth-toned Salomon XT-Whispers, perfect for an easy gorpcore moment; and my lilac Adidas x Wales Bonner Japan shoes, which add a subtle splash of color to neutral looks while complementing the rich hues in my autumn lineup. With those themes in mind, and inspired by some of this year’s most viral celebrity getups and standout street-style looks, I’ve gathered a quick guide to seven exciting sneaker outfits below.

Maybe you’re eager to invest in a new staple pair, or maybe you already have one of these favorites tucked away at the back of your closet. Either way, there’s plenty of fresh inspiration to copy and paste this season. Ahead, discover key pairs to shop (if you don’t already own them) and the chic pieces to wear with them.

Y2K icons like Vans and Converse are quietly making a comeback, stylishly co-signed by stars like Bella Hadid and Margot Robbie. Here, Robbie nails the cold-weather off-duty formula, swapping classic booties for black All Star high-tops to give her cozy gray polo, leather trench, and straight-leg jeans a more casual edge. A zesty red beanie completes the look, breaking up the muted tones.

By Anthropologie 100 Percent Brushed Cashmere Beanie

Even a few years after Adidas Sambas surged in popularity, the legendary indoor soccer-inspired shoe has cemented its status as a bona fide modern classic. Zoë Kravitz was recently spotted wearing a black-and-white pair while filming her new rom-com, Die Alive, in New York, styling them with flowy black trousers and a burgundy sweater and scarf. It’s a polished yet easygoing ensemble perfect for everything from bakery runs to overdue weekend catch-ups.

Saks Basic Cashmere Easy Crewneck Sweater

Hollister High-Rise Pleated Wide-Leg Pants

Or, as aforementioned, try switching up a neutral outfit with a colorful pair of kicks. As Jennifer Lawrence shows, an emerald- or forest-green sneaker feels just right for fall and winter, easily styled back to baggy jeans, a chunky turtleneck, and a navy coat.

The coolest outfits often hinge on contrast, mixing different textures, proportions, and aesthetics. This Paris Fashion Week showgoer went polished on top with a striped button-down and plaid blazer (also creating a nice pattern play) and sporty on bottom with classic track pants and chunky black Asics sneakers. An oversized denim tote introduces yet another interesting element.

Favorite Daughter The Dean Plaid Notch Lapel Blazer

Speaking of contrast, I love the idea of using a chunky sneaker to give tailored separates a cool, nonchalant feel. A relaxed-fit tuxedo blazer and drapey wide-leg trousers are a match made in street-style heaven when teamed with a boxy white tee and metallic trainers, like the celebrity-favorite New Balance 530s.

Banana Republic Factory Double Breasted Tuxedo Blazer

Lauren Ralph Lauren Pleated Stretch Jersey Wide-Leg Pant

PerfectWhiteTee Harley Cotton Boxy Crew Tee

Retro runner silhouettes, with their slim, streamlined uppers and slightly thicker soles, are another IYKYK trend to keep on your radar. Taking a cue from this Paris Fashion Week attendee, don’t be afraid to work traditionally spring-ready colors into your look: a baby-blue cable-knit sweater and soft yellow track jacket look lovely—and still feel relevant—alongside a chocolate-brown maxi skirt, matching sneakers, and a rich burgundy handbag.

Vineyard Vines Cashmere Cable Crewneck Sweater

Just like a go-to pair of low-profile kicks, a good ol’ graphic tee can be styled with just about anything in your closet—and it’s the kind of playful, sentimental piece that’ll spark joy every time you reach for it. For a unique combo, pair your favorite artist tee with sporty pieces like a nylon skirt and track jacket. An oversized briefcase-style bag adds a sleek touch.

#6719. How to Read the ‘Percy Jackson’ Books in Order of Release

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

With the new third season of Percy Jackson and the Olympians on Disney+, this is a great time to dive into Rick Riordan’s books, whether you’re reading them for the first time, rereading them, or picking up where you left off once upon a time. In true camp fashion, here’s your itinerary for reading all of the Percy Jackson books in order. (Or the Camp Half-Blood Chronicles, if you’d prefer to call them that.)

In the world that Riordan has created, demigods roam the Earth in modern times. For the most part, they’re indistinguishable from regular kids. Some of them, like Percy Jackson himself, don’t even know that their parents are literally figures from Greek and Roman mythology. Since there are so many of those centuries-old tales, Riordan has been able to turn them into a ton of young adult adventures.

In the first book, Percy Jackson learns that he’s a demigod and enrolls at Camp Half-Blood. He meets Annabeth Chase and Grover Underwood, and the three of them go on a quest to find Zeus’ lightning bolt.

This time, the teenage demigods go looking for the Golden Fleece. They’re joined by Tyson, Percy’s brother who happens to be a cyclops.

In the third book, which happens to be the basis for the current season of the Disney+ series, the group expands and they go on a rescue mission. For who exactly? You’re going to have to read to find out!

Camp Half-Blood is under attack (again), and our heroes enter the mythical Labyrinth to save it from invading forces.

In the (original) grand finale, the fight moves from Camp Half-Blood to Manhattan.

Fifteen years later, Riordan returned to his original hero with a duology about Percy’s last year in school. This means quests and college applications.

The final (unless there’s more) book is set at Halloween as he pet sits for Hecate. Should be easy!

But wait, there’s more! Riordan has dozens of other stories, long and short, in this universe. Here’s enough to get you started:

This is a series about a group of seven demigods who embark on a quest together.

This series is, like it says on the tin, about Apollo.

Nico also appears in Heroes of Olympus. This continues his story.

This series is set in the same universe, but all about Egyptian mythology.

These short stories are Percy Jackson and Kane sibling crossovers.

This trilogy is about Annabeth’s cousin and his dealings in Norse mythology.

The most recent book, co-written by Annabelle Oh, has a female protagonist: Katie Kim, daughter of Ares. It will get a sequel called The Rite of Aphrodite in March 2027.

#6718. These Cluster Lashes Deliver Lash-Extension Drama Without the Commitment

Get a lash-studio look in minutes, even if you’re a beginner.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

There’s a moment, usually somewhere between the third coat of mascara and the fourth clump, when you realize you want more than a tube can give. Cluster lashes are the answer a lot of us didn’t know we needed. Small, feathery sections of lash that sit exactly where you want drama, with none of the commitment of full extensions or the fuss of a traditional strip, cluster lashes deliver custom volume with a lightweight, natural-looking finish that blends seamlessly with your own lashes.

Cluster lashes give you the dimension of a lash-studio set in about five minutes, and they’re forgiving enough for beginners (yay!). Want a wispy cat-eye flick at the outer corners? Done. A fuller, doll-like fringe across the lid? You got it. Because you control the placement, the result looks like your lashes on their best day, and nothing like falsies (which is perfectly cool if that’s your vibe).

But not all clusters are created equal. The right pair is lightweight, flexible, and nearly invisible at the base, and it stays put from morning coffee to last call.

Below, we’ve rounded up some of the best cluster lashes to give your eyes as much glamour and definition as you want.

#6715. Blade Runner 2099 Ends on a Touching Note About Empathy

Blade Runner 2099 might not be getting a second season, but the show’s final episode provided a tidy ending for everyone involved. The final episode sees the show’s main characters, Cora and Olwen (played by Hunter Schafer and Michelle Yeoh, respectively) reaching the Tyrell Launch Complex on a quest to find out where Tyrell’s memory is located.

The journey isn’t without its twists and turns: One person who is the key to obtaining Tyrell’s memory winds up dead, and Olwen’s future hangs in the balance as she gets closer to the end of her lifespan. Cora’s life as a Replicant also has a few complications of its own, too.

Here’s what happened in the season finale of Blade Runner 2099.

Olwen and Cora spend a large portion of the final episode searching for Tyrell’s memory at the Tyrell Launch Complex, which belongs to replicant creator Eldon Tyrell and serves as documentation about Nexus-1 replicants Julius and Helena.

Inside the complex, Olwen and Cora split up. Olwen finds Clotilde and they talk about how Julius killed her. When Clotilde suffers a fall, Olwen doesn’t allow her to die again; in exchange for saving her, Clotilde shares where Julius is within the complex.

Meanwhile, Julius discovers Cora’s whereabouts in the complex. Shortly after, Julius and Cora fight, and he inflicts a serious stab wound on her. He also destroys Tyrell’s memory and insists that he’s the only one who can extend Olwen’s life, but Olwen finds the pair, jumps in, and kills Julius with a gunshot.

Olwen spends the majority of the series aware that she’s at the end of her lifespan. As a Replicant, she was given a fixed limit to how long she can live and has accepted her fate—but she’s offered a second chance at life by the end of the series.

After saving Clotilde, she later reveals that she saw Tyrell’s memory before he died and knows how to re-create Olwen as a Nexus-1. Clotilde re-creates Olwen in exchange for Olwen having saved Clotilde’s life. By the end of the episode, Olwen emerges from a birthing bag, and she later leaves Earth on a spaceship.

The series ends with Cora and Greta understanding each other. Cora, a human posing as a Replicant, heads back to LAPD and is given a Replicant test by Greta to confirm her identity, but this time, Cora turns the questions on Greta and asks about her life.

The pair talks about Greta’s doll, leading Cora to come to the realization that the girl is still capable of understanding what it means to love and be loved. Cora becomes emotional at the realization, leading Greta to comment that Cora has high levels of empathy. This means Cora technically fails the Replicant test, but Greta allows her to continue hiding her identity and her work as a Replicant.
