#6697. The 20 Most Iconic Colognes of All Time, According to GQ in 2026

The first notes of The Beach Boys’ God Only Knows are enough to understand you're listening to a masterpiece. Well, as a longtime fragrance editor, I get a similar feeling when I smell Guerlain’s iconic Vétiver scent (or, more recently, Penhaligon’s Halfeti). These fragrances smelled exceptional when they debuted, still do today, probably still will a hundred years from now. And while fragrance, like any art form, has its trends, the truly great scents (and songs) have a way of deflecting them and singing on.

All of this is why we wanted to take a moment to celebrate not only the colognes our editors are wearing today, but also the fragrances that have been enjoyed by millions of GQ readers over the years. To do so, I dug through the history of men’s fragrance to put together a list of some of the most iconic colognes of all time. Scents that defined an era, changed the course of perfumery, or smelled so good that folks never stopped wearing them.

Our criteria came down to three things: legacy, or how a fragrance helped shape the scent landscape; longevity, meaning it has remained relevant or recognizable; and wearability, because the best classics should (mostly) still smell great today, not just serve as nostalgic reminders of another time. You might love some more than others, but all have earned their place in the fragrance pantheon. Here are 20 of the most popular, influential, and enduring colognes ever made, and exactly why they’ve lasted.

Is there a more classic cologne than Acqua di Parma’s Colonia? Launched in 1916, this timeless citrus is the original Italian splash, and it would be criminal not to include it on a list of this caliber. That crisp, bright citrus opening is unmistakable, but there’s so much more going on beneath the surface as it dries down, with the supporting notes weaving in and out without ever stealing the show. Herbaceous hints of lavender and rosemary add herbal hues, while earthy vetiver and patchouli mingle with the smooth warmth of sandalwood as it settles on the skin. This is one of the great daily drivers, if not the very archetype of what an everyday cologne could or should be. It’s a 10/10 blend of citrus, aromatics, and woods, and more than a century on, still smells as sophisticated as ever.

I’ve written about Guerlain Vétiver so many times, you’d think I’d have run out of ways to sing its praises. But I could go forever. Few fragrances have done more to establish vetiver as a leading note, and since its 1959 debut, this masterpiece has remained every bit as relevant and wearable. It beautifully captures vetiver’s fresher, greener side, rather than leaning into its smoky, earthy character. There’s a crispness, with bright citrus, a touch of spice, and grassy vetiver balanced by subtle tobacco and woods, like the first cool, earthy dew on a spring morning. Elegant and confident, it oozes the kind of sophistication I imagine old-school Hollywood leading men appreciating, yet it wears just as well today, whether you're suited up or simply going about your day.

Ask me to name the most influential luxury fragrance of the 21st century, and there’s a good chance I’d say Aventus (notice, I said “luxury”). I’ve seen firsthand just how many guys made their first foray into high-end fragrance with this icon, and while I now often gravitate toward some of the deeper cuts in Creed’s storied catalog, every time I wear Aventus, the compliments roll in. Since its 2010 debut, it has inspired countless imitators and the inevitable fragrance fatigue that comes with being a phenomenon, yet I don’t think it’s lost an ounce of its appeal. To me, it's the ultimate wedding fragrance, a perfect blend of bright citrus and pineapple, aromatic freshness, and rich, smoky woods that’s refined without being fussy. The defining fragrance of the 2010s? I’d make that argument any day. Aventus will always have a place on my shelf.

No. 89 is one of the great British gentlemen’s fragrances, and few colognes capture Bond-like elegance quite like it. And it tracks: Introduced in 1951, No. 89 counted James Bond creator Ian Fleming among its famous admirers, while Cary Grant and Sir Alec Guinness were reportedly devotees as well. The scent itself is traditional in the best way, a classically composed fougère with crisp, almost barbershop-like citrus and lavender freshness, subtle florals, warming spice, and a smooth, woody base of vetiver and oakmoss. Aromatic, gently spicy, and impeccably polished, it’s the olfactory equivalent of a beautifully tailored Savile Row suit. More than seven decades on, No. 89 is proof that class and character never go out of style.

Back in the ’90s, CK One was everywhere. From Wall Street execs to flannel-wearing creatives; its intoxicating freshness just captured the zeitgeist. It was also one of the first mainstream fragrances marketed as unisex. (Almost an afterthought for fragrance fans today, it was a fairly novel concept then.) And while countless fragrances have followed in its footsteps, this citrusy, green, woodsy, lightly sweet profile still more than holds up. A surprising amount is going on here, too: a bright medley of citrus and tropical fruits, a floral heart with hints of spice, and a clean, musky base of cedar, sandalwood, and amber. It’s an icon that helped define a generation, and it works just as well day or night, on pretty much anyone. And perhaps most impressively, after all these years, CK One still smells unmistakably like CK One.

Much like his clothing, Ralph Lauren’s original Polo fragrance has left an indelible mark on American style. Launched in 1978, the unmistakable green-bottled cologne helped define what a classic masculine fragrance could smell like: sophisticated, confident, and unapologetically aromatic. And while nearly five decades have passed, there’s still nothing quite like it. That signature pine-heavy character is front and center, surrounded by a rich blend of herb and spices with bergamot adding brightness and leather lending rugged, earthy depth. It’s bold, woody, green, and distinctly old-school, but that’s precisely its charm. It’s undergone reformulations over the years, and its assertive character won’t appeal to everyone, but there’s no denying its place in the fragrance hall of fame. Some colognes come and go, but Polo isn’t going anywhere.

When I spotted that frosted bottle in my dad’s medicine cabinet and wore it for the first time, I had no idea that this scent would spark a lifelong fragrance obsession. Armani’s 1996 icon, Acqua di Giò, has been worn by millions, referenced endlessly, and spawned countless flankers, but the original remains one of those rare mass-appeal designer colognes that have stood the test of time. Why? Because it’s just so wearable. Long before the niche fragrance boom, when designer colognes dominated department store counters, Acqua di Giò caught on like wildfire, appealing to just about every kind of guy, whether for the office or date night. At its core is a beautifully nuanced blend of citrus, fresh marine accords, and soft florals, grounded by a musky amber base. On skin, it’s breezy, clean, and subtly woody, with an oceanic quality. If you’ve never rocked it or haven’t reached for it in years, do yourself a favor and revisit this classic.

Imitation is the highest form of flattery, and so many colognes still try to smell like Cool Water. Launched in 1988, it became a full-blown phenomenon in the ’90s, and for a generation of guys, it was practically a rite of passage. Along with CK One, it was non-negotiable during my formative years, and even then, before I’d developed my own distinct taste, I knew I didn’t want to smell like everyone else. But there was something about this intoxicating green aquatic I just couldn’t resist. Its blend of lavender, mint, rosemary, and neroli nodded to classic gentleman’s fougères, but Cool Water took that aromatic DNA somewhere new—a singular scent that’s herbal and richly marine, softened by a woody, musky warmth. While it might smell dated to some, there’s no denying its place among the most iconic fragrances of all time, and I’d argue it still smells amazing on the right dude, especially on a spring or summer day.

Terre d’Hermès is (probably) my favorite designer fragrance of all time. When Jean-Claude Ellena created it back in 2006, he delivered something entirely different from the crowd-pleasing designer colognes of the day. There’s an earthiness to it that I find irresistible, a marriage of citrus, dry woods, spice, and that unmistakable flinty, mineral character that gives it so much personality. It’s sophisticated without being stuffy, with a gorgeous, almost boozy warmth that makes it feel sultry and downright sexy. I particularly love wearing it in colder weather, when those warm, woody facets really come alive, but I can reach for it almost anytime. Plenty of designer colognes are simply pleasant, but Terre d’Hermès is distinctive and endlessly wearable.

Few colognes in modern history have generated as much conversation, inspired as many memes, or become quite as zeitgeist-y as Dior Sauvage. Love it or roll your eyes at its popularity, its influence on modern men’s perfumery is undeniable. Since its debut in 2015, Sauvage has become one of the world’s bestselling fragrances, at times even claiming the number-one spot. And it’s easy to see why. That unmistakable blend of citrus, spice, and all things ambery nice is crisp and fresh, with a warm, woody drydown that’s oh so smooth. It’s a crowd-pleaser in the truest sense, versatile enough to wear just about anywhere, though I think it really shines after dark. Sauvage has since spawned an entire family of flankers, but the original EDT started it all.

You've smelled Bleu de Chanel on plenty of guys. A beautifully composed citrus-spice-wood classic, it works just as well with a navy suit as it does with a white tee and jeans. Note: When Chanel's legendary perfumer Jacques Polge introduced it in 2010, he didn't just create another handsome, crowd-pleasing cologne; he helped define the modern blue fragrance, a fresh, clean, citrusy, and woody genre that's become a mainstay. Sixteen years on, the original remains one of the most approachable and endlessly versatile colognes ever. Its bright citrus opening gives way to an aromatic blend of cool mint, warming spices, and subtle florals, all grounded by a rich, woody base with touches of earthy vetiver, patchouli, and resinous warmth. There really is only one Bleu.

This was a tough call. Ask any scent head about Tom Ford's fragrances, and they’ll surely bring up Neroli Portofino and Tobacco Vanille, both arguably just as iconic (and good) as this one. But if you polled fans on the most influential Tom Ford fragrances, I think Oud Wood would edge out the competition. Launched in 2007, it helped introduce Oud to a broader Western audience, making the traditionally intense ingredient feel approachable. It’s elegant and beautifully blended, with a smoky, almost creamy heart of oud, sandalwood, and rosewood, accented by subtle spice and the warmth of vanilla and amber. It’s heady yet easy to wear, with an addictive richness that never wears thin.

I’ve worn pretty much every Le Labo under the sun, and while my personal favorites lean toward the eucalyptus and vetiver side of the house, there’s simply no telling the story of modern perfumery without Santal 33. Love it or find it utterly overplayed, you’ve smelled it. Since its 2011 debut, it’s wafted through elevators, the backseats of cabs, countless weddings and date nights, and just about everywhere in between. More importantly, it helped bring sandalwood into the mainstream, and became a reference point for an entire genre of warm, woody fragrances. But before everyone started rocking it, Santal 33 was, and still is, a brilliant composition: creamy sandalwood, dry cedar, cardamom spice, and leathery warmth. It’s a niche legend that has suffered from its own success, yes, but give it another ten years, and don’t be surprised if the very fragrance many got tired of smelling becomes cool all over again.

If you’re remotely a scent head, chances are you dig at least one, if not several, of Parfums de Marly’s fragrances (my personal favorite is the oud-praline magic that is Haltane). But if I had to sum up the house in one word, it would be regal. It does refined, layered elegance exceptionally well, and while some folks might champion Althaïr or Castley, to my nose, the fougère-forward Layton best epitomizes the house. Launched in 2016, it’s one of the finest fougères I’ve ever smelled, rich yet airy, with classic lavender reimagined alongside crisp apple, bright citrus, cardamom, woods, and a healthy dose of vanilla. It’s practically the template for what a modern fougère aspires to be, and its enduring popularity suggests plenty of fragrance fans would agree. It doesn’t hurt that the matte blue bottle with its signature silver dome is so flauntworthy.

Few fragrances in recent times have had the same cultural impact as Baccarat Rouge 540, becoming a signature scent for everyone from socialites to fragrance obsessives. Yes, it's polarizing, and some might argue its ubiquity has stripped it of some of its magic. But it's become a modern classic for a reason, starting with the masterful nose behind it: Francis Kurkdjian, founder of his namesake maison. Launched in 2015, it's a luminous floral amber, with saffron lending spice, jasmine bringing a soft warmth, and a generous dose of amberwood creating that glowing aura. Sultry, heady, and diffusive, it's a straight-up unisex phenomenon that smells a little different on everyone who wears it, yet still announces itself before you even enter a room. Whether you're a devotee or a detractor, there's no denying it's left its mark.

It came down to Bal d’Afrique and Gypsy Water, though plenty of Byredo fragrances could have earned a spot here. But I’m the editor, and I’m going with my all-time favorite from one of the houses that helped bring niche perfumery into the mainstream. Launched in 2008, Gypsy Water was one of the first niche fragrances I ever fell for, and it holds such a special place in my heart that I still wear it regularly. There’s something almost holy about its airy blend of incense, soft woods, bright lemon, and a whisper of vanilla and amber, all coming together with that subtle yet magnetic, almost skin-scent quality Byredo does so well. Artsy, chic, and easy to love, this woody aromatic never tries too hard to make an impression yet often leaves a lasting one.

Since its 2015 debut, Xerjoff’s Naxos has achieved near-mythic status, winning over everyone from fragrance-obsessed TikTokers to yacht-owning gentlemen with decidedly expensive taste. And I get it. This is an almost impossibly well-blended composition that somehow manages to be both decadent and incredibly wearable, making it a great place to start if you’re looking to venture into niche perfumery. On paper, the combination of tobacco, aromatic lavender, fruity freshness, and a prominent honey accord sounds like it shouldn’t quite work, but man, does it ever. That rich sweetness is a defining feature, especially in the opening, before the herbal, earthy tones and a hefty dose of vanilla come together in a downright sumptuous yet crisp way. The hype may have quieted down since its social media heyday, but Naxos deserves its flowers as a modern niche standout.

Halfeti has become a signature of mine, though I’m admittedly a little late to the game, having received my first bottle as a birthday gift last year, well after it had achieved iconic status in scent circles. But better late than never, especially when a fragrance is this good. Named after a Turkish town known for its roses, it leans into a rich, heady floral style, layered with citrus, herbs, and spice. Bergamot and grapefruit lift the opening before saffron and cardamom give way to a deep base of leather, oud, cedar, sandalwood, and amber. It’s a lot, but it all hangs together gorgeously, creating a woody, spicy, aromatic scent that’s elegant and sexy, perfect for special occasions or date nights. But I’d happily wear it just about anywhere, including when I’m not going anywhere at all.

Musc Ravageur embodies the risk-taking spirit of niche perfumery. Launched in 2000, it helped redefine what a modern musk fragrance could be at a time when fresh, clean fragrances dominated the men’s market. More than a quarter century later, it remains a cult favorite, thanks in no small part to its warm, almost funky (in a good way) richness. It opens with herbal lavender, spicy coriander, and a hint of bright citrus before melting into clove and rose, all resting on a base of musk, vanilla, tonka, amber, and patchouli. A benchmark for animalic musks, it’s an alluring, sultry, and unapologetically sensual aroma, equally suited to being a signature scent or an olfactory companion for wherever the evening leads.

Amouage has dropped so many olfactory bangers over the years that choosing just one for this list wasn't easy. The Omani house has built its reputation on rich, complex compositions that keep you coming back to sniff your pulse points, and while I could make a case for several of its fragrances, Reflection Man feels quintessentially Amouage. Launched in 2007, it’s become a luxury niche benchmark, proving that a floral-forward fragrance can be every bit as rich and sophisticated as its woody or spicy counterparts. It opens with an herbaceous blend of rosemary, berries, and orange before orris, jasmine, and ylang-ylang bring a lush, earthy depth, all grounded by a smooth base of vetiver, patchouli, and sandalwood. The result is a layered floral-woody scent that’s distinctive without being difficult to wear, and versatile enough for everything from a boardroom to a black-tie gala. Pricey, yes, but worth every spritz.

#6694. 8 Best Nose Hair Trimmers GQ's Editors Have Tested in 2026

Perhaps it never occurred to you that you might need a list of the best nose hair trimmers until this very moment. Fear not—reasonably priced, speedily shipped (thanks, Amazon), simple-to-use options populate this list of our favorites. Meaning—whenever you have a general facial hair emergency that calls for rogue hair removal, you need not add an ear hair trimmer or brow trimmer to your cart. You need only think back to this moment and thank your lucky stars for choosing a gadget to cut hair—wherever it may appear.

Similar to other battery-operated beard trimmers, back shavers, razors, hair clippers, or body hair trimmers, these trimmers are ergonomic and designed with durability in mind. You can use many of them on wet or dry hair to trim nose hair, and like other electric razors you may be familiar with, most can actually be used for nose & ears (and also as eyebrow trimmers).

If you’re looking for a quick, pain-free way to get rid of unwanted nose whiskers, Philips Norelco’s Nose Trimmer Series 5000 can absolutely handle that. But this versatile grooming device also covers most hair-detailing needs with precision and gusto. Add in the affordable price, and that’s why it stands as our pick for the best nose hair trimmer, bar none.

The 5000 Series includes three types of trimmers: a linear-style trimmer, plus the rotary and detail attachments. Depending on your preference, you can use either the linear or rotary trimmer for both nose and ear hairs as well as working as an eyebrow trimmer. Personally, I appreciate the size of the cylinder on the rotary trimmer, which is wider than most and is able to capture overgrown hairs better than any other trimmer I tested. The linear trimmer works great at managing eyebrows (thanks to two different guard lengths) and tending to fuzzy ear canals. Perhaps the unsung hero of the kit is the straight-edge detail trimmer, which is more than capable of daily touch-ups. Think sideburns, mustaches, or even edging beards in a pinch.

The 5000 Series is battery-powered, cordless, easy to wash with a rinse in the bathroom sink, and you already know that anything made from Philips Norelco has longevity. If what you’re after is a no-frills device to manage most facial hair needs, consider this trimmer twenty bucks well spent. Adam Hurly

True to its name, Wahl’s Micro Groomsman is easily the smallest nose hair trimmer we recommend—but don’t let its diminutive size fool you. This is a perfectly pared-down trimmer that’s great for home use, and even better for travel. Heck, it’s so small you might forget you stashed it in your dopp kit.

The Micro Groomsman offers two interchangeable heads, including a rotary trimmer for nose and ears, and a linear trimmer for detailed work. You can also attach an eyebrow guard to manage any overgrown caterpillars up top. The compact design feels great in the hand, and the stainless steel blades are cinch to clean. Even if you have a primary grooming device you love, we readily recommend the Wahl Micro Groomsman as a go-to backup option or a permanent accessory in your travel kit. Adam Hurly

Got a partner who absolutely hates when you leave trimmed hair in the sink? This one’s for you. An upgrade from the standard Panasonic ER-GN30 (shown in the video), this trimmer offers everything you’d want from a dedicated nose hair trimmer, but what sets it apart is a vacuum system that sucks up clippings in an instant. Easy clean-up, yes, but the vacuum system also helps prevent bacteria build-up. We call that a win-win.

Otherwise, this tool is fairly minimal with no fancy attachments. You get a rotary trimmer, and that’s all. Granted, it’s a powerful one that’s able to mow down the coarsest strands. All told, the GN70-K is best for the person who just needs a dependable nose and ear trimmer that will save them from the mess. Adam Hurly

A whole grooming kit is excessive if all you need to tackle is the occasional nose hair. But if you’re also maintaining a beard, tidying body hair, or cutting your own hair, Philips’ 9000 starts to make much more sense. The current kit has 23 tools and accessories, including a nose and ear head, with a stainless-steel handle and rubber grip. Philips rates it for up to six hours between charges.

Long-term testing notes: Philips' previous iteration of the Multigroom 9000 was my go-to grooming tool. Alas, I only realized how much I relied on its nose hair shaving attachment after I lost the thing. Now there's a new (albeit broadly similar) update of the same device, and I'm back to trimming as much hair as humanly possible with one device. My advice for anyone else who picks up this trimmer? Be sure to check every piece when packing up after a trip. Adam Hurly

My nose hairs have become alarmingly ambitious with age, which is unfortunate given how little enthusiasm I have for plucking, waxing, or the local Turkish barber’s fire tricks. The Weed Whacker 3.0 Pro, thankfully, gets the job done with considerably less drama. Alongside its rotary nose and ear head, there’s a separate eyebrow blade with a 2mm comb. USB-C charging saves another proprietary cable, and the travel lock stops it switching on in your bag.

Long-term testing notes: During my review testing, the rotary head cut through my thick, wiry hairs quickly, without the pulling and sneezing fits I’ve experienced with cheaper trimmers. It was still ticklish, but the compact head was easy to maneuver around my nostrils and ears. The LED helped in my dimly lit bathroom, showing hairs I might otherwise have missed. I also liked the solid-feeling matte-black handle and how easily it rinsed clean. The claimed hour of runtime held up in testing, too. It’s the best dedicated nose trimmer I’ve used. Esat Dedezade

If you have a beard and your grooming routine requires a more exacting tool, ConairMan’s Nose and Ear Trimmer kit will serve you well. I think of this more as a mustache and beard-line tidier-upper, as opposed to a nose hair trimmer.

The tool has a rotary blade that makes quick work of nostril hairs. But its two-faced trimming and shaving head is its best calling card—you get a straight-edge detailer on one side, and a shaver on the other. If you’re looking for a tool that can truly tend to the lower half of your face, ConairMan offers an excellent package that’ll get the job done. Adam Hurly

Philips’ 7000 costs less than the 9000 above and still covers far more than nose hair. The US kit includes 22 tools and accessories, with a precision comb for short facial hair and heads for wider grooming jobs. Finding somewhere to keep all those small parts is part of the deal.

My main complaint, as always, is the proprietary charging connection. The supplied cable plugs into USB-A at the other end, but you still need Philips’ cable to charge the trimmer. Braun, Remington, and other brands are just as guilty of this, by the way.

Long-term testing notes: In my three-week test of the 7000, its nose and ear attachment cut comfortably without pulling, snagging, or irritation. It was much less unpleasant than plucking. The wider kit also earned its place on my chest and underarms, so the extra attachments felt useful. Esat Dedezade

If you already have a Panasonic MultiShape, the nose-trimming head gives its rechargeable handle another job. It swaps with the system’s beard trimmer, shaver, and toothbrush heads, so there’s one less complete gadget to pack when you travel.

For a new buyer, the lithium-ion handle and nose head cost around $81.99 together. The $21.99 attachment won’t work on its own, and the other heads cost extra. That makes the MultiShape easier to justify if you’ll use more of the system. For nose hair alone, a dedicated trimmer is a simpler buy.

Long-term testing notes: During several weeks with a broader MultiShape kit, I found the nose head cleared unwanted hair without pain or friction. The lightweight handle was comfortable, and switching from trimming to shaving or brushing his teeth took little effort. That ease of moving between jobs made the system particularly useful for travel. The handle and nose head linked here are sold separately from the other attachments he used. Owen Gough

While many nose hair trimmers tout motors with serious RPMs and boast about their long battery life, the truth is, you won’t be using it much—maybe once every couple weeks for a mere 5 or 10 seconds. But that’s no excuse to skimp on them, either. Here are a few things to consider:

We’ve tested all manner of nose hair trimmers for this guide, from simple battery-powered models to grooming kits with interchangeable heads. We look at how comfortably they cut, whether they pull or miss hairs, and how easy they are to handle and clean. Extra attachments need to be useful, and we consider battery life and charging alongside performance.

The guide draws on Adam Hurly’s years of grooming reviews and Ian Burke’s experience of living with the Zorami trimmer long after receiving it as a joke gift. My additions bring in testing for British GQ, alongside Owen Gough’s assessment of the MultiShape system. The entries identify whose findings support each recommendation. You can read more about our editorial standards at About GQ.

The best men’s nose hair trimmer is Philips Norelco’s Nose Trimmer 5000 Series, according to GQ. The powerful device offers dual trimming options (for internal and peripheral nostril hairs) and also functions as a facial hair, eyebrow, and ear hair trimmer.

Whether you are using a rotary head or a linear head, nose hair trimmers are built with a protective guard, which ensures the blade doesn’t come in direct contact with the skin. The protective guard also helps you get a clean cut so the device doesn’t pull or pluck at your nose hairs. Generally speaking, nose hair trimmers do not hurt.

Not only can nose hair trimmers be washed, but it is important to disinfect them between use, first by flushing out stray hair clippings. Let them dry before storing, too, to avoid rust and bacterial proliferation between each use. It’s a good idea to clean the device again before using it, since it is going into your nose, after all.

Do you need a nose hair trimmer, and how often should you use it?

If visible nose hairs bother you, a trimmer is a simple way to keep them tidy. There’s no fixed schedule to follow – a quick trim when hairs start showing is usually all that’s needed. Concentrate on those visible hairs around the nostril opening, rather than trying to remove everything inside. Ear hair and eyebrows can be tidied as needed, using the appropriate attachment and comb.

To trim your nose hair, simply stick the trimming tip of the device in your nostril and apply a gentle circular motion. Here are a few more tips to keep in mind, according to Simone:

The cleaning systems for nose hair trimmers vary. “Some nose hair trimmers are waterproof, so it’s easy to just rinse under water, if you are the sole user, and just let it air dry,” says Simone. If you share the device with somebody else, then it’s going to be all the more important that you disinfect between uses (ideally before and after each trim). “Make sure you have tapped out the hair trapped inside to avoid clogging,” adds Simone. “For a disinfectant, I would use a sterilizing alcohol pad or barber’s disinfectant, and just wipe the tool gently dry.”

Simone (who is professionally mononymous) is a celebrity men’s groomer. Her A-List clients include Mahershala Ali, Ramy Youssef, Jason Schwartzman, John Magaro, and Brandon Perea.

Adam Hurly has been covering men's grooming for GQ since 2016. He also writes about travel. Adam is a Sioux Falls, SD native, University of Kansas graduate, former New Yorker, and current expat in Berlin. His favorite season is Oscar Season, his favorite book is Dinner At the Homesick Restaurant, and he is a proud superfan of Survivor.

#6688. The Art of Being Veruschka: Fashion Pays Tribute to a Legend

“There is and will always be only one Veruschka,” Richard Avedon wrote in the May 1972 issue of Vogue in an impassioned tribute to his muse. More than half a century later, his words still ring true. Since news broke of the German supermodel’s death at the age of 87, generations of fashion’s image-makers, designers, and models have been reflecting on her immeasurable influence.

A protagonist of some of the most fantastical fashion editorials ever committed to the pages of Vogue and a prolific cover star, Veruschka helped expand the possibilities of what a model could be. Her body was her artistic medium, her imagination as instrumental to the making of a photograph as that of the person behind the lens. Her creative curiosity would endure long after her heyday in the 1960s and ’70s, finding expression in her experiments with body painting and camouflage, her performance art, and her occasional, much-feted returns to the runway.

For those who knew and worked with Veruschka, though, there was as much to marvel at away from the camera: her generosity of spirit, quiet eccentricities, intellectual curiosity, and her sense of humor. In the tributes shared with Vogue below, fellow models recall their first encounters with a woman whose photographs once adorned their bedroom walls, while designers and photographers reflect on the creative communion they shared with her—and how rare a model like Veruschka is today.

I am so sad to hear the news of Veruschka’s passing. When I started modeling in 1966, she was one of my idols—she was so beautiful and exotic. I had always wanted to meet her, and then in the 1980s, while I was appearing on Broadway in the musical My One and Only, a knock came on my dressing room door after the performance. I opened it and, to my surprise and huge delight, Veruschka was standing there. It was so lovely to finally meet her, and she was absolutely divine, and we chatted for ages. She certainly is one of the greatest of all models. RIP, beautiful lady.

Veruschka was an extraordinary artist that I was blessed to know. Thank you, Vera…

I remember sitting in the library at Saint Martins, transfixed by the camouflage photographs in Veruschka: Trans-Figurations, the book she made with Holger Trülzsch. Years later, when I had the chance to work with her, I knew I wanted her to close my spring/summer 2011 show. We flew her in from Berlin and made her a bespoke dress for the finale. What I loved about working with her was how open she was to the ideas behind the collection and how wholeheartedly she embraced them. She was 71 and absolutely majestic on the runway. She had a presence unlike anyone else. A creative powerhouse and a true original.

Being a model is an art form, and she was one of the people who truly mastered it. She had both creativity and respect for what she was doing. When we worked with her, she was incredibly impressive. There was an energy, a grace, and a lasting presence that you don’t see so often today. The lifespan of a model’s career seems to have become so much shorter. I sometimes wonder if, in the way the industry works today, we’re leaving less room for someone to become truly iconic.

When I was a young girl, my bedroom walls were covered in photographs of Veruschka. To me, she looked like she had stepped out of another world—a dream I looked up to, one of the incredible women who had paved the way before me. I used to lie in bed, staring at those pictures, wondering what it would be like actually to know someone like her. Years later, that dream literally knocked on my door.

We ended up living together at our agent Zoli’s house on East 63rd Street. For months, after a long day’s work, we would settle in for the evening with our sketchbooks. I can still see her drawing her little cats—curled up, stretching out, or peeking from the corners of the pages—while I sketched beside her. We didn’t need to say much; the quiet between us always felt so full.

Veruschka was always an enigma to me, a woman with a poetic soul and a nature I could never quite put a name to. She had a wicked little smile and a twinkle in her eye when she’d look up from her drawing, as if she knew a secret about the world she might someday share. Her voice was low and sensuous, and she was always kinder than anyone needed to be.

Sometimes, while watching her sketch, I would think back to that little girl staring at the pictures on her wall. I wished I could tell her that one day she would be sitting right beside her icon, and that Veruschka would be her friend. I have always loved Veruschka, and I always will. Knowing she taught me that dreams really do come true. She was mine.

Verushka was really the first super supermodel, she was literally larger than life. I grew up with her images and like every other girl I wanted to be and look like her.

She influenced fashion in an enormous way by having that statuesque beauty but also being an incredible performer, She was not afraid of breaking boundaries creating new visions with other artists like especially all the body paint images which are now more than ever a great inspiration. May she rest in peace.

#6687. Speculoos Scents Are the Cozy Perfume Trend Taking Over Winter

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Just as fragrance notes make up a perfume’s composition, fragrance families can also encompass their own layers of niche categories. Case in point: speculoos scents. Consider this emerging perfume profile an offspring of the beloved gourmand family. Gourmands home in on different kinds of sweet notes, whether it’s fruity, creamy, or, in the case of speculoos, reminiscent of freshly baked treats. Jeniece Trizzino, VP of Innovation and Physical Product at Scentbird and Drift, explains why this emerging category feels so seasonally appropriate and how to add them to your current scent library.

Like any gourmand, the note is built as an accord, a blend of materials your nose reads as one smell. “Ethyl maltol gives the burnt sugar. Vanillin gives the vanilla. Tonka bean adds a soft, hay-like warmth. The spices do the rest. Woods, patchouli, or musk sit underneath, so it smells like a perfume and not a dessert,” she notes.

Much like traditional gourmands, speculoos are built on familiar sweet cues: vanilla, caramel, chocolate, cream, and fruit. “Think cocoa with caramel and condensed milk. It’s creamy and comforting, and it leans toward dessert,” says Trizzino. A speculoos scent is sweet too, but it’s more spice-forward and toasted. The main difference is that speculoos scents lead with cinnamon, nutmeg, and cardamom. The addition of burnt sugar and browned butter allows it to smell baked, not syrupy. “You still get the comfort, with more going on. It can add complexity, not just more sweetness,” she adds.

Speculoos scents are carving out a place in fragrance as traditional gourmands evolve. “The very literal vanilla, caramel, and dessert scents can feel predictable now,” Trizzino points out. “The move is toward bitter, savory, and smoky contrasts. Traditional gourmands aren’t going away, but speculoos is one way to keep the comfort and add depth.”

Key notes: speculoos (cinnamon, cardamom), vanilla, sandalwood

Mancera’s Amore Caffè moves speculoos to the café. It’s built like an affogato. Black coffee and amaretto open it, then vanilla ice cream with a hint of speculoos, over vanilla, brown sugar, and grey amber.

Key notes: black coffee, amaretto liquor, vanilla ice cream, speculoos, vanilla pods, brown sugar, grey amber

Across its library, Kayali has nailed gourmand scents. What puts Freedom Musk Latte in this new category is its creamy, cozy, latte-inspired notes of cinnamon, chocolate musk, and café latte, punctuated with a burst of mandarin and pink pepper.

Key Notes: bergamot, café latte, chocolate musk

Key notes: cinnamon, coffee, cocoa, vanilla, musk

Jousset Parfums’ Mascarpone & Speculoos goes all the way to dessert. Mascarpone, butter, and whiskey open it. Coffee, cinnamon, cream and caramel follow, then vanilla, sugar and biscuit. It’s creamy, rich and unapologetically gourmand.

Key notes: butter, mascarpone, whisky, black coffee, caramel, cinnamon, cream, biscuit, sugar, vanilla

What makes these scents so seasonally appropriate is that winter is when people reach for scents that feel warm, comforting, and a little enveloping. Speculoos does all three. “The baking spices bring warmth. The toasted sugar and biscuit notes bring a familiar kind of comfort. It has the appeal of a favorite winter drink or something baking at home, but you can wear it,” says Trizzino.

Winter perfume has a reputation for being sweet and heavy. In contrast, speculoos is sweet, but the spice keeps it from being just sugar. “You get the comfort without the weight. And it travels. A recognizable food scent works in candles, diffusers, body care, and perfume, and it makes an easy gift.”

Although there’s already a lot of depth, speculoos scents are also layerable. “I actually think layering is where speculoos gets really interesting. It has enough warmth and sweetness to work as a base, but it’s also distinctive enough to change the personality of another fragrance.”

Trizzino says the best pairings depend on what you want to bring out of it:

For darker layering: woods and amber add depth. Cedar, sandalwood, amber, and vetiver balance the sweetness and make speculoos feel warmer and more sophisticated. Smoke, rum, and leather take it darker still. Tobacco, incense, rum, and leather add depth and make it a rich, comforting gourmand.

For richer layering: coffee and other gourmand notes lean into the dessert quality. Vanilla, tonka, chocolate, milky notes, and coffee make it indulgent. Baked fruit makes it seasonal. Apple, pear, or dates sit naturally with the toffee, vanilla, and baking spices of a winter gourmand.

For lighter layering: florals soften the spice. Jasmine, rose, and orange blossom add a more unexpected dimension and keep it from feeling too heavy. Citrus adds contrast. Bergamot, mandarin, and orange cut through the sweetness and give the scent a brighter opening. And a soft skin musk makes it more intimate. It can take speculoos from something that smells like a bakery to something warm and personal on skin.

Trizzino’s layering advice is to start with the fragrance you want to be most prominent and build around it. “You don’t need two scents that smell exactly alike. Sometimes the best combinations come from contrast. A simple way to start is with a familiar base like vanilla or sandalwood, then add one contrasting accent: fruit for a brighter blend, smoke or rum for a darker one. You can layer across formats too, with a perfume over a bodycare product,” she explains.

Speculoos doesn’t have to smell like dessert. It can smell like spice, warmth, woods, or skin. That’s what makes it fun to wear.

#6686. Maple Bourbon Chicken With Brussels Sprouts

If you, like most people, hear the term “bourbon chicken” and have no idea what it is, let me call it by its more common name: toothpick chicken. Yes, the suspiciously delicious chicken shelled out at mall food courts on toothpicks is most likely bourbon chicken. Popular at American Chinese or Cajun-style Chinese restaurants, the sticky-sweet chicken is the utter definition of delicious, luring weary shoppers into food courts to find refuge from the bubblegum pink sweatpants and ripped denim. This version takes cues from the classic but swaps in some autumnal ingredients to make it fit for the season. Charred brussels sprouts leaves and pecans add bulk, while maple syrup and apple cider vinegar give it that delightful tart-sweet flavor we know and love. Serve it over rice or noodles or all by its lonesome for a hearty dinner that will take you back to the '90s.

More chicken dinner winners here: Sheet-Pan Hot Honey Chicken and Peaches; Basil Chicken Stir-Fry; and Ginger-Garlic Chicken and Vegetable Stir-Fry.

Whisk one 1½" piece ginger, peeled, finely grated, 3 garlic cloves, finely chopped, ⅓ cup bourbon, ⅓ cup pure maple syrup, ¼ cup apple cider vinegar, ¼ tsp. crushed red pepper flakes, 1 Tbsp. cornstarch, ¼ cup low-sodium soy sauce, and ½ cup water in a small bowl to combine. Set sauce aside.

Toss 1½ lb. skinless, boneless chicken breasts, halved crosswise, thinly sliced against the grain, remaining 1 Tbsp. cornstarch, and remaining 1 Tbsp. low-sodium soy sauce in a medium bowl until chicken is coated; set aside.

Heat 2 Tbsp. vegetable oil in a large skillet over medium-high. Add 1 lb. brussels sprouts, halved through stems, leaves separated, and cook, stirring very occasionally, until just tender and blackened in spots, about 5 minutes. Transfer to another medium bowl.

Add more oil if pan looks dry and, working in batches if needed, arrange reserved chicken in a single layer and cook, turning occasionally, until golden brown and cooked through, about 5 minutes. Transfer to bowl with brussels sprouts.

Add reserved sauce to same pan and bring to a simmer. Cook, stirring constantly, until thickened, about 1 minute. Remove pan from heat and return brussels sprouts and chicken to pan; top with 1 cup salted roasted pecans, coarsely chopped. Toss to coat in sauce. Serve chicken and brussels sprouts with steamed white rice if desired.
